# KB CORE — Joe Friel — The Cyclist's Training Bible (2018)

Companion file: `friel-cyclists-training-bible_biblioteca.md` (full plans, workouts, exercises, recipes, templates). IDs like `CTB-L2-004` point there.

## C01 Overview & Scope

### [CTB-C01-001] Overview & Scope

**What the book is.** *The Cyclist's Training Bible* (5th ed., 2018, VeloPress) by Joe Friel (master's in exercise science; coach for 30+ years; co-founder of TrainingPeaks). It is a complete, self-coaching system for training and racing road cycling (road races, criteriums, stage races, time trials). Its basic principles are said to transfer to other endurance cycling events (mountain bike, cyclocross, gravel, gran fondo, century, ultradistance), but the specifics are for road racing and time trials.

**For whom.** Novices (year 1), intermediates (years 2-3), advanced riders (3+ years, the main target) and elites, plus masters/over-50s, juniors, women and time-limited riders. Each group has a different focus: frequency (novice), duration (intermediate), intensity (advanced).

**Problem it solves.** Most riders train randomly ("whatever the group ride does"), overvalue hours/miles and under-recover. The book gives a purposeful, individualized, planned and measured way to reach "high performance": being fitter, faster and achieving high goals.

**Central thesis.**
- High performance is an attitude and lifestyle first; mental limits usually bind before physical ones.
- Training is purposeful: clear goal -> expert instruction -> specific practice -> immediate feedback.
- Training is individualized (phenotype, limiters, recovery speed): "a research study with one subject."
- Periodization: the closer the A race, the more the workouts must resemble the race.
- For advanced riders intensity matters more than volume (author's unsupported estimate ~60% intensity / 40% volume); load is best measured with TSS.
- Fitness is realized in recovery; deliberate overreaching without overtraining; frequent easy days; polarized distribution.
- Taper builds form (readiness + freshness), not fitness.
- Measure with heart rate + power + RPE and field tests; keep a training diary.

**Organization.** Part I Mind and Body (Ch 1-2); Part II Training Fundamentals (Ch 3-4); Part III Purposeful Training (Ch 5-6: start-up, tests, abilities, limiters); Part IV Planning Your Season (Ch 7-9: ATP, week, alternative models; the "hub" of the book); Part V Stress and Recovery (Ch 10-11); Part VI Competitive Edge (Ch 12 muscular force, Ch 13 tapering, Ch 14 diary); Appendix A (ATP template), Appendix B (workouts and field tests), Glossary.

**Not covered / limits.** Mental training and psychology beyond basics (refers to other books); race tactics/strategy (refers to Prehn); detailed power-meter theory (refers to *The Power Meter Handbook*, Allen & Coggan); emotional/non-training stress quantification; aging in detail (*Fast After 50*); detailed nutrition (only recovery-oriented guidance, no fueling plans or supplement protocols, supplements discouraged). Not a clinical text; the author's opinions are often labeled as experience, not research.
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling level:all subtopic:overview
src: Prologue; Ch 1-14; Epilogue
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C01-002] Purpose, audience and scope of the book (5th edition)

- Friel's first edition described the training philosophy and methodology he developed over 20 years as athlete, student and coach. This 5th edition was completely rewritten (blank page): content grew from about 70,000 to 130,000 words, and each chapter was preceded by a review of the research that changed in two decades. Only the table of contents remains similar.
- Audience: novices (first year), intermediates (second and third years, still building basic fitness), advanced riders (more than three years, with sound understanding of training), and elite athletes (several years in the sport, able to perform at a winning level in their category, with deep knowledge of training and sports science). The book tries to address all of them.
- The book is about competitive performance: becoming fitter, riding faster and achieving high goals. Together these three outcomes are called "high performance". Reading the book will not turn a reader into a Grand Tour professional, but it can take riding to the next level and achieve goals previously thought unattainable.
- Focus: developing physical high performance. Mental performance is introduced in Part I; other works by sports psychologists cover it in more depth.

evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C01-003] Part I intro: Mind and Body

Part I covers the two critical components of high-performance racing: mind (Chapter 1) and body (Chapter 2 onward). Author's claim: every athlete he has coached could improve; performance is most commonly held back by the mind, and it is also likely constrained physically (body's full potential not reached). Not knowing how to train effectively is also widespread. Mental toughness is an existing skill that only needs further development; new ways of thinking about oneself as an athlete are its basis. Chapter 2 begins with the mental perspective on physical preparation, then the philosophy and methodology of training the author uses with his athletes.
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling level:all subtopic:mind-and-body
evidence: author-experience
src: Part I intro | p. 1

### [CTB-C01-004] Part II overview: training fundamentals

Part II covers training fundamentals for coaching with an expert or self-coached. Chapter 3: basic sports-science concepts (training principles up to fitness, fatigue, form). Chapter 4: training intensity, called perhaps the single most important contributor to race performance; covers measuring intensity (heart rate, power, perceived exertion all contribute), intensity distribution over time, and the Training Stress Score. Prepares the athlete for developing the season plan in Part III.
tags: type:philosophy level:all sport:cycling subtopic:book-structure
src: Part II intro | p. 25

### [CTB-C01-005] Part III intro: Purposeful Training

Having purpose when training separates the best from the rest. Purpose starts with a compelling goal; the first step is a personal assessment of the gap (physical and mental) between current status and goal, and tools to measure progress (Chapter 5). Chapter 6 addresses the physical markers (abilities) of endurance fitness and ability-based training; knowing your limiters relative to abilities makes training purposeful.
tags: type:philosophy level:all sport:cycling subtopic:purposeful-training
evidence: author-experience
src: Part III intro | p. 55

### [CTB-C01-006] Part IV overview: planning your season

Part IV (Chapters 7–9) is the "heart" of the book: determining which workouts to do and when, via periodization. Ch 7 introduces linear periodization; Ch 8 planning a week; Ch 9 alternative periodization models with advantages and disadvantages. By the end of Ch 9 the athlete has an annual training plan. The author used a simple periodization model from the 1970s; linear is the one most amateurs use and works for most, even some pros.
tags: type:philosophy level:all sport:cycling subtopic:periodization
src: Part IV intro | pp. 87-88

### [CTB-C01-007] Part VI The Competitive Edge - scope

Part VI covers three topics that give a competitive edge: muscular force training (Ch 12, early season, prep and base 1), tapering/peaking for the A-priority race (Ch 13), and the training diary for planning and analysis (Ch 14). Aside from riding, muscular force workouts are described as the most effective training to boost performance, yet are the most commonly neglected of the six abilities (mainly for lack of time). If no time for the gym, similar results can be had with muscular endurance/force workouts on the bike.

## C02 Philosophy & Core Principles

### [CTB-C02-001] Core principles (summary)

**What the author optimizes for:** race-day performance in the A-priority race(s), reached through consistent, purposeful, individualized training, with recovery protected.

**Principles and reasoning**
1. **Attitude precedes results.** Daily small decisions (sleep, food, company, self-talk) accumulate; motivation must be intrinsic; mental toughness = commitment + tenacity + patience; tenacity is trained by hard race-like workouts. Patience = emotion control (pacing).
2. **Dream -> goal -> mission.** Max 3 seasonal goals, specific, measurable, challenging but attainable, timed. Limiters -> training objectives (max 4, with deadlines) -> a purpose for every workout.
3. **Purposeful training:** clear goal, expert instruction (coach, mentor, plan or self), specific practice (easier is allowed anytime; harder only after consulting the expert), immediate feedback.
4. **Four principles:** progressive overload, specificity (central early, peripheral/bike-specific later), reversibility ("use it or lose it"), individuality.
5. **Race-likeness timing rule (the only hard-and-fast periodization rule):** the closer to the A race, the more like the race; the farther, the less like it.
6. **Intensity over volume for advanced riders;** frequency first for novices, duration for intermediates. Measure load with TSS, not hours.
7. **Polarized distribution:** 70-80% of time at/below AeT; easy days truly easy ("the easier your easy workouts, the harder your hard workouts can be"). Making easy days moderately hard is "the single biggest mistake."
8. **Stress + recovery = training.** Workouts only create the potential for fitness; adaptation happens in recovery (sleep > food > hydration). Fatigue is necessary; deliberate overreaching yes, overtraining never. If tired several days, rest regardless of plan. Better undertrained than overtrained.
9. **Fix limiters, maintain strengths.** Limiter = goal-specific weakness; maintenance is easier than remediation.
10. **Build basics before advanced abilities;** rebuild basics after tapers/breaks ("you can never have too much base").
11. **Form beats fitness on race day;** taper = cut duration, keep intensity.
12. **Measure:** "that which is measured improves"; heart rate monitor + power meter, field tests every 3-4 weeks, diary of goal-relevant data plus feelings. RPE/feel remains key in racing.
13. **Plan, but stay flexible;** the plan is a pencil-written work in progress, reviewed weekly; do not mix this method with others' zones or workouts.
14. **Real food; avoid supplements and processed "athlete" foods;** beware recovery methods that blunt adaptation.

**Rejects:** random training, volume obsession, formula-based max HR (220-age), chasing fitness in the final weeks, training with too many goals, shortening sleep, supplements/NSAIDs/icing as routine recovery.

**Non-negotiables:** purpose for each workout, annual pro bike fit and physical assessment, recovery weeks every 3-4 weeks, at most 3 A races, consistency, tapering, sleep and real food, writing it down.

src: Ch 1-14, Epilogue

### [CTB-C02-002] High performance as an attitude and lifestyle

- "High performance" = fitness + speed + goals achieved, but it goes beyond results: it is as much an attitude as an indicator of race results, and attitude comes before results, "way before".
- It means living in a way that makes achieving high goals possible: how consistently and how disciplined you train; what, when and how much you eat; who you hang out with; how you think about yourself.
- A high-performance attitude is a life pointed directly at a goal that is relentlessly pursued. Chapters 1 and 2 touch on these matters.
- Attitude and lifestyle play only supporting roles; the book's main focus is physical training.
- Rewards of the challenge: racing well builds habits and an outlook that make you a better person as well as a better cyclist; it requires time, energy, purpose, dedication and discipline, and the benefits come later and are mostly recognizable only to the rider.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:high-performance subtopic:attitude subtopic:discipline sport:cycling
src: Prologue | pp. xii, xiv
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C02-003] What has changed since the first edition: the author's evolving positions

- The author acknowledges that this edition sometimes contradicts earlier editions: things change — the sport, sports science and the author himself — and the evolution will continue.
- Four previous editions offered one simple way to train for all riders; this edition individualizes by cycling phenotype (see Phenotype entry).
- First edition described only classic periodization; this edition introduces several seasonal planning methods in addition (Chapters 7, 8, 9, using the annual training plan template in Appendix A) and helps the reader choose the best one.
- Training analysis was minimal in the first edition; Chapter 14 now teaches measuring progress with an emphasis on examining only critical data (saves time and improves performance).
- The strength program (Chapter 12) was greatly updated with more options.

src: Prologue | pp. xii-xiv
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C02-004] Individualization by cycling phenotype

- This edition recommends how to train based on the rider's cycling phenotype: the sport-related strength of the rider, i.e. personal racing characteristics as climber, sprinter, time trialist or all-rounder. Every rider fits one of these categories, and the training methodologies are built around the concept (described in Chapter 2).
- Individualized training based on abilities and limiters remains from the first edition (Chapter 6) and is "at the core of successful training for endurance sports"; it is closely related to goals and objectives and to the workouts in Appendix B.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:phenotype subtopic:abilities-limiters subtopic:individualization sport:cycling
src: Prologue | pp. xii-xiii
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C02-005] High performance is an attitude: daily small decisions

- High performance is as much a way of thinking and behaving (an attitude) as a race result. Get the attitude right and the physical part and results take care of themselves.
- If mental attitude lags behind physical performance, full potential is never reached. Any doubt about being able to win probably leads to failure regardless of training hours. A winning attitude must be developed and maintained.
- Hundreds of daily thoughts and small decisions (time use, workouts, food and drink, self-talk, who to ride/hang out with, bedtime and wake time, how you think of yourself) have at least as great an impact on race performance as on-bike training.
- Overarching question preceding all of them: are you making decisions that promote high performance or not?
- Individually insignificant decisions accumulate over days, weeks, months and years and determine who you become and how you perform.
- True high performers are mentally fit as well as physically fit and use nearly every thought and decision to create opportunities for success; daily life is about high performance. Some degree of obsession is required for the highest levels; top athletes are highly committed to goals.
- Author encourages further study of mental fitness (other books, successful people).
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling level:all subtopic:attitude subtopic:daily-decisions
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Mental Performance" | pp. 3-4

### [CTB-C02-006] Motivation

- High performance is rooted in motivation: an inner drive to excel to cope with mental and physical stress of training and racing. It starts with commitment to the goal and a lifestyle aligned with its demands. The higher the goal, the more daily actions must contribute; at the highest level everything (food, friends, etc.) is focused on the goal.
- Motivation drives early rising for workouts, healthy food choices, finishing painful workouts, and spending ~20 minutes analyzing session data after a 3-hour ride.
- It must be intrinsic: the coach cannot supply it, only suggestions and perspective. It comes from love of the sport (reading, watching, talking cycling, defining yourself as a cyclist, wanting to push limits).
- Other motivated riders (the team) can make it contagious.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:motivation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Motivation" | pp. 4-5

### [CTB-C02-007] Dreams, goals and missions

- High performance always starts with a dream that persists for a long time before becoming a goal. Elite athletes the author coached came from normal backgrounds and did not initially see themselves as capable.
- Turning a dream into a goal means making changes. Questions: willing to take first steps? What to change in life? Can you commit? How great are the sacrifices, and will you make them? What if I fail? What if I succeed?
- Many athletes have only wishes (vague, never defined or pursued). Give a lingering dream shape and substance over time to reach a goal.
- Next comes the will to pursue it: a mission, requiring a change of mindset and a purposeful attitude; the athlete finds a way despite obstacles and setbacks.
- The bigger the dream and higher the goal, the longer it takes. Once committed, the goal becomes the mission, the purpose in every daily decision; the more challenging, the more life must be focused around it. Keep dreaming. Goal setting detail is in Chapter 5.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:goal-setting subtopic:dreams
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Dreams, Goals, and Missions" | pp. 5-6

### [CTB-C02-008] Patience (pacing is emotion control)

- Cycling is a patience sport; the longer the race, the more patience needed. A time trial is more a test of patience than a race (example: athletes visibly anaerobic 1 mile into a 25-mile TT).
- Peak performance takes months and years, not hours and days; the approach to training also needs patience.
- Impatient athletes start workouts, interval sets or races much too fast (unnecessary attacks) and fade. Cause is often the passionate mission itself; commitment must be held in check by patience.
- Goals are achieved late in the workout, interval set or race, not in the first interval. "Pacing" is actually emotion control: controlling emotions in the early stages of anything.
- Becoming patient: no easy fix; practice daily. Author makes athletes repeat a workout (e.g. first interval too fast) until done right; after a race started too aggressively, holds a long post-race discussion. Teaching patience is the hardest thing for him as a coach.
- Be your own coach: notice impatience, remind yourself before hard workouts/races to contain emotions early to finish strong, and remind yourself at season start that patience means small daily gains over months.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:patience subtopic:pacing
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Patience" | pp. 8-9

### [CTB-C02-009] Commitment and tenacity

- Missions survive setbacks only with commitment and tenacity. When fatigue says stop (legs on fire, heart pounding, riders dropping, thoughts of giving up), this moment measures the cyclist; the committed rider keeps going a little longer.
- Commitment must exist on every training ride: hard, fatigued training days prepare you for race situations. Training prepares the mind as well as the body. Example: several 2-minute intervals at top-end power with minimal recovery, making the last one hardest.
- Tenacity (mental toughness) is not all-or-nothing: some riders cope with extreme suffering; some are sensitive and give up mentally despite fitness; some quit at first suffering (no commitment, unprepared).
- Tenacity is improved by race-like training: workouts should occasionally test limits by challenging you to continue a few seconds longer. A few more seconds often decides podium contenders.
- It must be worked on every day; it is the result of hard workouts. Being fully committed produces a tenacious high-performance attitude.
- Missed workouts are the first sign of lacking commitment; without consistent training tenacity never develops. Foundation of commitment is training consistency.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:tenacity subtopic:commitment
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Commitment and Tenacity" | pp. 9-11

### [CTB-C02-010] Potential depends on training history

Friel's way of estimating what a rider can achieve is to look backward at past training, not to predict the future.
- A rider who has raced several years on a well-designed program is probably near their potential. Expect only a handful of percentage points of further gain.
- A rider who is new to the sport, whose training has been random, or who often misses workouts has high potential for improvement, in double-digit percentage points. Following the book's principles and guidelines produces progressive improvement.
- Most riders fall in between: they train regularly but without direction, do frequent group rides and occasional hard workouts, miss strings of workouts, and finish deep in the pack. They have considerable room to improve, but reaching their potential takes dedication.
- Exact potential cannot be stated. Improvement depends on training consistency and a focused high-performance attitude, and also on physical potential (physiology).
- Options for a rider who doubts they can follow the book's principles closely: buy a generic online plan written by someone who understands bike racing (it gives structure, but the rider must still follow it), or hire a coach with years in the sport and success producing competitive riders. A coach is expensive, but with high goals expert guidance makes success more likely.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:potential subtopic:training-history sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "The Right Stuff" | pp. 15-16

### [CTB-C02-011] Training beliefs: a research study with one subject

- Training is a research study with one subject: you. Research results are arithmetic means. Some subjects improve more than the average, some less, and some decline on the same protocol, because humans are not robots. Friel cannot promise personal greatness from the guidelines alone; be ready to adjust based on what works for you. What works for everyone else is irrelevant if it does not work for you.
- Training is an act of faith. There are no guarantees, but you must believe you are on the right path and follow the program from beginning to end.
- Individual differences: "slow responders" may take months to match gains that "fast responders" get in weeks or days on the same training. The cause is unknown, possibly largely genetic. Muscle type matters too: some endurance athletes have lots of slow-twitch muscle (aids endurance), others have plenty of fast-twitch (benefits power sports).
- Friel's suggestions come from the research and from what worked for most athletes he has coached. Adjust them using your own knowledge, and expect some trial and error.
- Training is a moving target: what worked last season may not work this season, because body and mind are always changing, sometimes rapidly. Always be prepared to adjust.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:individuality subtopic:responders subtopic:muscle-fiber-type sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Training Beliefs" | pp. 19-20

### [CTB-C02-012] Four steps to training with a purpose (Figure 2.2)

The principle of individuality says there is no one-size-fits-all training (explored further in Chapter 3). Even so, a few general methods work for most athletes, and one is a focused purpose: a reason for everything done in training. Friel's four-step cycle (Figure 2.2), used for many years with athletes:
1. Clear Goal
2. Expert Instruction
3. Specific Practice
4. Immediate Feedback
The loop runs continuously from step 4 back to step 1. Before setting the next workout's purpose, assess progress to date against the seasonal goal. If on track, continue. If the long-term trend says otherwise, reconsider the goal and adjust the training strategy.
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:purposeful-training sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Four Steps to Training with a Purpose" | pp. 20, 23

### [CTB-C02-013] Four training principles

Friel: four principles form the core of training for sport. Following them points in the right direction; violating one (seen in self-coached athletes) greatly decreases chances of success. Much of the book rests on them.
1. Progressive Overload
2. Specificity
3. Reversibility
4. Individuality
Summary: to become race ready, progressively increase training load and train specific to the demands of the race.
tags: type:principle level:all sport:cycling subtopic:training-principles
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Training Principles" | pp. 28-30, 38

### [CTB-C02-014] Principle of Progressive Overload

- To increase fitness you must gradually and steadily increase the amount of training; the plan must become progressively harder.
- Workload must exceed the current level of adaptation, stressing the body beyond what it did before. Without stress, fitness cannot improve.
- Fatigue after a hard workout or several hard days shows the body was overloaded; it responds positively if the overload was not too great.
- If overload is too great for adaptation, it is "overtraining": a breakdown instead of adaptation.
- Training should NOT get harder every day/week: workload must be reduced at times to recover. Without frequent recoveries built into weekly, monthly and annual training, a high level of fitness will never be achieved.
tags: type:principle level:all sport:cycling subtopic:progressive-overload
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | p. 28

### [CTB-C02-015] Principle of Specificity (central vs peripheral)

- Two categories of change: central (heart, lungs, blood) and peripheral (mostly muscles).
- Central changes occur regardless of endurance sport (the heart does not know running from cycling); crosstraining (e.g. running) benefits the central systems.
- Peripheral fitness is sport-specific: quadriceps know running from riding. Swimming, hiking, skiing or anything not very similar to cycling does not make the muscles fit for riding. To train muscles for riding, you have to ride.
- Crosstraining is largely ineffective for muscles. Possible exceptions: weight training and gym exercises such as plyometrics to increase power of cycling-specific muscles, but the movements must closely mimic how the muscle is used in riding (not just loading with weight). See Chapter 12.
- Both central and peripheral fitness are critical and cannot be separated in performance.
- Traditionally the season starts emphasizing central systems and gradually shifts to peripheral: the closer to the most important race, the more specificity. Crosstrain very early in the training year, then make the bike the focus as the season progresses.
tags: type:principle level:all sport:cycling subtopic:specificity subtopic:crosstraining phase:base
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 28-29

### [CTB-C02-016] Principle of Reversibility

- Reversibility = losing fitness. Every zero in the training diary loses fitness ("use it or lose it"). A single day off loses so little it could not be measured in a lab; after several days the loss becomes measurable.
- Fitness cannot be gained by resting. Feeling good after a day off is "form", not fitness.
- Days off are still warranted when greatly fatigued. Low-fitness athletes (possibly you early in the season) may need a day or more fully off every week; without a complete break they risk excessive overload and eventually overtraining. A highly fit athlete may need only a light training day instead (see Chapter 11).
- Reduced training for several consecutive days also loses fitness. Tapering ("peaking") is necessary for high-level racing (relates to form), but spending much time tapering/peaking for many races loses too much fitness and performance declines; related to the number of highest-priority races per season (Chapter 13).
- Most common/significant fitness loss (absent injury/illness) is the long, necessary break at season end (Chapter 7).
- Fitness is always changing, positively or negatively; the athlete controls the direction.
tags: type:principle level:all sport:cycling subtopic:reversibility subtopic:detraining
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 29-30

### [CTB-C02-017] Principle of Individuality

- Each athlete is unique, so training must be unique. Uniqueness often must be discovered by trial and error.
- Examples: good sprinter but poor climber/time trialist; femur long relative to leg length (associated with powerful, economical pedaling); gains strength/muscle easily or not; handles heat well or poorly.
- You cannot do what a training partner or favorite pro does and expect the same results; the program must match your distinctiveness.
- Author's coaching experience (30+ years, hundreds of athletes): what worked for most did not work for all.
- Pointers: unique characteristics determined from Chapter 5; plan design in Chapters 7, 8, 9.
tags: type:principle level:all sport:cycling subtopic:individuality
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 27, 30

### [CTB-C02-018] Duration, intensity, frequency: the variables of workout design

- Only two factors define a workout: duration (how long) and intensity (how hard). They yield all workouts (intervals, tempo, recovery spins). The third variable is frequency (how often). Example: once a day for a week = frequency 7; riding daily plus lifting twice a week = 9.
- Duration, not distance: the body reacts to how long a high intensity is maintained, not miles/km. Same distance into a headwind vs calm needs different power and time.
- Intensity is inversely related to duration: the longer the race/workout, the lower the sustainable intensity (power-duration curve, Ch 2).
- Frequency depends mostly on lifestyle; limited time makes smart planning critical: right workouts at right times, no sloppy aimless training, every session must count. Hence a detailed training plan (Part IV).
tags: type:principle level:all sport:cycling metric:volume metric:power subtopic:duration subtopic:frequency
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 30-32

### [CTB-C02-019] Intensity, not volume, is the key for the advanced rider

- Only three workout components can be manipulated: frequency, duration, intensity. For an advanced rider (about 3+ years in the sport) frequency and duration should be a given; if volume is still the central focus, the rider has not reached the summit of "advanced".
- Frequency and duration are the central concerns for novice and intermediate riders. For the advanced athlete, intensity determines peak race performance, not volume.
- Intermediates (a couple of years in the sport) develop quickly by increasing weekly volume, but that only goes so far and then performance plateaus. Research in several endurance sports shows that for the advanced athlete high performance results from how hard rather than how much.
- Why riders fixate on volume: hours/miles/km are easy to total, while the cumulative intensity of a week is hard to express as one number. The belief forms in the early intermediate stage, when volume equates to performance.
- Volume does not become unimportant; it becomes less important as fitness increases.
- Author's opinion: intensity accounts for 60% of race-day fitness and volume 40%. Friel states there is no research proving this; it is based on 30+ years of coaching.
- Measure all three: effort (RPE), heart rate and power. An advanced rider becomes much more race ready by being proficient with all three.
tags: type:philosophy level:advanced sport:cycling subtopic:intensity-vs-volume metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Training Intensity", "Training Stress Score" | pp. 41, 52

### [CTB-C02-020] Start-up process each season is non-negotiable

- Friel has every athlete repeat the chapter 5 start-up formula each year as they start back into training. The activities are more than suggestions: they matter as much to race preparation and performance as on-bike training.
- Reason: many small tasks done at the start of the season shape how the whole season is trained and how useful the training is. They seem trivial and are tempting to skip, but they are often the key to success.
- If already well into the season (e.g. 8 weeks to the next important race with serious training started): restart the season by following the chapter's guidelines; they fit in without significantly interfering with race preparation.
- If in the midst of the race season: delay all start-up procedures until a break of several weeks before the next high-priority race.
- Regardless of timing, eventually incorporate the steps.
tags: type:principle level:all sport:cycling phase:prep subtopic:season-start-up
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "Getting Started" | pp. 57-62

### [CTB-C02-021] That which is measured improves (measurement and training tools)

- Coaching philosophy: "that which is measured improves." Repeated measurement of something important keeps it in the forefront of daily thoughts, and daily decisions begin to reflect the goal. Without measurement, goals are merely hopes and wishes.
- Friel requires everyone he coaches to use a heart rate monitor AND a power meter when riding: they give accurate data, and reliable information is the most critical component for training decisions. Without them you are guessing about your engine.
- Uses beyond live display: analyze workouts, spot trends, design future workouts, even if you never glance at the device during a ride. Questions answered: Is FTP improving? Is aerobic fitness improving? How does this compare with the same time last year? Is there enough race-like intensity in training? How many matches were burned in the race and how can you train to produce more? They replace memory and guesswork.
- Not necessary for everyone: novices in their first year get little benefit (their challenge is simply getting on the bike regularly); experienced athletes with an excellent feel for intensity, especially self-coached, may not be held back without them.
- Cost: even the least expensive power meter reliably provides intensity measurement; used equipment from bike shops and clubs is an option.
- Cautions: you must invest time learning to use them. Do not ride in traffic looking at handlebar numbers (safety). Over-dedication to numbers can cause loss of "feel": in racing, perceived exertion/feel remains the key; the devices serve training by letting you measure progress and get workout intensity right, which also improves feel.
- Recommended further reading by the author: Total Heart Rate Training; The Power Meter Handbook.
tags: type:principle level:all sport:cycling metric:power,hr subtopic:training-tools
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "Training Tools"; Summary | pp. 57-59, 69

### [CTB-C02-022] Race preparation starts from the duration and intensity demands of the event

- Specific races require specific training. Events differ mainly in their emphasis on duration and intensity. The principles apply to any endurance cycling event (mountain bike, cyclocross, gravel, gran fondo, century, some track, multisport, ultradistance).
- Preparation begins by developing the fitness needed for the event's duration and intensity demands. Duration is easy to work out (20-min time trial, 45-min criterium and 3-hour road race need different workout durations).
- Intensity is more intricate. Road race outcomes are decided by intermittent episodes of very high intensity lasting seconds to minutes; this is unlike a marathon. Time trial fitness depends on how steadily and how long a relatively high intensity can be sustained (somewhat like a marathon).
- For the advanced rider, manipulating training intensity is the key to effective race preparation. Predicting intensity and duration is at the heart of program design.
- Dilemma: training concurrently for a road race and a time trial makes difficult, contradictory demands. There are time constraints and adaptive constraints; the body has limited capacity to be maximally fit for both long steady intensity and brief highly variable intensity. Grand Tour winners are exceptional because they perform well in both.
- Understanding the physiology makes workouts meaningful: you pre-stress the body for race-day stresses to produce deep physiological changes that can be measured with the Chapter 5 tests.
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling subtopic:race-specificity level:all
src: Ch 6 "Preparing to Race" | pp. 71-72
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C02-023] Training philosophy: the closer to the race, the more race-like the training

- Core philosophy: "The closer you are to your race, the more your training must be like the race." Workouts gradually morph into races; if you have done it in training, you can do it in a race. "Faith becomes confidence."
- Franz Stampfl: "Training is principally an act of faith" (no guarantees; doubt harms race performance), so the method must be credible and believable.
- Corollary: the farther from the A-priority race, the less race-like training should be. This is the only hard-and-fast rule of periodization; otherwise periodization is flexible and the athlete may experiment.
- Examples: 40 km TT goal at 200 W means increasingly longer intervals around 200 W; a long climb as limiter means increasingly similar long climbs with a fast group; a hot race means frequent heat training in the last several weeks pre-race.
- Method: know the event's demands and bring strengths and limiters into alignment with them.
- Training is a steady, gradual building process. Physiological changes have timetables and cannot be rushed ("nature must run its course").
evidence: author-experience
tags: type:philosophy type:principle level:all sport:cycling subtopic:race-specificity subtopic:periodization
src: Ch 7 "Planning Overview" | pp. 89-90

### [CTB-C02-024] The plan must be dynamic, flexible and a work in progress

- A rigidly followed plan that ignores fatigue and life demands is worse than no plan. Daily feel must be considered and the plan modified; this almost always means taking a break when needed (see Chapter 11, "recovery on demand").
- Plans are written in pencil or electronically: in 30+ years of coaching, no athlete's plan went unaltered (illness, lifestyle, necessary breaks).
- Reason for a written plan: a dynamic guide and decision aid made at season start with a big-picture view and without emotion ("before the season starts you see the forest; after, trees get in the way"). Not to impress.
- Planning is like having a coach: it makes workouts purposeful and builds confidence. Athletes who decide workouts "as they roll out of the driveway" are less likely to be prepared. The higher the goal, the more planning matters.
- Self-coached athletes should look dispassionately at where they are; emotional reactions to a poor workout, race or week can reverse progress. Decisions must reflect long-term best interest.
- Review and modify next week's planned workouts at the end of each week.
- Save each season's plans and adjustments plus season-ending notes to ease future planning.
evidence: author-experience
tags: type:principle type:methodology level:all sport:cycling subtopic:plan-flexibility subtopic:self-coaching
src: Ch 7 | pp. 90, 92, 96, 110-111

### [CTB-C02-025] Training philosophy: workouts become progressively race-like

- As you train for a key race, workouts gradually take on the characteristics of the race (time trial workouts become like a time trial; criterium workouts in the last weeks are highly intense; same for road and stage races).
- The farther from the race, the LESS like the race the workouts should be. Example: weight lifting is nonspecific (you never stop and lift weights in a race); long steady rides at aerobic threshold are also general.
- General training makes up the bulk of the prep and base mesocycles. It builds the basic fitness needed for race-specific workouts in build, peak and race.
- Bottom line: gradually shift focus from general to specific. This assumes the athlete is striving for a very high goal in A-priority races; for low-key events planning is of little value.
- Planning is tedious the first time, but the payoff later in the season is worth it.
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling level:all subtopic:general-to-specific subtopic:periodization
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Planning a Week" | pp. 115-116

### [CTB-C02-026] Four training principles apply to every planning model

Regardless of periodization model, the four training principles of Chapter 3 remain constant and must be followed:
- Progressive overload: workload must gradually increase over time; if it stalls too long, fitness plateaus. What changes is workout frequency, duration, intensity, or a combination.
- Specificity: workouts must resemble what will be done in the goal race (e.g., road race or crit training must include high intensity and appropriate duration).
- Reversibility: fitness is lost as training load decreases. This is useful only to be on form for a race and to recover from hard training; otherwise avoid it.
- Individuality: training must reflect personal strengths and limiters. Limiters (race-specific weaknesses) are the primary focus for the A race while strengths are maintained. It is far easier to maintain a strength than to improve a limiter.
Additional dictum: workouts must become increasingly like the A-priority race as race day nears. If training gradually becomes different from race demands (e.g., replacing goal-intensity work with long slow distance), reversibility erodes race-specific fitness (e.g., high-intensity performance).
Only three variables can be modified: frequency, duration, intensity (besides mode). Periodization is the management of these three to produce race readiness at the right time.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:training-principles subtopic:periodization sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 145-146

### [CTB-C02-027] Training as everything in life: stress and recovery balance

- For the high-performance rider, training is everything done in life, not only riding. Job, thoughts, company, food, life events (job change, moving, divorce, financial problems) all produce stress and affect race results.
- Excessive training and lifestyle stress must be removed during recovery to restore a normal, manageable stress level. Recovery is usually physical but can be mental (listening to music, chatting with friends).
- Stress beyond the body's current state is necessary to create the potential for fitness; recovery is when that potential becomes fitness. The balance between stress and recovery produces race results; one without the other is meaningless.
- Effective training is the balanced blending of stress and recovery. "That's it. Nothing else."
- Mental/emotional stress is outside the book's scope but must be considered; the body reacts much the same regardless of the cause. Some athletes must cut back or stop training to cope with a psychologically stressful event.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:stress-recovery-balance subtopic:lifestyle-stress
src: Part V intro; Ch 10 "Training Stress" | pp. 161-163, 171
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C02-028] Recovery is as critical as hard workouts

- Hard workouts create fatigue, which represents only the potential for fitness. Fitness is realized as you recover and adapt (overcompensation).
- Recovery is just as critical to cycling success as hard workouts. A rider who is good at one but not the other falls well short of potential. Recovery is perhaps the single most important aspect of the athlete's daily lifestyle and must be a habit done without thinking.
- No improvement in fitness without at least some fatigue. Fatigue is good because it implies the potential for fitness; decreasing fatigue over time indicates adaptation (realized fitness).
- Balance: too many hard or too few recovery days leads toward overtraining; only easy rides or frequent days off lead to loss of fitness (reversibility principle, Ch 3).
- Key to training: be conservative. It is better to do too little training than too much; better slightly undertrained and enthusiastic than constantly tired and apathetic.
- Fitter athletes can sometimes improve remarkably by merely training easier more often (consistent with polarized 80/20, Ch 4: very easy at or below the aerobic threshold, very hard at or above the anaerobic threshold, little time between).
- For the serious rider, the majority of training days in a week should be devoted to recovery rather than hard workouts. Stress is easy to get right (do the planned hard workouts); riders undervalue recovery.
tags: type:philosophy type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:recovery-balance subtopic:overcompensation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 intro, "Recovery and Adaptation", Summary | pp. 173-174, 181, 192

### [CTB-C02-029] Weight lifting as a skill; benefits and AA rationale

Benefits: greater strength/pedal force/power; strength improves in hours after a session due to anabolic hormones (testosterone, growth hormone); bone density improves (serious cyclists have rather weak bones); nervous-system pedaling changes only if exercises closely simulate bike movements. Lifting is a skill like pedaling and cornering; many repetitions are needed. First sign of skill is an increase in loads lifted, then cycling strength improves. That is why the program starts with anatomical adaptation (AA); skipping it to reach heavy loads is counterproductive. The aim is more power, not bulk: more force/torque without increased sensation of effort (e.g., higher power at FTP for 40-60 min without feeling harder). Research exists both ways; the author favors lifting.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling system:neuromuscular subtopic:weight-lifting phase:anatomical-adaptation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 pp. 197, 220

### [CTB-C02-030] Form, not fitness, predicts race success

- Friel: the best predictor of race success is not fitness but form. Fitness must be high, but trying to raise it further in the last 2 weeks before the race will not help.
- Example: two riders with identical fitness 2 weeks before a race; the one who focuses on form in the final days beats the one intent on increasing fitness.
- Fitness is the product of increasing training load, so decreased load causes a loss of fitness. You cannot become fitter by training less. When tapered athletes race better and feel fitter, the cause is form, not fitness.
- Fitness does not increase during the taper; a small amount of fitness is lost before the race (Ch 3).
- Taper is scary because it cuts load in the last 2-3 weeks before the A-priority race. Most athletes' instincts say do more.
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling phase:taper level:all subtopic:form-vs-fitness
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 13 intro, "Fitness", "Form" | pp. 229-230

### [CTB-C02-031] Why keep a training diary

- Aside from the bike, the best race-preparation tool is a training diary. Used properly it steers performance for the whole cycling career.
- Purpose: keeps focus on goals and objectives. Without frequent reminders of why you ride, the purpose fades and you are only accumulating pointless hours, miles, km or TSS. The diary reminds you daily why you train.
- It measures the key factors that improve limiters and pinpoints unwanted trends early so training can be corrected. It answers: Is my FTP increasing? Is my power-to-weight ratio where it should be? How is training load progressing versus my best season? What are workouts predicting for race performance? How did I taper for this race last season? What happened in this race last year?
- It is a planning tool: the seasonal plan is recorded in it. At season end it supplies answers for the next season.
- Without a written record you are left with memory and speculation; a serious racer needs one.
- The history is valuable to a coach: with a new client's diary the coach can see what works and what doesn't and starts quickly instead of guessing.
- Author's view: keeping a diary is the single most important thing a rider can do aside from riding. It also serves as an early warning system for injuries and overtraining.
tags: type:philosophy level:all sport:cycling subtopic:training-diary
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "The Training Diary" | pp. 237-239

### [CTB-C02-032] Epilogue: self-coaching, flexibility and meticulousness

- The book's method is the best the author knows, not the only way; it evolves. Past champions (Anquetil, Merckx, Hinault, LeMond) trained simply (much saddle time with fast riders); the future may be in the past.
- Success keys: mental (dedication, discipline, drive: "how badly you want it") plus genetics (nature) and opportunity (nurture). If dedicated and disciplined regardless of genetics, the battle is 80 percent won; the remainder is how you train. Some are "completers" not "competers," which is fine.
- Self-coaching is complex and never finished; coaches spend years learning. Power-based training is the current technology to master: read The Power Meter Handbook, then Training and Racing with a Power Meter (Allen and Coggan).
- Flexibility: be willing to change plans regardless of time invested. If tired, replace a scheduled high-stress workout with something easier allowing recovery; pressing on digs a deeper hole and may ruin the season. Insert recovery when needed whether or not the plan calls for it. Self-coached riders often neglect rest and results suffer.
- Meticulousness: follow Appendix B workout guidelines closely, especially high-intensity workouts. Work and recovery intervals have specified intensities and durations; significant changes to either likely reduce or eliminate the benefit.
- Caution on mixing methods: merging widely different methodologies is risky; e.g., don't use Appendix B workouts with someone else's intensity zones. Slight tweaks to fit your schedule are fine.
- No one-size-fits-all or single magic workout: you are a research study of one subject; it takes thoughtful trial and error. Performance depends on desire (created within) and knowledge (become a student of the sport).
tags: type:philosophy level:all sport:cycling subtopic:self-coaching subtopic:flexibility subtopic:rest
evidence: author-experience
src: Epilogue | pp. 253-255

## C03 Integral Model

### [CTB-C03-001] Integral Model

**1. Chain: beliefs -> mechanisms -> method -> decisions**
- *Beliefs:* mind limits first; training is individual and an act of faith turned into confidence by race-like preparation; intensity decides races for advanced riders; recovery is half of training.
- *Mechanisms* (see "Three markers of endurance fitness and phenotypes", "Supercompensation", "Fitness, fatigue and their relationship", "Overcompensation and adaptation during recovery"): fitness = aerobic capacity x anaerobic threshold (as % of capacity) x economy; stress -> fatigue -> recovery -> overcompensation; fatigue changes in hours/days, fitness in weeks (hence taper makes form); the brain governs fatigue (central governor), so tenacity is trainable; races are decided at and above AnT in brief episodes (seconds to minutes), while AeT fitness delivers you there.
- *Method:* measure intensity (RPE/HR/power; zones from FTHR/FTP), express load as TSS, organize abilities by periods, protect recovery, taper, analyze via diary.
- *Decisions:* what workout today, how hard, how long, when to rest, when to test, which period next.

**2. Variables manipulated.** Only duration, intensity, frequency (plus mode). Combined: volume (frequency x duration), load (volume x intensity), dose (hardness of one workout), density (spacing of hard sessions). Order of priority by level: novice frequency -> intermediate duration -> advanced intensity. Intensity distribution polarized (Figure 4.2: 70-80% low, 15-25% moderate, 5-15% high). Above all: race-likeness vs. time to the A race.

**3. Tools and where they fit**
- *Zones:* "Table 4.2 How to Determine Heart Rate Zones" and "Table 4.3 How to Determine Power Zones" from FTHR/FTP; RPE ("Table 4.1 ..."). HR zones stable, power zones rise with fitness; power preferred for short/hard work, HR for effort/easy work.
- *Tests:* FTP/FTHR (20 min minus 5%), FAeT/EF, 5-min aerobic capacity, sprint power, 10 km TT ("T1: ..." to "T5: ..."); done after 3-5 recovery days at the end of mesocycles, FTP every 3-4 weeks, AeT test every 3-4 weeks (Sidebar) or 6-8 weeks (Appendix B) [see LOG].
- *Abilities:* three basic (aerobic endurance, muscular force, speed skills), three advanced (muscular endurance, anaerobic endurance, sprint power), each advanced one built on two basics (Figure 6.2); "Table 6.1 ..." maps limiters to abilities. Workouts are in L2.
- *Periods:* "Table 7.1 ..." and "Table 7.4 ..." (prep, base 1-3, build 1-2, peak, race, transition) with 3-week (slow recovery/over 50/novice/intermediate) or 4-week (fast recovery) mesocycles, the last 3-5 days being R&R. Weight lifting phases AA -> MT -> MS -> SM staggered against the bike (Table 12.3).
- *Volume:* annual hours/TSS by category ("Table 7.2 ..."), weekly spread ("Table 7.5 ..." / "Table 7.6 ..."), daily spread ("Table 8.3 ..." / "Table 8.4 ..."); all ballpark.
- *Race priority:* A (max 3; 1-2 week peak + race week), B (max 8; 2-5 day reduction), C (unlimited; treated as workouts).
- *Monitoring:* morning warnings (Table 11.1; score >=7 = recovery), HRV, TSS balance, diary.
- *Models:* linear default; slow-recovery fixes (3-week blocks, 9-day weeks), time-limited, inverse weeks, reverse linear (only long moderate A races), undulating, block (elites only), simple plan.

**4. Decision flow for a new athlete**
1. Screen mindset, lifestyle consistency, support ("Coach's screening of an athlete's mental makeup"); assess potential from training history ("Potential depends on training history").
2. Start-up in the first four weeks ("Start-of-season assessment: three parts, within four weeks"): PT assessment + pro bike fit, Mental Skills Assessment (Sidebar 5.1), field tests for baselines.
3. Define phenotype and the A race demands; identify limiters ("Identifying and addressing limiters"; gap analysis).
4. Set <=3 goals and <=4 objectives ("ATP Step 1", "ATP Step 2").
5. Set annual volume ("ATP Step 3"), prioritize races ("ATP Step 4"), work back from first A race to assign periods ("ATP Step 5"), weekly volume ("ATP Step 6").
6. Fill weight lifting and ability columns ("ATP workout columns...", "Ability workouts by period").
7. Build a standard week (Table 8.1): breakthrough sessions with recovery before/after, group rides only from base 3, daily volume per Tables 8.3/8.4.
8. Execute with purpose; each Sunday review and write next week in the diary; record morning warnings.
9. Every 3-4 weeks: R&R, then test, reset zones, check EF/FTP trend against objectives.
10. Adjust: missed workouts rules ("Missed workouts: ..."), illness return, recheck EF after races to decide base 3 vs build.
11. Taper ("Taper definition and core principle..."; hard session every third day, race-week 90 s intervals), diary the final days, post-race analysis, tweak next time.

**5. Tensions and acknowledged trade-offs**
- Risk vs reward: must flirt with overtraining; manage by dose/density and frequent recovery.
- Taper loses a little fitness to gain form; hence max 3 A races.
- Training for TT and criterium goals concurrently conflicts physiologically; focus on one at a time.
- Motivation is essential yet its dark side is refusing rest.
- Recovery speed vs adaptation: shortening recovery may blunt adaptation, except during stage races/crash training.
- Specificity vs foundation: general work (weights) early, race-like later; reverse and block models bend this and only suit specific athletes.
- Rigid plan vs flexibility: plan must be dynamic; "when in doubt, leave it out/take the conservative route."
- Volume still matters but less than intensity for the advanced; time-limited athletes raise intensity as duration falls.
- Author admits many claims are personal experience (e.g., 60/40 split) and evolved from earlier editions.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling level:all subtopic:integral-model
src: whole book
evidence: author-experience

## C04 Mechanisms

### [CTB-C04-001] Training Stress Score (TSS) as a better load measure than hours or distance

- TSS, developed in the last 20 years, is a much more effective way to gauge training load than adding up hours, miles or kilometers in the saddle each week.
- Author's claim: learning to train with TSS is one of the smallest and yet most effective changes to increase fitness and race faster; it focuses training on what is important for high performance. Chapter 4 teaches what TSS is and how to use it.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:tss metric:load sport:cycling
src: Prologue | p. xiii
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C04-002] Form, tapering and peaking (overview)

- Achieving a peak of fitness at the right time is not well understood by most riders.
- Tapering is a complex undertaking that removes fatigue while maintaining fitness; the result is called "form". It is another balancing act related to recovery.
- Chapter 3 introduces the three-part concept (fitness, fatigue, form); the full explanation is held until Chapter 13 so the reader first understands the intermediate training methodologies.
tags: type:mechanism phase:taper level:all subtopic:form subtopic:fitness-fatigue-form sport:cycling
src: Prologue | p. xiii
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C04-003] Mental toughness and the central governor theory

- Key moment of a race: at your limit, fatigue sets in and the mind accepts compromise (goal not that important). The fully committed rider gets through; others settle for less. Commitment is passion for the goal; it must be present every day, not only race day.
- Graham Jones, PhD (professor of elite performance psychology; Harvard Business Review paper on Olympic athletes): medalists versus non-medalists: paid meticulous attention to goals; strong inner drive to stay ahead of competition; concentrated on excellence; not distracted by other people or athletes; shrugged off own failures; rebounded from defeat easily; never self-flagellated; celebrated wins; analyzed reasons for their success; very confident of abilities.
- Mental toughness is an everyday state of mind (every thought and action), appearing in hard races. The author adds patience, which Jones alluded to but did not address.
- Tim Noakes, PhD (South Africa) central governor theory: fatigue occurs in the brain; the brain constantly monitors body signals and decides when to pull the plug as a self-protection mechanism to prevent bodily damage. Overcoming the brain's reluctance takes great commitment; the author believes such tenacity is trainable in committed riders, with workouts that produce suffering preparing the mind as much as the body.
- Racing is about managing suffering: road race outcomes are decided by intense episodes of seconds to a couple of minutes; time trials require holding high intensity on the threshold of extreme suffering for a long time.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:mental-toughness subtopic:central-governor
evidence: study | Graham Jones, PhD (Harvard Business Review); Tim Noakes, PhD (central governor theory)
src: Ch 1 "Mental Toughness", "Commitment and Tenacity" | pp. 8-10

### [CTB-C04-004] Three markers of endurance fitness and phenotypes

Physical predictors of road cycling success depend on race goals ("horses for courses"). The cycling disciplines are called "phenotypes".
- Climbing: needed for hilly races.
- Sprinting: a generous helping of top-end power is needed for criteriums.
- Time trialing: the ability to hold a relatively high, steady intensity for a long time.
- All-rounders: riders good at two or three of climbing, sprinting and time trialing. They are respectable in several disciplines but excel at none. This fits a rider targeting a race series with varied events or a weekend stage race.

Each phenotype is assessed on three markers of endurance fitness. Every rider has a unique mix of them, and together they determine potential for different races.
- Aerobic capacity (VO2max): how much oxygen you can use when riding at maximal effort for a few minutes. The more oxygen processed to produce energy, the greater the aerobic capacity.
- Anaerobic threshold (sometimes called lactate threshold, though physiologically not exactly the same): the percentage of aerobic capacity you can sustain for a long time, such as 40 to 70 minutes. The higher the percentage, the greater the anaerobic threshold fitness.
- Economy: how much energy you waste while riding. It is largely determined by physical structure, but also includes pedaling style, position on the bike and bike-handling skills. The less energy wasted, the better the economy.

Knowing the gap between race demands and current physical state shapes how to train and how to adjust lifestyle (nutrition, emotional stress, sleep). "Limiters" are covered in Chapter 6; the detail on phenotypes is also in Chapter 6.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:aerobic system:threshold level:all subtopic:phenotypes subtopic:economy subtopic:vo2max
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "The Right Stuff" | pp. 16-17

### [CTB-C04-005] Figure 2.1 Power-duration curves by phenotype

Figure 2.1 illustrates typical power-duration curves for a climber, sprinter, time trialist, and all-rounder.
- Axes: Vertical axis is POWER (watts), labeled 0 to 1,400 in 200 W increments. Horizontal axis is DURATION: 1s, 2s, 5s, 10s, 20s, 40s, 1m, 2m, 5m, 10m, 20m, 40m, 1h, 2h, 5h.
- Note: Power outputs are examples using fairly common elite-rider power data, not definitive values for each phenotype.
- Sprinter: Highest power at shortest durations (~1,300–1,400 W at 1s). Decreases steeply between 10s and 2m; lowest power profile of all phenotypes from ~5m onward. Produces high power for several seconds but cannot sustain moderate power long.
- All-rounder: Starts near ~1,350 W at 1s. Drops sharply through 10s to 1m, then stabilizes at an intermediate power level tapering toward 5h.
- Time trialist: Starts around ~1,250 W at 1s. Declines steadily to 1m, maintaining relatively higher power than sprinter and all-rounder from ~2m to 2h, converging near 5h.
- Climber: Starts lower at 1s (~1,200 W). Exhibits the flattest slope, maintaining sustained higher power across the 20s to 10m range relative to other profiles, then drops significantly from 1m to 20m, joining the other lines past 40m.
(Values are approximate readings from the figure profile.)
Workout data analysis is examined in greater detail in Chapter 14.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:power-duration-curve subtopic:phenotypes
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Power-Duration Curve and Phenotype" | pp. 17-18, 24

### [CTB-C04-006] Physiological demands of each phenotype

- Climber: the left end of the power-duration curve is lower and the slope flatter than the sprinter's, but power is maintained over time. The climber needs a very high aerobic capacity, a high anaerobic threshold and excellent economy when climbing. Climbing places the greatest total demands on physiology. One of the best predictors of climbing performance is the power-to-weight ratio (watts per kilogram, W/kg). High power and low weight give great potential when gravity is the main environmental obstacle.
- Sprinter: needs excellent economy, meaning great aerodynamics both in and out of the saddle, plus excellent pedaling and bike-handling skills. The sprinter must also reach the last kilometer with the leaders, which requires somewhat high aerobic capacity and anaerobic threshold. Sprinters are unlikely to be good climbers (courses suited to them are relatively flat). They usually lack time trial talent because their curve is low at time trial durations.
- Time trialist: power at anaerobic threshold, commonly called functional threshold power (FTP), is the best predictor of time trial performance. A high FTP is critical for all riders but especially for time trialists. On flat courses aerodynamic drag, not gravity, is the main impediment, so body weight is not a factor. Good time trialists are often relatively big riders (big muscles) because high sustainable power is what drives body and bike through the air. A big rider's drag in an aero position is not significantly greater than a small rider's. Results come down to who generates the most absolute power for the race duration. The size difference matters on climbs, not when aerodynamics is the constraint.
- All-rounder: does not excel at climbing, sprinting or time trialing but is good in at least two. Often in contention when the course does not favor specialists (e.g., an uphill sprint or a somewhat hilly time trial). The curve varies with the individual blend of talents.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:threshold system:vo2max metric:power level:all subtopic:phenotypes subtopic:power-to-weight subtopic:ftp
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Power-Duration Curve and Phenotype" | pp. 17-18

### [CTB-C04-007] Supercompensation

- A high-dose workout only produces the potential for fitness; fitness is realized in the following hours of rest and recovery, especially sleep. Easy days are when the body becomes more fit.
- Low-dose workouts follow high-dose ones to prevent breakdowns. Alternating stress and rest is necessary to become fitter. Supercompensation = the adaptation of increased fitness resulting from alternating stress and rest.
- It cannot be forced or sped up. Fast and slow responders exist, likely genetic (individuality). To avoid overtraining while improving, watch how the body responds and do not artificially speed the process.
- Applying only high dose and high density without adequate recovery likely causes overtraining (Chapter 10): a condition far beyond fatigue, like a severe illness (Lyme disease, mononucleosis, chronic fatigue syndrome); it has ended otherwise successful careers. Must be avoided.
- A rising weekly training load combined with rest makes supercompensation likely.
tags: type:mechanism level:all sport:cycling subtopic:supercompensation subtopic:overtraining
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | p. 34; summary p. 39

### [CTB-C04-008] Fitness as race readiness; four ways to gauge it

Friel proposes thinking of fitness as "race readiness". Four ways to determine changes:
1. Race results: ultimate measure, but late (rearview mirror).
2. How workouts go and feel: indicates direction (increasing, stable, decreasing) but subjective, not foolproof.
3. Lab/clinic testing on an ergometer: objective, good data if repeated a few times a season, but expensive for multiple tests over weeks.
4. Tracking daily training load over time: inexpensive, fairly accurate indirect measure. If training load rises, fitness is rising because you can handle greater dose and perhaps density. A mathematical model, less accurate than lab test or race result, but helpful: steadily increasing weekly load over several weeks means gaining fitness; decreasing load means losing fitness.
tags: type:monitoring level:all sport:cycling metric:load subtopic:fitness-assessment
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 34-37

### [CTB-C04-009] Fitness, fatigue and their relationship

- Rising weekly training load implies fitness gain and also fatigue; falling load means losing fitness and shedding fatigue (recovering). Fitness and fatigue trend in the same direction; to become fitter you must become fatigued ("fatigue is your friend"); without frequent fatigue you lose fitness.
- Fatigue appears before fitness: after a hard workout you are tired tomorrow, but fitness change is not measurable then. Fitness changes slowly (weeks), fatigue rapidly (hours and days).
- TrainingPeaks Performance Management Chart shows training load progress across the season and reveals fatigue as well.
tags: type:mechanism level:all sport:cycling metric:load subtopic:fitness-fatigue
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 36-37

### [CTB-C04-010] Form and the taper logic

- "Form" originated in late-1800s European horse racing (bookie's sheet listing recent performance), adopted by bike racing.
- In cycling, form means racing well; the rider is fresh: much fatigue recently shed with only slight loss of fitness. Form consists first and foremost of freshness; a fatigued rider cannot be on form regardless of fitness; significant fatigue always limits performance.
- Way to be fresh on race day: rest in the preceding days (taper). During a taper fatigue and fitness both fall, but fatigue falls much faster, so fitness is lost very slowly while nearly all fatigue is shed. On race day you feel as if you gained fitness, though it is less fatigue: you are on form. Goal: lose little fitness, shed nearly all fatigue (Chapters 11, 13). Rest and recovery also contribute to form.
tags: type:mechanism level:all sport:cycling phase:taper subtopic:form subtopic:taper
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 37-38

### [CTB-C04-011] Speed as a measure of intensity (why not used)

- Speed is the ultimate measure of performance (the fastest rider wins), but it is a poor measure of training intensity because it is highly sensitive to wind and hills.
- Riding into a wind makes speed disagree with effort; a tailwind speed does not mean you will be fast racing. Even a slight wind or low-grade hill has a large impact.
- Conclusion: speed is not a good way to measure training on the bike relative to using intensity.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling subtopic:speed metric:pace level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 41-42

### [CTB-C04-012] Using RPE: value, practice and group effect

- RPE is the most basic way to express intensity, but it has great value: it keeps you in touch with your total body rather than only external numbers.
- Road racing requires a subjective sense of how hard you are working. In a mass-start race you seldom can make critical decisions from power or heart rate; whether to go hard depends largely on how you feel. The real-time intensity of a road race is best measured by perceived exertion.
- Before HR monitors and power meters, RPE (in descriptive words) was how all riders gauged intensity. The numeric scale was added by sports scientists to make it more precise and more useful with a training plan.
- Skill: learned through frequent use. Assign a numeric RPE often during workouts, especially those with variable intensity (intervals, group rides); do it on every ride to get good at split-second race decisions.
- Group effect: RPE is usually lower in a group (race, group ride, training with a buddy) than riding alone at the same power or heart rate; we can work harder at a lower perceived exertion with others, likely due to motivation.
- RPE is entirely subjective; objective tools (heart rate monitor, power meter) make it more accurate.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling metric:rpe level:all subtopic:rpe-use
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 42-43

### [CTB-C04-013] Heart rate monitor vs power meter: what each measures

- Author requires the riders he coaches to have both. Each measures intensity uniquely, giving information the others do not.
- Heart rate monitor: invented late 1970s in Finland for Nordic skiers; popular in the 1980s; common across endurance sports by early 1990s. Heart rate tells how intensely you are working (effort), not performance: the last finisher can have the same average HR as the winner; it does not say how fast you go. Similar to RPE but more precise; the best tool to accurately measure effort.
- Power meter: invented about ten years after the HR monitor by Uli Schoberer (German engineer turned cyclist); about 20 years to be widely adopted. Designed to accurately measure intensity (performance). It directly measures only force applied to the pedal (torque) and pedal speed (cadence); their combination is expressed in watts. Before it, cyclists focused on volume; it revolutionized training.
- Further detail on power meters: The Power Meter Handbook (Friel); Training and Racing with a Power Meter (Allen and Coggan).
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling metric:hr,power level:all subtopic:measurement-tools
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | p. 43

### [CTB-C04-014] Threshold behavior: heart rate vs power at AnT; races decided above AnT

- With training you can raise power at AnT (ride faster before hitting AnT), but heart rate at AnT stays about the same despite fitness changes. This plays a role in what is called fitness.
- Road racers spend most race time between AeT and AnT, but outcomes are almost always determined at and above AnT: very brief episodes lasting seconds to a few minutes (sprinting, climbing, breakaways, closing gaps, "guttering" opponents). Hence advanced riders must focus on high-intensity training.
- Time trials (steady pace) are usually decided by highest speed and power near AnT; constant surging for seconds/minutes is generally not a good idea (see Ch 6).
- AeT fitness is what gets you to the point in the race where above-AnT fitness kicks in (hard climb, attack, sprint). Both are critical.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:threshold metric:hr,power level:advanced subtopic:race-determinants
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 45-47

### [CTB-C04-015] The three physical metrics of endurance fitness

- Only three physical metrics of endurance fitness must be developed through training: aerobic capacity, anaerobic threshold, and economy. All humans are the same in these three; what varies is how well each is developed. Every rider has a unique blend.
- None alone determines race outcome; strategy, tactics, pacing, nutrition, heat adaptation and more also matter.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling subtopic:fitness-determiners level:all
src: Ch 6 "What Is Fitness?" | pp. 72-73
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C04-016] Aerobic capacity (VO2max): meaning, determinants and how to raise it

- Aerobic capacity (VO2max) is the physiological proficiency for using inhaled oxygen to produce energy: the maximal volume of oxygen. More oxygen processed means more energy and greater power.
- The fastest athletes often have the highest aerobic capacities, but VO2max alone does not predict finishing order. It is the "ticket to the club": world-class riders must have high aerobic capacity, but all top riders do. Much of it is inherited; training is needed for it to play a role in results.
- Starts with the heart: stroke volume (oxygen-carrying blood pumped per beat). Two ways to raise it:
  1. Long-duration, high-volume workouts: the heart responds to lots of saddle time at elevated intensity, above about 50 percent of VO2max.
  2. High-intensity intervals, especially near the power associated with VO2max (determined with the functional aerobic capacity power field test, Appendix B). The heart approaches its maximum pumping rate; stroke volume rises more quickly and completely than with long rides alone.
- Most seasoned athletes use both strategies.
- Other contributors: muscle aerobic enzymes, blood vessel diameter and ability to dilate, blood volume, hematocrit (red cell count). Each has inherited and trained components.
- Body weight: VO2max is expressed as mL O2 per kg per minute, so losing body weight (especially excess fat rather than cycling-specific muscle) raises VO2max. Weight gain slows uphill riding at the same power. On flat rides weight is not a disadvantage, so big riders with huge aerobic capacities do well on the flat and are dropped uphill.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:vo2max metric:power subtopic:stroke-volume level:all
src: Ch 6 "Aerobic Capacity" | pp. 72-74
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C04-017] Anaerobic threshold and race demands

- Time trials are raced at about the anaerobic threshold, a little above or below depending on duration. A good time trialist builds a very high anaerobic threshold, using FTP as the progress marker; much training is at or near FTP. FTP is the easily established field-test substitute for the anaerobic threshold (test in Appendix B).
- Road race outcomes depend largely on brief, high-intensity episodes well above anaerobic threshold: a breakaway attempt or response is at or near aerobic capacity for a few minutes; race-winning sprints of a few seconds are at about anaerobic capacity, well above aerobic capacity.
- AnT also matters in road racing: long steady high-intensity climbs are done at or near FTP, and an established break typically proceeds at around FTP. A high FTP is beneficial, but road race results largely depend on what happens above threshold.
- AnT is highly trainable and responds well to workouts at or near threshold; it is expressed as a percentage of aerobic capacity (higher percentage = greater capability for fast racing, especially time trials and long steady climbs).
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power subtopic:ftp level:all
src: Ch 6 "Anaerobic Threshold"; Summary | pp. 74, 85
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C04-018] Economy: definition, limits and what can be improved

- Economy is how efficiently oxygen is used while riding. Oxygen consumed relates directly to energy expended when riding aerobically. Like a car's mileage rating: mL of oxygen used per mile at various intensities. The less oxygen used for a given power, the more economical. Sport science understands it less than the other two, but it may be the most important.
- Economy likely matters more as races get longer: tiny wasted energy per pedal stroke multiplies over hours. Sloppy technique or a poorly fitted bike causes unnecessary fatigue. Hence the author stresses an annual professional bike fit and skill drills early each season.
- Largely uncontrollable factors: bone structure (long thighbones relative to leg length give better leverage; narrow shoulders aid time trial aerodynamics), percentage of slow-twitch fibers (changeable only slightly, mostly genetic), mitochondria number.
- Controllable factors:
  - Technique (pedaling, cornering, climbing, sprinting). Changing technique makes you temporarily LESS economical (higher HR and RPE at a given power) for weeks or even months; afterward you are more economical at the same HR.
  - Reduce excess body weight for climbing; lighter and more aerodynamic equipment (aerobars on the TT bike, light aero wheels, aero helmet and frame). Biggest energy-saving benefit is still likely a professional bike fit.
  - High-volume training and high-intensity intervals also improve economy, with a limit.
  - Intensity and frequency are most likely to improve economy; training at high power with high cadence improves economy at all speeds and powers.
  - Long-duration workouts while trying to change technique are often counterproductive: fatigue makes skills revert to old habits. Best: frequent short sessions concentrating on skill development, ideally at the very start of the base period. Drills: "Speed Skills" in Appendix B.
  - Plyometrics (explosive jumping, bounding, hopping) and short powerful hill repeats improve muscular force and pedal power, benefiting economy.
  - Weight lifting: debated; the author believes it improves economy from experience with coached athletes after a few winter weeks of lifting (details Chapter 12).
  - Lots of aerobic endurance riding trains fast-twitch fibers to take on slow-twitch characteristics.
