tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling metric:rpe metric:hr subtopic:economy level:all
src: Ch 6 "Economy" | pp. 74-76, 85
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C04-019] Basic vs advanced abilities in linear periodization

- Basic abilities (triangle corners): aerobic endurance, muscular force, speed skills. Advanced abilities (sides): muscular endurance, anaerobic endurance, sprint power; these are the race-like abilities determining race-day preparedness.
- Advanced abilities are products of basic ones: muscular endurance from aerobic endurance + muscular force; anaerobic endurance from aerobic endurance + speed skills; sprint power from muscular force + speed skills.
- Linear ("classic") periodization: starts with basic abilities, emphasis shifts gradually to advanced, with considerable overlap, not a sudden jump. It is the oldest, simplest, most-used model.
- Base = "general preparation" (training to train; non-race-like things such as weights). Build = "specific preparation" (race-like work e.g. race-like group rides).
- Periodization origin: Soviet Union 1950s-60s, then European distance runners early 1970s; Tudor Bompa is considered a founder.
evidence: author-experience
tags: type:mechanism type:periodization level:all sport:cycling subtopic:basic-advanced-abilities subtopic:linear-periodization
src: Part IV intro; Ch 7 | pp. 87-91

### [CTB-C04-020] Eustress vs distress (Selye) and training stress capacity

- Hans Selye (mid-20th-century Austro-Hungarian endocrinologist) proposed two types of stress: eustress (healthy) and distress.
- Eustress: training load is manageable and produces increased fitness; it produces fatigue followed by fitness (Chapter 3).
- Distress: excessive training; results in excessive fatigue called overtraining. Society generally uses "stress" to mean distress (implying mental/emotional aspects), which is not how Selye described the term.
- Selye: predictable physiological reactions occur when an organism experiences either of these types of stress.
- It is sometimes necessary to flirt with distress in order to produce high performance (e.g., crash training for a stage race, Chapter 8). Distressful training is serious and can even be dangerous.
- What sets exceptionally high performers apart is how much physical training stress they can handle. Pros: 25 hours on a saddle and 500 miles a week is normal; typically average around 150 TSS per week throughout much of the season [Likely unit/typo error in source — recorded as printed; context of 25 h/week indicates ~150 TSS/day or ~1,000+ TSS/week]. Non-pros carry jobs (40-plus hours), kids, mortgage, etc.; managing it comes down to how much stress of all kinds can be handled.
- Finding your limit: push limits (more miles, more intensity, or both) only infrequently and only when lifestyle stress is very low (cycling camp, vacation from work), paying very close attention to the body.
tags: type:mechanism type:philosophy sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:eustress-distress subtopic:training-stress-capacity metric:load
src: Ch 10 "Training Stress" | pp. 163-164
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C04-021] Risk and reward in training (Figure 10.1)

- Risk: workouts/training methods with high potential for breakdown because they are strenuous. Breakdowns several times per season mean workouts are unnecessarily stressful or hard workouts are crowded too closely together. Sports medicine calls it "too much, too soon." It breaks the dose and density concept (Chapter 3): dose too great (distress) or, more likely, density wrong (hard workouts too compact in time).
- Reward: positive outcomes of appropriately stressful workouts (eustress): getting fitter and faster. Hard workouts must be kept at a dose and density that is high but just within physical limits most of the time. Crash training, used sparingly, is risky with high reward.
- Some risk is necessary for high performance; it is possible to be too conservative (little or no fitness gain for weeks). Low risk = low reward (mostly short, low-intensity workouts: no setbacks but poor race results). Serious riders nearly always overtrain, rarely undertrain. Analogy: stock investing (blue chip = low risk/low reward; new technology = both high).
- Overinvesting in high-risk training (excess volume or intensity) is likely to cause injury, illness, burnout, or overtraining.
- Figure 10.1 (risk-reward curve): X-axis workout difficulty (intensity, frequency, duration, mode), low to high; zones left to right: LOW RETURN, OPTIMAL, CAUTION, AVOID. Reward rises through LOW RETURN, peaks in OPTIMAL, declines through CAUTION and AVOID. Risk is low/flat in LOW RETURN, begins to curve up in OPTIMAL, rises steeply in CAUTION (crossing above reward near the CAUTION/AVOID boundary), spikes in AVOID.
- Key is balance: knowing what your body can handle, patience, commitment to the plan.
tags: type:principle type:mechanism level:advanced subtopic:risk-reward subtopic:dose-density metric:load
src: Ch 10 "Risk and Reward" | pp. 164-165
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C04-022] How overtraining occurs and time to onset

- Overtraining happens when an athlete ignores fatigue from overreaching and continues high-stress training with inadequate rest and recovery, leading to a decline in fitness, performance, and health.
- Time from overreached to overtrained varies:
  - Young riders: may press on for several weeks on the edge of exhaustion (a month or more) before becoming officially overtrained.
  - Senior (masters) riders and those relatively new to the sport: may become overtrained in less than 3 weeks.
  - Other individual characteristics may speed up or delay onset.
- If only overreached: shed fatigue by resting or training very easily for a few days, then return to normal high-stress training. Once overtraining syndrome has fully taken over, fatigue will not go away easily; the rider becomes listless, grumpy, and unmotivated (usually identified by spouse or close friend).
- Symptoms vary by athlete (individual hormonal strengths and weaknesses, workout duration and intensity interplay over several weeks); some contradictory (hungrier than normal vs loss of appetite) owing to symptom progression and source (excessively high duration vs intensity).
- Before arriving at overtraining syndrome, athletes often become sick or injured, forcing a halt to hard training: undesirable but preferable to overtraining syndrome ("the body's way of protecting itself from your avarice").
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:advanced level:masters subtopic:overtraining-onset subtopic:age subtopic:masters
src: Ch 10 "Avoiding Overtraining", "Summary: Training Stress" | pp. 166-167, 170
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C04-023] Overcompensation and adaptation during recovery

- Recovery restores and rebuilds the body: repairs damaged cells, creates new neural pathways, expands capillary beds, rebalances chemistry, develops muscle fibers. This makes affected systems slightly better able to handle the stress that produced the fatigue = overcompensation, the heart of adaptation.
- The three determiners of endurance fitness (aerobic capacity, anaerobic threshold, economy) improve slightly after recovery from a hard workout. Degree of improvement depends on type and magnitude of workout stress and quality and quantity of recovery.
- Changes in overcompensation: muscle strength improves; stroke volume increases; muscle capillary networks become denser (more oxygen and fuel delivery); blood volume increases; aerobic enzymes increase; glycogen stores are restocked. The full list is long.
- Recovery time needed depends on stress: a workout only slightly harder than current adaptation, ready again in about 48 hours or less; a workout far harder needs longer.
- Fatigue is nature's way of preventing back-to-back hammer sessions that would tear the body down beyond adaptation. Exceptionally high fatigue plus too little recovery leads to overtraining.
- Recovery and adaptation are probably not exactly the same: one can feel recovered (low morning warning score) before adaptation is complete. Adaptation may take longer than recovery. Primary mission after a hard workout is to become more fit, not simply to feel less tired.
- Rushing into a limit-testing workout before recovery is complete reduces adaptation (less fitness gained than with slightly longer rest).
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:aerobic level:all subtopic:overcompensation subtopic:adaptation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 intro, "Recovery and Adaptation", Summary | pp. 173, 178-181, 190-191

### [CTB-C04-024] Power = force x velocity; role of the nervous system in force

Formula: P = F × v. In cycling, force = pressure on the pedal; velocity = cadence. Power rises by turning cranks faster or by applying more force at the same cadence (bigger gear). Riding fast = turning a high gear at a high cadence. Chapter 12 addresses the force component. Strength is also needed in hips and torso so force is transmitted without power leaks.

Nervous system: strength is not only muscle size; muscles need not become bulky to create high force. The nervous system contributes in three ways:
1. Muscle recruitment: how many available fibers can be recruited. High loads and fast movements train fibers to respond; without such training some stay dormant.
2. Coordination: recruiting the proper fibers for the specific movement when needed, and leaving unneeded fibers inactive. Inappropriate fibers reduce efficiency and add metabolic cost. Hence exercises should simulate the key movement (driving the pedal down).
3. Synchronization: sequencing of activation; about a dozen muscle groups must fire in under one second per crank revolution. Simulating pedaling increases the rate at which fibers are activated.
Neuromuscular training is called the most effective off-the-bike physical preparation for high-performance racing. Greater force means better sprinting, climbing and time trialing.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:neuromuscular metric:power subtopic:neuromuscular-force
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 "Muscular Force Training"; "The Nervous System and Force" | pp. 195-196

## C05 Glossary

### [CTB-C05-001] Abilities (the six)

The author organizes all workouts into six categories called "abilities," each related to aerobic capacity, anaerobic threshold and economy.
- Basic abilities: aerobic endurance, muscular force, speed skills.
- Advanced abilities: muscular endurance, anaerobic endurance, sprint power.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:abilities level:all
src: Ch 6 "Abilities" | p. 76

### [CTB-C05-002] Ability

In the context of this book, ability is a category of workouts focused on an intended physical adaptation in preparation for racing. See adaptation, anaerobic endurance, muscular endurance, muscular force, speed skill, and sprint power.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:abilities
src: Glossary | p. 269
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-003] Active recovery / Passive recovery

Active recovery: low-intensity exercise intended to allow for recovery.
Passive recovery: a day or group of days with no workouts with the purpose of complete rest.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:recovery
src: Glossary | pp. 269, 275
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-004] Active recovery vs passive recovery

Active recovery: an easy ride (zone 1) as recovery (example workout AE1, Appendix B). Passive recovery: a day off the bike. Most advanced athletes find active recovery stimulates recovery; most novices and many intermediates find passive recovery (day off) usually better.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:active-recovery subtopic:passive-recovery
src: Ch 11 | p. 174

### [CTB-C05-005] Adaptation

The body's physiological adjustment to a training stress repeatedly placed on it over a period of time. The purpose of adaptation is to improve an aspect of fitness. See ability and fitness.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:adaptation
src: Glossary | p. 269
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-006] Aerobic / Anaerobic

Aerobic: in the presence of oxygen; aerobic metabolism primarily uses oxygen to produce energy. Also refers to any exercise intensity below the anaerobic threshold.
Anaerobic: literally "without oxygen." Very high-intensity exercise during which the demand for oxygen is greater than can be met. The primary fuel when anaerobic is carbohydrate. Also used to describe the intensity of exercise performed above the anaerobic, or lactate, threshold.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:aerobic system:anaerobic level:all subtopic:energy-systems
src: Glossary | p. 269
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-007] Aerobic capacity (VO2max)

The maximal volume of oxygen an athlete can process to produce energy during a maximal and prolonged exertion. Also known as VO2max, aerobic capacity and maximal oxygen consumption. Determined in a graded exercise test by measuring oxygen uptake in milliliters divided by the athlete's body weight (kg) and by the number of minutes at that maximal intensity level (VO2max = mL/kg/min). VO2max is the athlete's physical capacity for oxygen consumption during a maximal endurance exertion and is closely related to endurance fitness.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:vo2max metric:power level:all subtopic:vo2max
src: Glossary | pp. 269, 277
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-008] Aerobic endurance

In the context of this book, a category of workouts done at or near the aerobic threshold and intended to improve the athlete's aerobic ability.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:aerobic level:all subtopic:abilities
src: Glossary | p. 269
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-009] Aerobic threshold (AeT)

Lower of the two intensity reference points. Lab: lactate about 2 mmol/L (resting is about 1 mmol/L); RPE about 3 to 4 (about 3.5, "moderate"). At AeT the body removes the lactate-associated acid as quickly as it enters the muscles. Sustainable for several hours without agony. Can also be found with gas analysis (oxygen/carbon dioxide via face mask, noninvasive but expensive), which approximates lactate changes.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:aerobic metric:rpe level:all subtopic:aerobic-threshold
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 44-45

### [CTB-C05-010] Agonist muscles / Antagonist muscles

Agonist muscles: the primary movement muscles that contract to propel the body in activities such as cycling.
Antagonist muscles: muscles that have an opposing effect on the agonist muscles by opposing their contraction. Example: triceps is antagonist to biceps; the biceps flexes the elbow, the triceps extends it. When doing arm curls with a heavy weight, the triceps are the antagonists.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:muscles
src: Glossary | pp. 269-270
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-011] All-rounder

A rider who is good at two or three of the basic disciplines (climbing, sprinting, time trialing) but excels at none. Often the goal is a race series with different events or a weekend stage race.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:phenotypes sport:cycling
src: Ch 2 | pp. 16, 18

### [CTB-C05-012] Anaerobic endurance

Ability trained with short, highly intense intervals in zone 5 (about RPE 9). The author notes the name is a scientific misnomer dating to the 1960s: above AnT you are by definition anaerobic (hydrogen ions accumulate, short-term fatigue; fatigue is not caused by lactic acid), but oxygen is still being used, so you are also aerobic.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:anaerobic-endurance level:all
src: Ch 6 | p. 80

### [CTB-C05-013] Anaerobic threshold (AnT)

A high intensity level that occurs immediately before becoming anaerobic. Above this intensity, energy production becomes anaerobic, with energy supplied by stored carbohydrate. High efforts above the AnT can be continuously maintained for a period lasting from a few minutes to about an hour, depending on how great the intensity is.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:threshold level:all subtopic:anaerobic-threshold
src: Glossary | p. 270
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-014] Anaerobic threshold (AnT) and lactate threshold

Upper reference point. RPE about 7 ("very hard"); lactate about 4 mmol/L. Above it you begin to "redline"; suffering increases continually above 7. Lactate is produced faster than it is removed; acid (hydrogen ions and other chemicals) accumulates and restricts muscle contraction; the body cannot remove it unless you slow considerably, so the effort can be held only a limited time. A very fit athlete can hold AnT roughly an hour (with a lot of suffering); summary says typically about 45 minutes to just over an hour depending on endurance fitness.
"Lactate threshold" is sometimes used for the same 7 RPE; strictly, AnT is determined by gas analysis and lactate threshold by blood lactate, but the book treats them as quite similar (RPE 7).
Lactate itself is not the direct cause of fatigue or muscle soreness; it is a good predictor of exercise intensity (more lactate, greater exertion). Measured in mmol/L.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:threshold system:lactate metric:rpe level:all subtopic:anaerobic-threshold
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 44-45, 53

### [CTB-C05-015] Anaerobic threshold (AT)

The percentage of aerobic capacity that a rider is capable of sustaining for a long time, such as 40 to 70 minutes. Sometimes called "lactate threshold," though physiologically these are not exactly the same thing. The higher the percentage, the greater the anaerobic threshold fitness.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:threshold level:all subtopic:anaerobic-threshold
src: Ch 2 "The Right Stuff" | pp. 16-17

### [CTB-C05-016] Base period

In seasonal periodization, the training period during which the workouts are general, meaning not exactly like the demands of the targeted event. The purpose is to prepare the body for the training stresses of the build period. See build, peak, prep, race, and transition periods.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling phase:base level:all subtopic:periodization
src: Glossary | p. 270
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-017] Block periodization (definition)

Nonlinear model devised by Vladimir Issurin in the 1980s: each mesocycle (a block) focuses on only one type of workout/dominant ability while a secondary ability is maintained with about half the frequency.
tags: type:definition level:elite subtopic:block-periodization sport:cycling
src: Ch 9 | pp. 155-156

### [CTB-C05-018] Bonk

A state of extreme exhaustion during a very long endurance session; related to the depletion of glycogen.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:glycogen
src: Glossary | p. 270
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-019] Breakthrough (BT)

A challenging workout intended to cause a significant, positive, adaptive response. These workouts generally require 36 or more hours for adequate recovery.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:breakthrough-workout
src: Glossary | p. 270
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-020] Breakthrough workout (BT)

The hard workouts that help you break through to a new level of fitness. Every workout resulting in physical stress and fatigue is a BT. Advanced ability workouts are very effective; basic ability workouts are more subtle. Test: if you are tired the day after and feel the need to recover, it was a BT.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:breakthrough-workout
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | p. 131

### [CTB-C05-021] Build period

In seasonal periodization, the training period during which the workouts are specific, meaning very much like the demands of the targeted event. The purpose is to prepare the body for the training stresses of racing.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling phase:build level:all subtopic:periodization
src: Glossary | p. 270
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-022] Cadence / Cranks / Gear / Mash

Cadence: revolutions per minute of pedal stroke.
Cranks: the levers on which the pedals are attached.
Gear, high and low: one crank revolution of a high gear covers a greater distance than one in a low gear; a high gear generally requires greater force to turn the cranks.
Mash: to push a bicycle in high gear at a slow cadence.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:cadence level:all subtopic:bike-terms
src: Glossary | pp. 270, 271, 272, 274
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-023] Capillary

A small blood vessel between arteries and veins in which oxygen and fuel exchanges between tissue (such as muscle) and blood occur. Several capillaries at a site form a capillary bed. As aerobic fitness improves in a muscle, the capillary beds for that muscle are enlarged.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:aerobic level:all subtopic:capillaries
src: Glossary | p. 270
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-024] Carbohydrate loading

A dietary procedure intended to elevate muscle and liver glycogen stores by emphasizing carbohydrate consumption for a few days prior to a race.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:carbohydrate-loading
src: Glossary | p. 270
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-025] Cardiorespiratory system / Central nervous system

Cardiorespiratory system: the combined interactions of the heart, blood vessels, and lungs to supply fuel and oxygen to the working muscles during exercise.
Central nervous system: the brain and spinal cord.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:physiology
src: Glossary | p. 270
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-026] Central Governor Theory

A model of fatigue proposed by Tim Noakes, PhD, positing that fatigue is centrally regulated in the brain rather than solely in the peripheral muscles. The brain monitors all somatic and physiological feedback and initiates fatigue or reduces power output as a self-protective mechanism to prevent severe somatic damage.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:central-governor subtopic:fatigue
src: Ch 1 "Commitment and Tenacity" | p. 10

### [CTB-C05-027] Christmas stars

Riders who train with too much aggression at the start of the season and are in the best shape of the season in midwinter; by the time the race season starts they are often burnt out.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:overtraining
src: Ch 5 | p. 62

### [CTB-C05-028] Circuit training / Compound exercise / Free weights / One-repetition max (1RM)

Circuit training: selected gym exercises or activities performed rapidly in sequential order; a term often used in weight training.
Compound exercise: in weight lifting, an exercise using multiple joints, usually as recruited while cycling. Example: the squat involves hips, knees and ankles and is somewhat similar to the lower body movement when pedaling.
Free weights: weights such as barbells and dumbbells that are not part of an exercise machine.
One-repetition max (1RM): the maximum load an athlete can lift in one attempt.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:weight-lifting
src: Glossary | pp. 271, 272, 274
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-029] Climber / Sprinter / Time trialist / Phenotype

Phenotype: the individual cyclist's unique and specific aptitude for performance in a bike race when certain race talents are required. See all-rounder.
Climber: a cyclist with a unique capacity for riding uphill, usually marked by a high power-to-weight ratio.
Sprinter: a cyclist with the capacity to produce very high power output for a brief period, usually seconds; commonly related to high fast-twitch muscle composition.
Time trialist: a cyclist with the capacity for steadily producing a relatively high power output for a long period, especially in an individual time trial; common for a rider with high slow-twitch muscle composition.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:phenotype
src: Glossary | pp. 271, 275, 276, 277
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-030] Commitment

Defined by Friel as "passion for your goal." It is demonstrated not merely during competition, but through daily training consistency, adherence to standard preparation routines, and aligning all daily decisions (nutrition, rest, social life) with the demands of the goal.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:commitment
src: Ch 1 "Mental Toughness", "Commitment and Tenacity" | pp. 8, 10-11

### [CTB-C05-031] Concentric contraction / Eccentric contraction

Concentric: shortening of a muscle during its contraction, as with the biceps in an arm curl. When pedaling, the quadriceps is used concentrically.
Eccentric: lengthening of a muscle during its contraction, as when the biceps slowly lowers a weight lifted during an arm curl.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:muscle-contraction
src: Glossary | p. 271
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-032] Crash training

A training method of closely spaced breakthrough workouts over several consecutive days (typically 2 to 4 days) designed to simulate the cumulative fatigue and back-to-back demands of a stage race, followed by 3 to 5 days of rest and recovery. The name explicitly implies risk: without conservative management and careful monitoring, it carries a high likelihood of "crash and burn" resulting in overtraining, illness, or injury.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:crash-training subtopic:stage-race
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 132-133

### [CTB-C05-033] Crosstraining / Drafting / Drops / Hoods

Crosstraining: workouts involving activities not common to the athlete's primary sport (e.g., weight lifting and cross-country skiing for a cyclist).
Drafting: riding closely behind another cyclist to reduce effort.
Drops: the lower portion of turned-down handlebars on road bicycles.
Hoods: on drop handlebars, the covers over the brake lever mechanisms.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:bike-terms
src: Glossary | pp. 271, 273
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-034] Cycling phenotype

The rider's sport-related strength; the personal racing characteristic as a climber, sprinter, time trialist, or all-rounder. Every rider fits one of these categories.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:phenotype sport:cycling
src: Prologue | p. xiii

### [CTB-C05-035] Density

A measure of how closely spaced the high-dose workouts are. High density: hardest workouts very close, perhaps one day apart or back-to-back. Low density: several low-dose days between the hardest sessions.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:density
src: Ch 3 | p. 33

### [CTB-C05-036] Dose

How relatively hard a workout is in terms of duration or intensity. High dose: very long ride, highly intense session (intervals, group ride), or both. Low dose: typically short duration and low intensity.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:dose
src: Ch 3 | p. 33

### [CTB-C05-037] Duration / Frequency / Intensity / Volume / Workload

Duration: the length of time of a given workout.
Frequency: the number of times per week an athlete trains.
Intensity: the qualitative element of training referring to effort, speed, velocity, pace, force, and power.
Volume: a quantitative measure of training expressing how much training is done in a given time frame such as a week. Commonly based on cumulative TSS, total miles or kilometers, or collective hours. Results from the combination of individual workout durations and their frequency.
Workload: the total stress applied in training through the combination of frequency, intensity, and duration for a given period such as a week. Expresses quantitative and qualitative aspects in a single number. Common measurements: cumulative TSS and kilojoules (kJ).
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:volume metric:load level:all subtopic:training-variables
src: Glossary | pp. 271, 273, 278
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-038] Economy

The physiological cost of riding a bike, commonly expressed as liters of oxygen consumed for a given duration or distance. The more economical the athlete, the less oxygen consumed at any given pace or power.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:economy
src: Glossary | p. 271
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-039] Economy (cycling)

A marker of endurance fitness defined by how much energy a rider wastes while riding. Economy is largely determined by physical structure, but also includes pedaling style, position on the bike, and bike-handling skills. The less energy wasted, the better the economy.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:economy
src: Ch 2 "The Right Stuff" | p. 17

### [CTB-C05-040] Endurance / Fatigue / Overreaching / Overtraining

Endurance: the ability to persist, or resist fatigue, for a relatively long duration.
Fatigue: in endurance sport, the long-term accumulation of tiredness resulting from training.
Overreaching: training above a workload that would produce overtraining if such a load were continued long enough.
Overtraining: a physical and mental condition marked by extreme fatigue caused by training for an excessive period at a workload higher than the body can readily adapt to; the result of an imbalance between training stress and rest.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:overtraining
src: Glossary | pp. 271, 272, 274
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-041] Ergogenic aid

A substance, device, or phenomenon that can improve athletic performance. For example, caffeine is often considered an ergogenic aid for endurance athletes. Some ergogenic aids are banned from endurance sports.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:ergogenic-aid
src: Glossary | p. 271
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-042] Fartlek

A Swedish term for "speed play." An unstructured interval-type workout in which the intensity and duration of the intervals and the recovery times between them are completely subjective, spur-of-the-moment decisions.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:fartlek
src: Glossary | p. 272
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-043] Fast-twitch (FT) and Slow-twitch (ST) muscle fiber

Fast-twitch: brief contraction time, high anaerobic capacity, low aerobic capacity; suited for high-power activities such as sprints.
Slow-twitch: slow contraction time, low anaerobic capacity, high aerobic capacity; suited for low-power, long-duration activities.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:muscle-fiber
src: Glossary | pp. 272, 276
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-044] Field test

A way of measuring fitness without going to a lab or clinic, done on the road or trainer with heart rate monitor and power meter. Downside: variables not controlled (equipment, weather, nutrition incl. pre-test caffeine, warm-up, course) can cause 1-3% changes between tests; it is a "black box" test (gives a number, not physiological detail).
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:field-test
src: Ch 5 | pp. 66-67

### [CTB-C05-045] Fitness

In the context of competitive cycling, Friel defines fitness as "race readiness." It is indirectly reflected by the capacity to handle increasing training loads over time.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:fitness
src: Ch 3 | pp. 34-36

### [CTB-C05-046] Fitness (taper context)

Fitness: the result of many small physical performance gains over consecutive workouts, grown over weeks, months and years. In sports science, cycling fitness is an individual's mix of aerobic capacity, anaerobic threshold and economy (Ch 6). It may also be thought of as the product of an increasing training load (saddle time, distance, intensity, TSS). It is the starting place for race readiness.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:fitness
src: Ch 13 "Fitness" | pp. 229-230

### [CTB-C05-047] Fitness / Form

Fitness: in endurance sport, the combined product of the athlete's aerobic capacity, anaerobic threshold (as a percentage of aerobic capacity), and economy.
Form: the athlete's readiness to race. On race day the athlete should have a relatively high level of race readiness and be fresh, meaning without fatigue.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:fitness-form
src: Glossary | p. 272
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-048] Force / Torque

Force: the muscular work done to overcome a resistance (e.g., pushing down on a pedal).
Torque: in pedaling, the rotational force applied to the pedals.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:force
src: Glossary | pp. 272, 277
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-049] Form

Form: race readiness beyond physical fitness. It has two parts:
1. Readiness: being physically and mentally prepared, including a well-planned strategy, rehearsed tactics, preparation for expected environmental conditions (heat, humidity, cold, wind, rain, snow, course such as hills), nutrition before and during the race, hydration and equipment selection.
2. Freshness: the absence of fatigue. The only effective way to remove fatigue is to reduce training load. Less training means more form.
The properly tapered athlete sees form as faster racing.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling phase:taper level:all subtopic:form subtopic:freshness
src: Ch 13 "Form" | p. 230

### [CTB-C05-050] Functional Aerobic Capacity Power (FACP)

Power output associated with aerobic capacity (VO2max), closely associated with the anaerobic endurance ability. Measured using the functional aerobic capacity power field test (Appendix B) and tracked alongside body weight over time as an indicator of climbing potential.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:vo2max metric:power subtopic:facp level:all
src: Ch 6 "Aerobic Capacity", "Ability Limiters" | pp. 73, 83

### [CTB-C05-051] Functional threshold (FT), FTHR, FTP

Functional threshold: Andrew Coggan's (PhD, exercise physiologist and cyclist; early 2000s) field-test estimate of AnT, called "functional" because it is not an actual lab measure of lactate/anaerobic threshold. Products: functional threshold heart rate (FTHR) and functional threshold power (FTP). Based on the idea that a fit athlete can sustain AnT about an hour. Free and simple but not easy.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:threshold metric:hr,power level:all subtopic:functional-threshold
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 47-48

### [CTB-C05-052] Functional threshold power (FTP)

In cycling, an intensity level similar to the anaerobic threshold or lactate threshold, but based on a field test instead of clinic measurement of oxygen or lactate. The most common test lasts 20 minutes. FTP is determined by subtracting 5 percent of the average power.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power level:all subtopic:ftp
src: Glossary | p. 272
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-053] Glossary: Chapter 12 terms

- 1RM: one-repetition max; the greatest weight you can lift one time.
- Ballistic (explosive) movement: concentric portion done as fast as possible given the load, eccentric slow (about 1:2); loads 50% of 1RM or less.
- Agonist: primary working muscle for a joint movement; antagonist: the opposing muscles, which should relax.
- Concentric: muscle shortens while lifting; eccentric: muscle lengthens while lowering.
- Supersetting: alternating sets of two exercises.
- Functional force training: on-bike force reps (high gear, low cadence, seated) simulating weight lifting.
- Core: muscles from armpits to groin.
- Tonnage: load × reps summed over sets in a workout.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:glossary-ch12 level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 | pp. 198-201, 213, 225

### [CTB-C05-054] Glycogen

A source of fuel for exercise primarily derived from dietary carbohydrate. It is the body's storage form of sugar.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:glycogen
src: Glossary | p. 272
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-055] GPS device / Heart rate monitor / Power meter

GPS device: electronic mechanism, worn on wrist or on handlebars, that determines position and measures distance and speed via the U.S. Global Positioning System; data usually downloadable for analysis.
Heart rate monitor: electronic device that measures and displays pulse; data usually downloadable.
Power meter: electronic device that measures torque and cadence, thus providing a wattage reading that indicates intensity; data typically uploadable for analysis.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power metric:hr level:all subtopic:devices
src: Glossary | pp. 272, 273, 275
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-056] Growth hormone / Hamstring / Quadriceps

Growth hormone: hormone secreted by the anterior lobe of the pituitary gland that stimulates physical growth and development.
Hamstring: muscle on the back of the thigh that flexes the knee and extends the hip.
Quadriceps: large muscle in front of the thigh that extends the lower leg and flexes the hip.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:physiology
src: Glossary | pp. 272, 273, 275
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-057] Hammer

Slang for a fast, sustained, near-maximal effort.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:slang
src: Glossary | p. 273
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-058] High performance

Author's definition and usage: achieving three specific outcomes—fitness, speed, and goals—extending beyond race results to an attitude and a lifestyle pointed directly at a relentlessly pursued goal.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:high-performance sport:cycling
src: Prologue | p. xii

### [CTB-C05-059] Individuality, principle of

The concept that a training program must consider the specific needs and abilities of the person for whom it is intended, as athletes are unique in many ways and often vary considerably in their responses to training.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:individuality
src: Glossary | p. 273
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-060] Intervals / Work interval / Recovery interval / Repetition / Set

Intervals: a system of generally high-intensity work marked by short but regularly repeated periods of hard exercise interspersed with low-intensity recovery periods.
Work interval: high-intensity efforts in an interval workout separated by recovery intervals, commonly defined by duration and intensity.
Recovery interval: the relief period between work intervals, usually quite low in duration and intensity.
Repetition (rep): the number of times a task, such as a work interval or lifting a weight, is repeated.
Set: a group of repetitions.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:intervals
src: Glossary | pp. 273, 276, 278
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-061] Isolated leg training (ILT)

Pedaling the bicycle with one leg to focus on improving technique. Generally done on an indoor trainer.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:isolated-leg-training
src: Glossary | p. 273
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-062] Lactate / Lactic acid / Lactate threshold (LT)

Lactate: a chemical formed in the body that enters the bloodstream following production of lactic acid within the working muscle.
Lactic acid: by-product of exercise from incomplete breakdown of glucose in producing energy in muscles; produced during both rest and exercise.
Lactate threshold (LT): the intensity during exercise at which blood lactate begins to accumulate due to the body's inability to process it, resulting in labored breathing. Similar to the anaerobic threshold; LT is determined by sampling blood lactate, while AnT is measured by sampling inhaled and expired oxygen.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:lactate system:threshold level:all subtopic:lactate-threshold
src: Glossary | p. 273
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-063] Limiter

A goal-specific weakness that stands between you and the successful accomplishment of a seasonal goal. It is a mismatch between your weaknesses and the demands of the event you are training for. A weakness that the key races do not demand (e.g., poor climbing when the key races are flat criteriums) is not a limiter.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:limiters level:all
src: Ch 6 "Ability Limiters" | p. 81

### [CTB-C05-064] Long, slow distance (LSD)

A form of continuous training in which the athlete performs at a relatively low intensity, usually below the aerobic threshold, for a long duration.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:aerobic level:all subtopic:lsd
src: Glossary | p. 273
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-065] Mesocycle

A seasonal training period (e.g., Prep, Base 1, Build 2, Peak) lasting typically 1 to 4 weeks (microcycles), organized around specific training purposes and ability development.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:mesocycle subtopic:periodization
src: Ch 7 "Planning Overview" | p. 92

### [CTB-C05-066] Microcycle

A weekly subperiod of training; the constituent building block of a mesocycle (each mesocycle consists of 1 to 4 microcycles).
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:microcycle subtopic:periodization
src: Ch 7 "Planning Overview" | p. 92

### [CTB-C05-067] Mission

The will to pursue a goal: a purposeful attitude in which the athlete finds a way to make the goal happen regardless of obstacles and setbacks. Sequence: dream, goal, mission. A vague wish is distinct from a goal.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:mission
src: Ch 1 | pp. 5-6

### [CTB-C05-068] Morning warnings

The author's term for indicators of overreaching (early stage of overtraining) rated at the start of each day, most obvious on waking (Table 11.1). Cumulative score tells fatigue level.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:morning-warnings
src: Ch 11 | p. 177

### [CTB-C05-069] Muscular endurance

The ability to ride for a moderately long time at a moderately high effort, as in time trialing.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:muscular-endurance level:all
src: Ch 6 | p. 79

### [CTB-C05-070] Muscular endurance / Muscular force / Speed skill / Sprint power

Muscular endurance: category of workouts to improve the ability of a muscle or muscle group to perform repeated contractions for a long time while overcoming resistance.
Muscular force: category of workouts of brief repeats at maximal intensity with long recoveries between; purpose is to increase pedaling-specific strength.
Speed skill: category of workouts focused on improving the ability to efficiently move the body to produce optimal performance. See economy.
Sprint power: category of workouts done at maximal effort for a very brief time, typically at very high cadence with long recoveries between; intended to improve sprinting ability.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:abilities
src: Glossary | pp. 274, 276
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-071] Muscular force

The ability to use your cycling-specific muscles to overcome resistance.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:muscular-force level:all
src: Ch 6 | p. 77

### [CTB-C05-072] Natural recovery

Recovery that does not artificially shorten the adaptive process: sleeping, eating, drinking, and simply resting and avoiding physical stress. Contrast with alternative recovery aids that may produce a sense of being recovered before adaptation is complete.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:natural-recovery
src: Ch 11 | pp. 181-184

### [CTB-C05-073] Overcompensation (Ch 11)

The physiological reconstruction following a hard workout that makes the affected systems slightly better able to handle the stress that produced the fatigue. It is at the heart of adaptation (sports scientists' term for the adaptive time during recovery).
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:overcompensation
src: Ch 11 | p. 173

### [CTB-C05-074] Overload, Progressive overload and Specificity, principles of

Overload: a training load that challenges the body's current level of fitness and causes adaptation.
Progressive overload: the athlete's training workload must be gradually increased over time, accompanied by intermittent periods of recovery.
Specificity: training must stress the specialized systems critical for optimal performance in a given type of race in order to achieve the desired adaptations.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:training-principles
src: Glossary | pp. 274, 275, 276
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-075] Overreaching vs overtraining (overtraining syndrome)

- Overtraining (overtraining syndrome): little-understood condition in which the body is overwhelmed by training stress; a state of distress. Only common symptoms are poor performance and fatigue (which also occur when merely overreached).
- Overreaching: early stage of the progression that can lead to overtraining syndrome. Most self-described overtrained riders are actually overreached.
- Difference: when overreached, despite great tiredness, recovery is still adequate, so the body adapts and grows stronger. When overtrained, recovery is inadequate and performance and fitness decline despite a high training load.
- Overreaching is "managed overtraining"; also called "deliberate overreaching": training at high workload while constantly watching how you respond.
tags: type:definition level:advanced subtopic:overreaching subtopic:overtraining-syndrome
src: Ch 10 "Avoiding Overtraining", "Managing Overreaching" | pp. 166-169
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-076] Overtraining

A training overload that is too great for adaptation to occur, resulting in physiological breakdown rather than fitness gains. Far beyond normal acute fatigue, it presents similarly to severe chronic illness (such as Lyme disease, mononucleosis, or chronic fatigue syndrome) and can end an athletic career.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:overtraining
src: Ch 3 | pp. 28, 34

### [CTB-C05-077] Pacing

Defined by Friel as "emotion control": the discipline of containing one's emotions and holding effort in check during the early stages of a race, workout, or interval set so that energy is preserved for late in the event where competitive success is actually decided.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:pacing subtopic:patience
src: Ch 1 "Patience" | p. 9

### [CTB-C05-078] Pacing / Surging

Pacing: producing steady speed or power to carefully manage energy expenditure during a workout, race, or interval while producing the best possible performance. Unsteady pacing wastes energy.
Surging: riding unsteadily with a great deal of energy expenditure during brief accelerations.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:pacing
src: Glossary | pp. 274, 276
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-079] Peak period

In seasonal periodization, the period during which workouts are specific (very much like the demands of the targeted event) and workout durations are decreased while intensity remains high. It typically follows the build period and precedes the race period. Its purpose is to gradually produce form by allowing the body to recover from the previous period of hard training while steadily becoming race ready.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling phase:peak level:all subtopic:periodization
src: Glossary | p. 275
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-080] Perceived exertion

Your informed assessment of how hard you are working; one way of measuring intensity alongside speedometer, heart rate monitor, power meter and lactate analyzer.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling metric:rpe subtopic:perceived-exertion
src: Ch 3 | p. 31

### [CTB-C05-081] Periodization

The division of a training season into subperiods (mesocycles and microcycles), with the workouts in each period becoming progressively more race-like than those of the previous period as the athlete approaches the goal race.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:periodization
src: Ch 7 "Planning Overview" | pp. 89-90

### [CTB-C05-082] Periodization / Macrocycle / Mesocycle / Microcycle

Periodization: a seasonal planning method of structuring training into periods based on training volume and intensity, with each period focused on a specific training objective.
Macrocycle: a period of training that includes several mesocycles. Usually an entire season but may be the preparation period for a single race.
Mesocycle: a period of training generally 2 to 6 weeks long.
Microcycle: a period of training approximately 1 week long.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:periodization
src: Glossary | pp. 274-275
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-083] Phenotype (cycling)

The cycling disciplines themselves (climbing, sprinting, time trialing, all-rounder) are known as "phenotypes".
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:phenotypes sport:cycling
src: Ch 2 | p. 16

### [CTB-C05-084] Power-to-weight ratio (W/kg)

Power output divided by body mass, expressed as watts per kilogram (W/kg). One of the best predictors of cycling performance when climbing hills, where gravity is the primary environmental obstacle to performance.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:power-to-weight
src: Ch 2 "Power-Duration Curve and Phenotype" | p. 18

### [CTB-C05-085] Prep period

Preparation (prep) period: in seasonal periodization, the period during which the workouts are very general, meaning not exactly the same as the demands of the targeted event. Its purpose is to gradually return to a structured training program following a break from focused training during the preceding transition period.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling phase:prep level:all subtopic:periodization
src: Glossary | p. 275
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-086] Race period

In seasonal periodization, the period during which workouts are specific and workout durations are very brief while intensity remains high. It typically follows the peak period and culminates with the targeted race. Its purpose is to completely recover from the previous periods of hard training and become race ready.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling phase:race level:all subtopic:periodization
src: Glossary | p. 275
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-087] Rating of Perceived Exertion (RPE)

A subjective assessment of how hard one is working during exercise, usually using a 0 (low) to 10 (high) scale.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:rpe level:all subtopic:rpe
src: Glossary | p. 275
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-088] Recovery on demand

Backing off training for a few days when you feel the need, regardless of what the plan calls for. Companion to periodization on demand (moving to the next period once its objectives are achieved, regardless of period length).
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:recovery-on-demand
src: Ch 11 | p. 190

### [CTB-C05-089] Rest and recovery (R&R) period

In periodization, a period of reduced training following a block of hard training. Passive and active recovery are emphasized during R&R. R&R is typically included in training after about 2 or 3 weeks of focused training in the base and build periods.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:rest-and-recovery
src: Glossary | p. 276
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-090] Reverse linear periodization (definition)

Still a linear model but turned upside down: base period made of high-intensity, low-duration workouts; build period of low intensity with high volume.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:reverse-linear sport:cycling
src: Ch 9 | p. 152

### [CTB-C05-091] Session

A single workout or race.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:session
src: Glossary | p. 276
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-092] Slow responders and fast responders

Individual variation in the rate of training adaptation: fast responders reap physiological benefits from a given training method within weeks or even days, whereas slow responders may take months to achieve comparable gains under the exact same training protocol. The underlying mechanism is unknown but likely largely genetic.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:responders subtopic:individuality
src: Ch 2 "Training Beliefs" | p. 19

### [CTB-C05-093] Speed skills

Skill is the ability to make the movements of the sport in an efficient and effective manner. "Speed" here refers to leg speed (cadence), not bike speed: the movements are made at the speed necessary when racing without wasting energy.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:speed-skills level:all
src: Ch 6 | p. 78

### [CTB-C05-094] Sprint power

The advanced ability for sprinting at very high power outputs for a few seconds.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:sprint-power level:all
src: Ch 6 | p. 81

### [CTB-C05-095] Supercompensation

The physical adaptation of increased fitness resulting from alternating physical stress (overload) and adequate rest/recovery. A hard workout only creates the potential for fitness; supercompensation is realized during recovery, particularly sleep.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:supercompensation
src: Ch 3 | p. 34

### [CTB-C05-096] Tapering

A training method initiated a few days or weeks prior to an important race involving a gradual reduction in training volume in order to come into form on race day.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling phase:taper level:all subtopic:taper
src: Glossary | p. 276
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-097] Tempo

Commonly refers to a moderately hard intensity between the aerobic and anaerobic thresholds.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:tempo
src: Glossary | p. 276
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-098] Tenacity / Mental toughness

Tenacity (often termed mental toughness by athletes): not an all-or-nothing trait that an athlete either inherently possesses or lacks, but a trainable capacity possessed in varying degrees. It is the mental willingness and commitment to cope with extreme physical suffering and keep going in the face of fatigue when the brain urges the athlete to stop. Cultivated through race-like training that pushes physical and mental limits.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:tenacity subtopic:mental-toughness
src: Ch 1 "Commitment and Tenacity" | pp. 10-11

### [CTB-C05-099] Training / Training zones

Training: a comprehensive program or portions thereof intended to prepare an athlete for competition.
Training zones: consecutive categories of intensity based on heart rate or power specific to a cyclist's physical capacity, typically based on percentages of an athlete's unique physiological marker such as anaerobic threshold, lactate threshold, or functional threshold. Typically used to predetermine the intensity of a workout or portion of it.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:training-zones
src: Glossary | p. 277
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-100] Training diary vs. training log

- Log: a statistical record of what you did; a bunch of numbers (power meter and heart rate data with no comments or critical review).
- Diary: built around data but fleshed out with daily reactions to workouts, thoughts, feelings and training assessments; a record of metrics and of your reactions to them. A well-kept diary helps plan training, recall details of workouts and races, build self-confidence and stay accountable. For a well-informed rider it works much like a coach.
- "A log is a bunch of numbers. A diary takes you down the path to high-performance racing."
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:diary-vs-log
src: Ch 14 "Diary or Log?" | p. 238

### [CTB-C05-101] Training load

The combination of volume and intensity. If either increases while the other stays constant, or both increase, training load increases, giving greater fitness and increased fatigue. Capacity differs by individual (some manage over 20 hours per week with high dose and high density; others a much lower load) and by time available. Most athletes understand it poorly and measure it by hours, which dismisses intensity and overemphasizes combined durations. The Training Stress Score (TSS, Chapter 4) combines volume and intensity into a single number; Part IV teaches planning by TSS or time.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling metric:load subtopic:training-load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 33-34

### [CTB-C05-102] Training objective

A subgoal that defines what is needed to fix a limiter; a short-term goal to be accomplished during preparation for the most important races in order to achieve the overarching season goal.
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:training-objectives
src: Ch 5 | pp. 60-61

### [CTB-C05-103] Transition period

In seasonal periodization, the period during which workouts are relatively short and low intensity, allowing full recovery in the days or weeks immediately following a targeted race. Its purpose is to completely recover from the stresses of recent training and racing.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling phase:transition level:all subtopic:periodization
src: Glossary | p. 277
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-104] Undulating periodization (definition)

Nonlinear model that changes things around every few days/weeks by undulating duration and intensity; for cyclists, alternates a high-duration week and a high-intensity week regardless of period.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:undulating sport:cycling
src: Ch 9 | pp. 154-155

### [CTB-C05-105] Ventilatory threshold (VT)

The moment during steadily increasing exertion at which breathing first becomes labored. VT closely corresponds with the lactate and anaerobic thresholds.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:threshold level:all subtopic:ventilatory-threshold
src: Glossary | p. 277
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-106] Warm-up / Main set / Cooldown / Workout

Warm-up: the period of gradually increasing exercise intensity at the start of a session, to ready the body for the physical stress of the main set.
Main set: the primary portion of a workout focused on a specific training ability; typically follows warm-up and precedes cooldown.
Cooldown: low-intensity exercise at the end of a session intended to gradually return the body to a resting state.
Workout: a complete training session focused on a specific outcome, typically made up of warm-up, main set, and cooldown.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:workout-structure
src: Glossary | pp. 271, 274, 278
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C05-107] Workout purpose

The specific, granular goal assigned to an individual training session (e.g., target intervals, recovery ride, or skill practice), designated as "purpose" to distinguish it from higher-level seasonal goals and intermediate subgoals.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:workout-purpose
src: Ch 2 "Step 1: Clear Goal" | p. 21

## C06 Quantitative Reference

### [CTB-C06-001] Intensity measurement tools: perceived exertion, heart rate, power meter (historical note)

- Before heart rate monitors, riders set intensity strictly by perceived exertion. Heart rate monitors had been around about 20 years (by 1990s) and were relatively inexpensive.
- Power meters: technology developed in late 1980s; few riders had them in the 1990s because they cost about a month's salary for the average person and were mysterious. Prices have since dropped dramatically and training with power is common; heart rate and perceived exertion appear to be fading.
- Author's position: workouts in the book rely heavily on power metrics, but heart rate continues to play an important role and perceived exertion remains critically important for high performance.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:power-meter subtopic:heart-rate subtopic:perceived-exertion metric:power,hr,rpe sport:cycling
src: Prologue | p. xii
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C06-002] Volume vs intensity contribution to race-day fitness

Volume = frequency combined with duration; sum of weekly hours (e.g. 7 workouts × 2 hours = 14 hours weekly volume). For the typically experienced high-performance rider, volume accounts for roughly 40 percent of race-day fitness and intensity for the remaining 60 percent. Volume remains important but usually less than intensity. All zones are used in training whether using power or heart rate; time in each zone depends on target event, individual needs and current seasonal period (zones in Chapter 4).
tags: type:threshold level:advanced sport:cycling metric:volume metric:load subtopic:volume-vs-intensity
evidence: unsupported
src: Ch 3 "Volume and Intensity" | p. 32

### [CTB-C06-003] Table 4.1 The Borg 10-Point Scale for Rating of Perceived Exertion

| RATING | PERCEIVED EXERTION |
| :--- | :--- |
| 0 | Nothing at all |
| 1 | Very light |
| 2 | Light |
| 3 | Moderate |
| 4 | Somewhat hard |
| 5–6 | Hard |
| 7–8 | Very hard |
| 9 | Very, very hard (almost maximal) |
| 10 | Maximal |

RPE = a number put on the effort experienced at a given moment; scale 0 to 10.
tags: type:zones sport:cycling metric:rpe level:all subtopic:rpe-scale
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | p. 42

### [CTB-C06-004] Figure 4.1 Intensity reference points compared with RPE; three intensity categories

- Scale RPE 0-10. AeT marker between 3 and 4 (about 3.5). AnT marker at 7.
- Three broad intensity categories: below AeT, between AeT and AnT, above AnT.
- Lab values: rest about 1 mmol/L (RPE 0); AeT about 2 mmol/L (RPE 3-4); AnT about 4 mmol/L (RPE 7).
tags: type:threshold sport:cycling metric:rpe system:aerobic system:threshold level:all subtopic:intensity-reference-points
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 44-45

### [CTB-C06-005] Table 4.2 How to Determine Heart Rate Zones

| HEART RATE ZONES | MULTIPLY YOUR FTHR BY | YOUR HEART RATE ZONES |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Zone 1 | 81% | Lower than ______ |
| Zone 2 | 82%–89% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 3 | 90%–93% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 4 | 94%–99% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 5a | 100%–102% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 5b | 103%–106% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 5c | 107% | Higher than ______ |

Use the percentages of FTHR to set zones as determined by a field test. Usable whether FTHR came from the 20- or 30-minute test. (Zone 1 is "lower than 81%" and Zone 5c "higher than 107%" as laid out in the table.)
tags: type:zones sport:cycling metric:hr system:threshold level:all subtopic:hr-zones
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | p. 50

### [CTB-C06-006] Table 4.3 How to Determine Power Zones

| BIKE POWER ZONES | MULTIPLY YOUR FTP BY | YOUR BIKE POWER ZONES |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Zone 1 | 55% | Lower than ______ |
| Zone 2 | 55%–74% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 3 | 75%–89% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 4 | 90%–104% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 5 | 105%–120% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 6 | 120% | ______ – ______ |
| Zone 7 | 150% | Higher than ______ |

Use the percentages of FTP as determined by a 20-minute field test. Source: Adapted from Allen and Coggan, Training and Racing with a Power Meter. [Zone 6 is printed as 120% and Zone 5 upper bound also 120%; Zone 6 range and Zone 7 at 150% as printed — Likely typo/incomplete in source — recorded as printed]
tags: type:zones sport:cycling metric:power system:threshold level:all subtopic:power-zones
evidence: guideline (Allen and Coggan)
src: Ch 4 | p. 51

### [CTB-C06-007] Table 6.1 The Abilities to Focus on in Training for Given Limiters

| LIMITER | ABILITY |
| :--- | :--- |
| Climbing | Anaerobic endurance or muscular endurance |
| Time trialing | Muscular endurance |
| Sprinting | Sprint power |
- Climbing: anaerobic endurance for a "power" climber (courses with many short, steep hills); muscular endurance for a "time trial" climber (long steady climb). The long steady climb done like a time trial is usually the better option for a rider with a climbing limiter.
tags: type:zones sport:cycling subtopic:limiters level:all
src: Ch 6 Table 6.1 | p. 82

### [CTB-C06-013] Table 10.2 Progression from Moderate Overreaching to Overtraining Syndrome

| PROGRESSION | STAGE | COMMON SYMPTOMS |
| 1 | Moderate overreaching | High perceived fatigue; Minimal change in performance; 24–36 hours of rest restores training capacity; Performance improves following 1–2 days of rest |
| 2 | Functional overreaching | Very high perceived fatigue; Performance decline is possible; Several days of rest required to resume normal training; Performance improves following a few days of recovery |
| 3 | Nonfunctional overreaching | Very high perceived fatigue; Performance decline is obvious; Several days of rest required to resume normal training; Performance is not improved after a few days of recovery |
| 4 | Overtraining syndrome | Very heavy perceived fatigue; Performance decline is obvious; More than 1 month of rest needed to shed fatigue and resume training; Performance greatly diminished after adequate recovery time; Competitive season is over |
Stages 1-2 are deliberate, performance-enhancing overreaching; 3-4 are performance-degrading.

| REPS | FACTOR |
| 4 | 0.90 |
| 5 | 0.875 |
| 6 | 0.85 |
| 7 | 0.825 |
| 8 | 0.80 |
| 9 | 0.775 |
| 10 | 0.75 |
Find how many reps you can do with a given load for an exercise (choose a load you can lift only 4 to 10 times). Divide that load by the corresponding factor to estimate 1RM.

### [CTB-C06-015] Table 12.3 Details of the Weight Lifting Phases

| WEIGHT LIFTING PHASE | PERIOD | TOTAL SESSIONS | SESSIONS PER WEEK | LOAD (% 1RM) | SETS PER SESSION | REPS PER SET | RECOVERY BETWEEN SETS (MIN) |
| Anatomical Adaptation (AA) | Prep | 4–6 | 2–3 | 40–60 | 3–5 | 15–20 | 1–1.5 |
| Muscular Transition (MT) | Prep | 2–3 | 2–3 | 70–80 | 3–5 | 8–12 | 2–3 |
| Muscular Strength (MS) | Base 1 | 8–12 | 2–3 | 85–95 | 3–4 | 3–6 | 3–4 |
| Strength Maintenance (SM) | Base 2, Base 3, Build 1, Build 2, Peak | Indefinite | 1 | 60–85 | 2 | 6–12 | 1–2 |
No weight lifting in the 1-week race period. Emphasis shifts from lifting to riding as the season progresses: most stressful lifting (MS) in base 1 when bike training is least stressful; build force first (AA, MT, MS in prep and base 1), then maintain (SM) while building bike fitness. Doing stressful lifting alongside very high-intensity bike training makes both suffer. MS may spill into early base 2 (per summary); SM lasts until the week of the first A-priority race. MT usually late prep/early base 1.
tags: type:periodization type:dose sport:cycling phase:prep phase:base phase:strength phase:maintenance metric:1rm metric:volume level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 | pp. 220-221, 226

### [CTB-C06-016] Taper workout durations

- Two back-to-back recovery days after each hard ride: about half your normal workout duration.
- High-intensity sessions during the taper: about 80 percent of what you normally do for a high-intensity workout in the build period.
- Example of "short" for recovery rides in the peak period: 20 h/week rider: 90 min to 2 h; 10 h/week rider: 45 to 60 min.
- By the end of the peak period, weekly volume (hours or TSS) should be 30 to 50 percent lower than in the build 2 period.
tags: type:dose sport:cycling phase:taper level:advanced metric:volume metric:load subtopic:taper-volume
src: Ch 13 | pp. 232-233

### [CTB-C06-017] Table 14.1 Commonly Recorded Workout Data Based on Pure Cycling Phenotypes

| SUGGESTED WORKOUT DATA TO RECORD | CLIMBER | SPRINTER | TIME TRIALIST |
| General data for all workouts | Duration; TSS; Time by zone | Duration; TSS; Time by zone | Duration; TSS; Time by zone |
| Objective-specific data for race-like workouts | HKAE* loads (or force reps peak power); Watts/kilogram (W/kg); Surge power on climbs; 5-minute peak power & HR** on climb; 20-minute peak power & HR** on climb | HKAE* loads (or force reps peak power); 3–12 second peak power | HKAE* loads (or force reps peak power); 20–60 minute normalized power & HR**; 20–60 minute efficiency factor; 20–60 minute decoupling; Variability index |
| Test data (see Appendix B) | Aerobic threshold (T1); Functional threshold power (T2); Functional aerobic capacity (T3) | Aerobic threshold (T1); Functional threshold power (T2); Functional aerobic capacity (T3); Sprint power (T5) | Aerobic threshold (T1); Functional threshold power (T2); Functional aerobic capacity (T3); Time trial (T4) |
* HKAE = hip-, knee-, ankle-extension weight lifting exercises. ** HR = heart rate.
Notes: the all-rounder is excluded because it needs a rider-dependent combination of the three phenotypes' data; all-rounders pick and choose from the three. Phenotypes are "pure" (see Ch 2, Fig 2.1). An all-rounder/time-trialist with climber traits would not worry about surge power matching a pure climber's brief attacks; they hold a high steady sustained power to bring the climber back, but still must climb well if the goal race terrain requires. The table is suggestions only; there may be data important to you not listed. Three types: general data for all workouts (including recovery rides), objective-specific data for workouts simulating the A-race, and test data. Some power data need deeper power-based training knowledge (author's Power Meter Handbook; Allen and Coggan, Training and Racing with a Power Meter).
