src: Ch 14 Table 14.1 | p. 245

## C07 Testing & Assessment

### [CTB-C07-001] Estimating AeT without a lab

Simple, cheaper, less precise methods:
- Heart rate at about 65 percent of maximum heart rate.
- AeT is roughly 20 to 40 bpm below anaerobic threshold HR.
- With a HR monitor, assume you are quite close to AeT at 30 bpm below AnT; for the average-fitness rider this seems to work well.
Author notes AeT work is done frequently to fully develop the aerobic system.
tags: type:test sport:cycling system:aerobic metric:hr level:all subtopic:aerobic-threshold-estimate
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 44-45

### [CTB-C07-002] Field test options for functional threshold (FTP/FTHR)

Rationale: lab testing is accurate but needs frequent, costly repetition because power at AnT changes with fitness. A solo 1-hour max effort tends to under-read (low motivation); a 1-hour race (e.g., 40 km TT, 1-hour criterium) is more indicative.
Options:
- 1-hour race/effort: average HR and power = FT.
- 20-minute test: subtract 5 percent from results (Coggan).
- 30-minute test: average numbers over 30 min closely match the 20-min minus 5%; average of whole test is FTHR/FTP (no calculation).
- 8-minute all-out: subtract 10 percent from average power. [Note: p. 48 says 10%; p. 50 says 10%.]
- 5-minute test: p. 48 says subtract 15 percent; p. 50 says subtract 12 percent. [Inconsistent in source — both recorded as printed]
- Tests shorter than 20 minutes do not accurately predict FTHR; use the 20- or 30-minute test for heart rate. Shorter tests are for power only.
Accuracy: methods rarely give identical FTP; aerobically gifted riders do better on longer tests, anaerobically gifted on shorter ones; results probably within 5 percent above or below lab threshold. Values vary day to day (fatigue, lifestyle) and within a ride; only a ballpark number is needed ("Fenway Park, not Dodger Stadium").
Frequency: test every 3 to 4 weeks throughout the season so the upper reference point and zones stay accurate; more tests also improve pacing skill.
tags: type:test sport:cycling system:threshold metric:hr,power level:all subtopic:ftp-test
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 47-48, 50

### [CTB-C07-003] 20-minute FTHR/FTP test protocol (Coggan) step by step

- Course: one you can repeat; road with bike lane, light traffic, no stop signs, few intersections/corners; flat to slightly uphill (3 percent grade or less); need 5 to 10 miles (8 to 16 km) depending on grade and speed.
- Safety: keep head up, watch traffic, take no risks for data.
- Rest: do the test well rested; back off training for 2-3 days prior as for a race. A good time is the end of a rest and recovery week (Ch 11).
- Warm-up: at least 20 minutes, low intensity progressing harder; in the last 5 to 10 minutes do progressively shorter, harder accelerations at and well above RPE 7 (e.g., from 2 minutes down to 10 seconds) with nearly complete recoveries; then recover about 5 minutes at very low RPE; then start.
- Pacing: begin high but conservative (you should feel you could go much faster). Most common mistake: starting too fast then slowing considerably in the last minutes. First 5 minutes should feel relatively easy, around RPE 7. After each 5-minute portion decide to go slightly faster or slower for the next 5 (very slight RPE changes). For 8- or 5-minute tests start much harder (RPE 8 or 9) and make a pacing decision every minute.
- Finish: easy cooldown until HR and breathing return to resting levels.
- Analysis: upload to software. FTHR = average HR for the 20 min minus 5 percent (30-min test: average HR of whole test, no calculation). FTP = average power (not normalized power) for 20 min minus 5 percent (30-min: average power of whole test). 8-min: minus 10 percent. 5-min: minus 12 percent (see conflicting 15% in field-test entry). Single test gives both FTHR and FTP.
tags: type:test sport:cycling system:threshold metric:hr,power,rpe level:all subtopic:ftp-test-protocol
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 49-50

### [CTB-C07-004] Mental Skills Assessment scoring and interpretation

Scoring: add the responses (1-6) for each set of statements, then convert the total to a rating.

| MENTAL SKILL | STATEMENTS |
| :--- | :--- |
| Motivation | 2, 8, 17, 19, 23, 30 |
| Confidence | 1, 6, 11, 21, 26, 29 |
| Thought habits | 3, 5, 9, 24, 27, 28 |
| Focus | 7, 13, 14, 16, 22, 25 |
| Visualization | 4, 10, 12, 15, 18, 20 |

| If Total Is | Then Rating Is |
| --- | --- |
| 32–36 | 5 |
| 27–31 | 4 |
| 21–26 | 3 |
| 16–20 | 2 |
| 6–15 | 1 |

Interpretation:
- Serious athletes usually score high (4 or 5) in motivation. Dark side: not reducing training to rest after a hard block or refusing to back off in a taper; such athletes need a coach pulling back the reins; overtraining is likely (see Chapter 10). Manage enthusiasm.
- Confidence, thought habits, focus, visualization: a rating of 1, 2 or 3 indicates a need to improve that skill. Options: sports psychologist (most effective, most expensive); coach grounded in mental aspects; books by sports psychologists (Sidebar 5.2).
- Do the assessment at the start of the season.
tags: type:test level:all sport:cycling subtopic:mental-skills
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "Mental Assessment", Sidebar 5.1 | pp. 63-65

### [CTB-C07-005] Clinical tests vs field tests; how field tests are used

- Clinical (lab) test: technician controls equipment and warm-up, gives detailed physiology; costs money, needs scheduling; not a real-world test (ergometer, gas mask, blood draws).
- Field test: any time, on the road as you race, cheap, easy to schedule; risk of error from uncontrolled variables (1-3% test-to-test swings from equipment, weather, nutrition incl. pre-test caffeine, warm-up, course). Minimize all variation between tests (same equipment, warm-up, nutrition, course).
- Friel uses both with athletes for a broad view of the seasonal starting point; field tests are repeated periodically all season to measure progress.
- Four field tests: FAeT (aerobic endurance), FTP/FTHR, functional aerobic capacity, sprint power.
tags: type:test level:all sport:cycling subtopic:field-test
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "Fitness Assessment" | pp. 66-67

### [CTB-C07-006] Field test warm-up (all tests, Sidebar 5.3)

- Warm up well before any test; for most athletes at least 20 minutes.
- Start at low intensity, steadily ride faster and harder.
- In the last 5 to 10 minutes: progressively shorter and increasingly intense accelerations at and well above RPE 7 (see Table 4.1), e.g. starting at 2 minutes and gradually becoming 10 seconds, with nearly complete recoveries after each effort.
- Then recover about 5 minutes at very low RPE; then start the test.
tags: type:test level:all sport:cycling metric:rpe subtopic:warm-up
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 Sidebar 5.3 | p. 68

### [CTB-C07-007] Functional Aerobic Threshold (FAeT) Test

- Purpose: measure trends in aerobic endurance fitness over the season. Best done after a few days of reduced training load. May also be used as a workout to maintain/build aerobic endurance (workout AE2, Appendix B). Needs heart rate monitor and power meter.
- Protocol: after warm-up, ride steadily on flat to gently rolling course or indoor trainer for 1 to 3 hours; longer race = longer test. Goal race criterium or time trial: 1 hour. Goal race 3-hour road course: 3 hours.
- Hold AeT heart rate throughout. AeT HR from clinic/lab test, or estimate: likely 20 to 40 bpm below FTHR. Without data, use 30 bpm below FTHR plus and minus 2 bpm. Example: FTHR 152 -> AeT range 120 to 124.
- Analysis: efficiency factor EF = normalized power (NP) for the AeT portion / average heart rate for the same portion. EF rises as aerobic fitness improves; expect it to fall during greatly reduced training (e.g. end of season) — normal.
- Frequency: year-round, at least every 3 to 4 weeks; same course every time if possible.
tags: type:test level:all sport:cycling system:aerobic metric:power,hr subtopic:efficiency-factor subtopic:faet
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 Sidebar 5.3 | p. 68

### [CTB-C07-008] FTP and FTHR 20-minute test (Sidebar 5.3)

- Purpose: determine FTP (power meter required) and FTHR (heart rate monitor required) as in Chapter 4. Do after 3 to 5 days of active rest and recovery. (Also in Appendix B.)
- Course: flat to slightly uphill (3 percent grade or less), wide bike lane, light traffic, no stop signs, few intersections and corners; roughly 5 to 10 miles (8 to 16 km) depending on speed and gradient. Safe course is critical; keep head up. Indoor trainer allowed.
- Protocol: warm up well, then ride as a 20-minute time trial. Hold back slightly in the first 5 minutes (most start much too fast). At the end of each 5-minute portion decide whether to go slightly faster or slower for the next 5.
- FTHR estimate: average heart rate of the 20-minute portion minus 5 percent. Then use Table 4.2 for HR zones.
- FTP estimate: average power (not normalized power) of the 20 minutes minus 5 percent. Then use Table 4.3 for power zones.
- Alternative: 5-, 8-, and 30-minute tests (Chapter 4); the 20-minute test is preferred.
tags: type:test level:all sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power,hr subtopic:ftp subtopic:fthr
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 Sidebar 5.3 | pp. 68-69

### [CTB-C07-009] Functional Aerobic Capacity Test (5-minute)

- Purpose: determine functional aerobic capacity power (lab equivalent: VO2max). Power meter required. Best after 3 to 5 days of reduced training.
- Course: safe — light traffic, no stop signs, few intersections, no turns, wide bike lane; flat to slightly uphill (3 percent grade or less), reusable each time. Look ahead, never head down. Indoor trainer only if very stable (forceful side-to-side rocking).
- Protocol: after warm-up, ride a steady all-out effort for 5 minutes. Average power for the 5 minutes is a good predictor of power at aerobic capacity.
tags: type:test level:all sport:cycling system:vo2max metric:power subtopic:aerobic-capacity
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 Sidebar 5.3 | p. 69

### [CTB-C07-010] Sprint Power Test (8 pedal strokes)

- Purpose: gauge sprint progress. Power meter required. Do after a couple of days of reduced training load.
- Course: flat to slightly uphill, roughly 50 to 100 yards long depending on sprint speed and gradient; light traffic, wide bike lane, no intersections, no stop signs.
- Protocol: thorough warm-up; from a rolling start pedal as forcefully and quickly as possible for 8 right- or left-side pedal strokes (16 total strokes) in preferred sprint posture (standing or sitting; hands on hoods or drops). Average power over the 8 strokes = sprint power.
tags: type:test level:all sport:cycling system:neuromuscular metric:power subtopic:sprint-power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 Sidebar 5.3 | p. 69

### [CTB-C07-011] T1: Aerobic Threshold (AeT) Test

- Purpose: test of aerobic fitness. Needs heart rate monitor and power meter.
- Conditions: after 3 to 5 days of greatly reduced training (rest and recovery).
- Protocol: follow workout AE2. Afterward divide normalized power for the AeT portion by average heart rate for the same portion = current efficiency factor (EF).
- Interpretation: EF rises as aerobic fitness improves. During greatly reduced training (e.g., end of season) EF decreases, indicating loss of aerobic fitness; that is normal and expected, as fitness must decline at certain times of the year.
- Frequency: throughout the season, at least every 6 to 8 weeks; use the same course every time if possible. Results after a short R&R break are a better progress indicator than AE2 in a normal week, because fatigue is unlikely to be limiting.
tags: type:test sport:cycling system:aerobic metric:power,hr level:all subtopic:efficiency-factor subtopic:aerobic-threshold
src: Appendix B, T1 | pp. 265-266
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C07-012] T2: Functional Threshold Power (FTP) and Heart Rate (FTHR) Test

- Purpose: determine FTP (power meter required) and FTHR (heart rate monitor required) as described in Chapter 4.
- Conditions: after 3 to 5 days of rest and recovery. Course: flat to slightly uphill (3 percent grade or less), wide bike lane, light traffic, no stop signs, few intersections and corners; probably 5 to 10 miles (8 to 16 km) depending on speed and hill. Safe course is critical. Use the bike you will use for your A-priority race. Keep head up. Indoor trainer also allowed.
- Protocol: warm up well. Ride as if doing a 20-minute time trial. Hold back slightly in the first 5 minutes (most riders start much too fast). At the end of each 5-minute portion decide whether to go slightly harder or easier for the next 5 minutes.
- Calculation: FTHR = average heart rate of the 20-minute portion minus 5 percent; then use Table 4.2 for HR zones. FTP = average power (not normalized power) minus 5 percent; then use Table 4.3 for power zones.
- Alternatives: 5-, 8-, or 30-minute tests (see Chapter 4); the 20-minute test is preferred.
tags: type:test sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power,hr level:all subtopic:ftp subtopic:fthr
src: Appendix B, T2 | p. 266
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C07-013] T3: Functional Aerobic Capacity Test

- Purpose: estimate power at aerobic capacity (lab-determined VO2max). Requires a power meter.
- Conditions: after 3 to 5 days of rest and recovery. Safe course: light traffic, no stop signs, few intersections, no turns, wide bike lane; flat to slightly uphill (3 percent grade or less); reuse the same course each time. Look ahead; do not ride with head down. Indoor trainer only if very stable (forceful side-to-side rocking).
- Protocol: after warm-up, ride a steady top-end effort sustainable for 5 minutes. Average power for the 5 minutes is a good predictor of power at aerobic capacity.
tags: type:test sport:cycling system:vo2max metric:power level:all subtopic:functional-aerobic-capacity
src: Appendix B, T3 | pp. 266-267
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C07-014] T4: Time Trial (10 km)

- Conditions: after 3 to 5 days of rest and recovery. Safe road: light traffic, few intersections, no stop signs, wide bike lane; flat to slightly uphill (1 or 2 percent). Keep head up. Best on the time trial bike when preparing for a time trial. Any gearing, shifting allowed. Treat as a race. Note start and finish points to reuse the same course. Indoor trainer allowed.
- Protocol: thorough 15- to 30-minute warm-up, then a 10 km time trial on a mostly flat course.
- Interpretation: expect faster times (similar conditions) as anaerobic endurance and muscular endurance improve. Record time, average heart rate and power in the training diary.
tags: type:test sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power,hr level:all subtopic:time-trial
src: Appendix B, T4 | p. 267
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C07-015] T5: Sprint Power Test

- Purpose: gauge sprint progress. Power meter required. Done after 3 to 5 days of reduced training load.
- Course: after thorough warm-up, flat to slightly uphill (less than 4 percent) road section roughly 50 to 100 yards or meters (depending on sprint speed and gradient); light traffic, wide bike lane, no intersections, no stop signs.
- Protocol: from a rolling start, pedal as forcefully and quickly as possible, counting 8 right- or left-side pedal strokes (16 total strokes), in your preferred posture (standing or sitting, hands on hoods or drops; experiment). Peak power for the 8 strokes is the measure of sprint power.
tags: type:test sport:cycling system:neuromuscular metric:power level:all subtopic:sprint-power
src: Appendix B, T5 | p. 267
evidence: author-experience

## C08 Methodology & Progression

### [CTB-C08-001] Periodization as the hub of the book; historical context

- The author considers seasonal planning (Chapters 7, 8, 9; template in Appendix A) the hub of the book; chapters before and after are meant to enhance the individual training plan.
- History: periodization was a closely guarded training secret of Eastern Bloc countries in the 1950s-1960s, and remained an enigma in the West into the early 1980s. Before it took hold, most riders trained however they felt and decided the workout as they rolled out of the driveway; saddle time was considered the best predictor of performance.
- Several periodization methods are presented beyond classic periodization, with guidance on which suits the rider.
tags: type:periodization level:all subtopic:periodization subtopic:annual-training-plan sport:cycling
src: Prologue | p. xii
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C08-002] Strength training options (overview)

- Chapter 12 offers more options for developing the muscular force needed for high power.
- If time constrained, not all strength training needs to be in the gym: it can be done on the bike with no weight lifting.
- For a traditional gym program, the weight lifting exercises were updated for maximum benefit per time invested, with additional alternatives when time and energy allow.
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:strength-training subtopic:time-constrained sport:cycling
src: Prologue | p. xiii
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C08-003] Step 1: Clear Goal and workout purpose

- A well-defined goal describes the outcome sought, the reason you train. A vague goal makes purposeful training pointless. Goal criteria and the season's goals come in Chapter 5, which also introduces a third goal level between seasonal goal and workout.
- Subgoals lead to the seasonal goal. At the most granular level every workout has a goal, which Friel calls a "purpose" to avoid confusion with the other goal levels.
- Example purposes: "Do 2 intervals of 20 minutes each at the power sweet spot for muscular endurance with a 5-minute recovery between them"; "Complete a long ride of 3 hours, including 2 hours of steady aerobic endurance"; "Ride easy in zone 1 for 1 hour for recovery"; "Ride with friends just to have fun". A purpose need not be hard-core.
- The reason for a workout purpose is to avoid haphazard training, common among self-coached riders. Heading out with no plan accomplishes little and leaves you unprepared. A steady diet of purposeless training produces mediocre performance.
- Key question before every session: What is the purpose of this workout? Don't turn a pedal until you can answer it.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:workout-purpose subtopic:goal-setting sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 | pp. 20-21

### [CTB-C08-004] Step 2: Expert Instruction

- Goal performance is the accumulation of daily purposes achieved over many weeks. Training purposes should form a progression from the start-of-season state to the goal.
- Planning this progression is complex (science of training in Chapter 3), so engage an expert to say which workouts to do and when. The expert can be a coach, a trusted mentor, a training partner who can design a plan, a purchased online plan, or yourself.
- Generic plans are designed for a large category of athletes with similar characteristics and outcomes. A purchased plan can be your expert only if those match yours.
- Self-coaching is possible, especially for a student of training, but the learning curve is steep and often gives a shallow performance progression because of poor decisions and setbacks. Many riders lack the time or inclination to study sports science.
- Without expert instruction the chance of reaching the seasonal goal is greatly diminished. The expert should understand your goal and give specific guidelines. Daily guidance covers questions such as how long intervals should be, how to vary intensities within a workout to develop the energy systems, how to move to improve skills, and when strength workouts should be scheduled relative to on-bike sessions.
- Newcomers improve rapidly with almost anything. Experienced riders preparing for high-performance racing need more than suffering on group rides.
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:coaching subtopic:self-coaching sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 | pp. 21-22

### [CTB-C08-005] Dose and density progression and individual differences

- Dose and density are individual (principle of individuality) and depend on seasonal fitness.
- Start of season after weeks of recovery: both dose and density low to allow beneficial adaptation. They increase through the year so the athlete is race ready by the first high-priority race. Progression must not be overly aggressive or breakdown is likely.
- Moving from novice to intermediate to advanced, both typically increase.
- Race-specific dose: all advanced athletes must periodically do high-dose workouts specific to the goal race. Criterium riders: short-duration workouts with very brief, high-intensity intervals in the last weeks before the race. Multi-hour road races: longer workouts, somewhat lower-intensity but longer intervals. Within these parameters dose is similar regardless of athlete.
- Density varies considerably between athletes training for the same events. Younger, fitter riders train with high density (closely spaced hard workouts). At the extremes of aging (juniors and aging seniors) density is lower, with more easy low-dose workouts between high-dose sessions.
tags: type:methodology level:advanced level:masters sport:cycling subtopic:dose subtopic:density phase:build
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | p. 33; summary p. 38

### [CTB-C08-006] Intensity distribution: polarized training (Figure 4.2)

- Over a season, total time at or below AeT should be around 70 to 80 percent; the remaining 20 to 30 percent above AeT, with a sizable chunk above AnT.
- Figure 4.2 (% of training time by RPE): LOW (below AeT, RPE about 1 to 3.5) 70–80%; MODERATE (AeT to AnT, RPE about 3.5 to 7) 15–25%; HIGH (above AnT, RPE 7 to 10) 5–15%.
- Reasoning: doing anaerobic work 4-5 days a week would cause constant fatigue, overtraining risk and unproductive sessions (HR and power low despite high RPE). Easy days keep you fresh so hard workouts are good. "The easier your easy workouts, the harder your hard workouts can be."
- Common mistake: overdoing the middle region (AeT-AnT) to raise average intensity; it compromises the high-intensity sessions that decide races, and anaerobic fitness is less developed. "The single biggest mistake in training is to make easy workouts moderately hard (between AeT and AnT), thus decreasing recovery." Lots of easy riding is needed.
- Self-coached riders typically do about half their training above AeT, mostly between the thresholds.
- Research cited by the author: 70-80% low gives greater fitness and race readiness than other distributions.
- Seasonal planning: vary the balance of low/high workouts through the season (reduces burnout/overtraining and plateaus), but the overall seasonal distribution should be polarized as in Fig 4.2. Detailed in Part IV.
tags: type:periodization sport:cycling system:aerobic system:threshold metric:rpe,volume level:advanced subtopic:polarized-training
evidence: author-experience (cites research, none named)
src: Ch 4 | pp. 46-47, 53-54

### [CTB-C08-007] Goal hierarchy: seasonal goals, limiters, training objectives, daily purposes

- Chain: dream -> seasonal goal (needs a plan) -> training objectives (fix limiters) -> daily purposes (each workout).
- Seasonal goals: limit to three. More goals make it hard to manage the components to develop, and they may conflict (e.g. crit series podium vs 40 km TT personal best in the same weeks: crits need much anaerobic fitness, TT needs extremely high aerobic fitness; the body cannot fully adapt to divergent demands). Focus on one goal at a time and separate goals sufficiently over the season.
- Goal criteria: specific, measurable, difficult but attainable, definite timeline, progress reviewed frequently.
  - Good example: "breaking 1 hour for a 40 km time trial on September 10."
  - Bad: "ride faster this year" (not measurable).
  - Too unrealistic (novice aiming to win masters national road race): a dream, not motivating, training has no purpose.
  - Too easy: no challenge, training has little purpose. Goals should be just beyond reach.
- Limiter: the something standing between you and the goal right now (if you could achieve the goal now it would be an accomplishment, not a goal). The primary purpose of training is to fix the performance limiter.
- Training objective: subgoal that defines what is needed to fix a limiter; a short-term goal accomplished during preparation for the most important races, usually achieved in workouts or tests (e.g. power for a given duration, a goal FTP). May also address lifestyle matters or mental roadblocks. Measurable is better. (Limiters detailed in Chapter 6; goals and objectives written down in Chapter 7; weekly planning in Chapter 8.)
- Daily purposes: every workout must have a purpose, not random. Purpose may be hard (improve power, develop endurance) or easy (easy ride, day off to recover). Hard workouts create potential for fitness; adaptation happens during recovery. Know what you will do each day to move a tiny step toward the objective and how you will recover. High performance requires daily action and planning.
- Everything matters if the goal is high: food, sleep, training partners, even thoughts.
tags: type:methodology level:all sport:cycling subtopic:goal-setting subtopic:limiters subtopic:training-objectives subtopic:daily-purpose
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "Seasonal Goals", "Training Objectives", "Daily Purposes" | pp. 59-62

### [CTB-C08-008] Basic abilities: role, definitions, training and timing

- Basic abilities are the foundation on which race-specific fitness is built. If weak, later advanced training is limited and potential is never reached. Focus on them early in the season (base period) and return to them after long tapers and lengthy post-race recoveries, because they fade during low-training-load periods.
- Aerobic endurance: long, somewhat easy, very steady workouts in zone 2, below RPE 5 (Figure 4.1). Contributes to training volume (high volume boosts VO2max). Effects: some fast-twitch fibers take on slow-twitch endurance characteristics; blood carries oxygen better; more capillaries; muscle cells make more energy-producing enzymes. The single most important of the six abilities: "Poor aerobic endurance is a game-stopper." Progress is tracked with the functional aerobic threshold field test (Sidebar 5.3, Appendix B) used frequently through the season.
- Muscular force: most closely related to economy; overcoming air resistance, headwind and gravity. Needs strong but not bulky primary mover muscles (mostly legs; torso stability matters). Weak muscles mean always slow however aerobically fit. Trained against resistance: weight lifting, hill climbing, riding into wind. Workouts are repetitions at very high intensity against resistance ("simple but not easy"). Early on this may be weight lifting (Chapter 12), but strength must be transferred to the road to be bike-specific.
- Speed skills: pedaling is deceptively complex (many muscles firing and relaxing in a pattern). Critical time to develop is early in the base period; long endurance or force work on the road with poor skills is a waste of time. The newer to the sport, the more critical. Skills are easy at slow cadence; they must stay economical at the high cadences of racing; sloppiness at high cadence wastes energy. Drills in Appendix B. Cornering, sprinting and climbing skills are less complex and are developed in specific-ability rides.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling phase:base system:aerobic metric:rpe subtopic:basic-abilities level:all
src: Ch 6 "Basic Abilities" | pp. 76-78
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C08-009] Advanced abilities: workouts, durations and timing

- Each advanced ability sits on a side of the triangle between two basic abilities that form its foundation (Figure 6.2): Muscular endurance = aerobic endurance + muscular force; Anaerobic endurance = aerobic endurance + speed skills; Sprint power = muscular force + speed skills.
- Muscular endurance: moderately long, moderately high effort; near anaerobic threshold HR and power; intensity around RPE 7, shorter than aerobic endurance workouts and less intense than muscular force repetitions. Trained with long (6- to 12-minute) repeats with short (90-second to 4-minute) recoveries, or long (20- to 40-minute) steady efforts in HR or power zones 3 and 4 or RPE 5 to 7. Marker of improvement: increased FTP from the field test. Most important ability for time trialing; also gets you up a long steady climb and delivers you to the brief high-intensity episodes in road races. Done a lot in late base and throughout the build period. If either foundation basic ability is poor, muscular endurance is poor.
- Anaerobic endurance: single best workout type for improving aerobic capacity fitness once basics are honed; also one of the most effective for road racing. Short (a few seconds to a handful of minutes), highly intense intervals in zone 5 or about RPE 9, with equal to somewhat shorter recoveries (details Appendix B). Use sparingly and with great caution: "very strong medicine. It's not candy." High reward means high risk; emphasize only at a few select times in the season; monitor your body during and right after sessions to balance stress and recovery.
- Sprint power: very short (less than 20-second) maximum-effort intervals with long (several-minute) recoveries. Important for criteriums and flat road courses, especially for sprinters. Product of speed skills and muscular force developed in base, paying off in the build period.
- Timing: building basics may take 8 to 12 weeks; extending basic training a few more weeks is never wrong if time allows before the priority race. Advanced ability training beyond about 8 to 12 weeks can be counterproductive, especially anaerobic endurance, whose stress far exceeds the others; risk of burnout, injury, illness.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling phase:build system:threshold system:vo2max system:anaerobic metric:rpe metric:power subtopic:advanced-abilities level:all
src: Ch 6 "Advanced Abilities" | pp. 78-81
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C08-010] Overlap of basic and advanced ability training

- Most riders should separate basic and advanced abilities with an overlap of only 2 to 3 weeks (see Part IV periodization). Highly experienced advanced riders may use a somewhat more liberal approach.
- Cautious option: insert minimal advanced-ability work during early base while basics remain primary. Keep advanced portions short with long recoveries between high-intensity repetitions. This may stimulate slight adaptations and ease the early-to-late-season transition.
- Example: after muscular force repetitions in a base-period on-bike workout, add 3 or 4 intervals of 20 seconds at anaerobic endurance intensity with 20-second recoveries.
- Such a workout is best done only by veteran riders with many years of racing, exceptional ability to monitor their bodies and good training knowledge. It is easy to do too much high intensity early and wreck the season.
tags: type:methodology type:caution sport:cycling phase:base level:advanced subtopic:overlap
src: Ch 6 | p. 79

### [CTB-C08-011] Table 7.1 The Common Periods in a Linear Periodization Model

| PERIOD | LENGTH | PURPOSE | PRIMARY ABILITY FOCUS |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Preparation | 1–4 weeks | Prepare to train | Basic abilities |
| Base | 9–12 weeks | Train to train | Basic abilities |
| Build | 6–8 weeks | Train to race | Advanced abilities |
| Peak | 1–2 weeks | Taper for race | Advanced abilities |
| Race | 1–3 weeks | Remove fatigue; Sharpen fitness | Advanced abilities |
| Transition | 1–4 weeks | Rest and recover | Basic abilities |
Periods are "mesocycles"; each is 1–4 weekly "microcycles".
tags: type:periodization type:plan level:all sport:cycling subtopic:periods
src: Ch 7 | p. 91

### [CTB-C08-012] Figure 7.1 Linear periodization for the first important race

Sequence and durations: PREP 1–4 weeks; BASE 1 3–4 weeks; BASE 2 3–4 weeks; BASE 3 3–4 weeks; BUILD 1 3–4 weeks; BUILD 2 3–4 weeks; PEAK 1–2 weeks; RACE 1–3 weeks; TRANSITION 1–4 weeks.
Emphasis: basic abilities start low in Prep, peak in size during Base 1–3, decline through Build 1–2, stay small through Peak/Race/Transition. Advanced abilities small through Prep, Base 1, Base 2, early Base 3; expand late Base 3; largest in Build 1, Build 2, Peak, Race; small in Transition.
Note: the full process applies to the FIRST important race; later races need not repeat it because fitness accumulates, though peak and transition cause slight-to-moderate loss, especially of basic abilities (longer peak/transition = more loss).
tags: type:periodization type:plan level:all sport:cycling subtopic:linear-periodization
src: Ch 7 | pp. 92-93

### [CTB-C08-013] Preparing for second and third A races (Figures 7.2 and 7.3)

- Ideal spacing between major races: 12–16 weeks. After race: short transition (1 week or a few days), then Base 3 (3–4 weeks), Build 1 (3–4), Build 2 (3–4), Peak (1–2), Race (1–3), Transition (1–4) (Fig 7.2). Figure 7.2: dashed line between Build 1 and Build 2.
- 7–11 weeks between races (Fig 7.3): Race 1 week; Transition 1 week (or a few days); Base 3 2–4 weeks (basic emphasized, some advanced work); Build 2 2–4 weeks; Peak 1 week; Race 1–3 weeks; Transition 1–4 weeks. Build 1 is dropped.
- Fewer than 7 weeks: decide on transition (2 or 3 days rest, strongly suggested whether or not you feel you need it; it is more mental than physical, prevents burnout in a few weeks). Longer taper+transition = more likely need a Base 3. If taper plus post-race break combined was about 10 days or fewer, go to build and advanced abilities. With 4 weeks or fewer remaining: mostly Build 2, then very short taper: 3–5 days peak and a shortened race period. Valid if basic fitness still strong.
- Key check: aerobic endurance via FAeT test (Sidebar 5.3, Appendix B) vs last test. If efficiency factor decreased by more than 10 percent, strongly recommended: short Base 3 with heavy aerobic threshold training (workout AE2, Appendix B). Otherwise go directly to build.
- Experienced veterans may blend aerobic endurance with advanced abilities in a hybrid period; risky, needs deep self-knowledge.
- Final decisions are made after each important race, not in preseason; reread this section then.
evidence: author-experience
tags: type:decision-rule type:periodization level:all sport:cycling subtopic:multiple-a-races subtopic:efficiency-factor
src: Ch 7 | pp. 93-95

### [CTB-C08-014] Annual Training Plan (ATP): six steps overview and formats

- Best time to build the ATP: start of training year (Nov, Dec, Jan); if mid-season, plan the remaining weeks. Switching mid-season: change gradually, adopt routines slowly.
- Formats: paper calendar, electronic calendar, TrainingPeaks ATP feature, or the paper ATP in Appendix A (also in Cyclist's Training Diary); copying for personal use is permitted.
- Do not finalize the ATP until Chapters 8 and 9 are read (Chapter 9 gives alternative models).
- Six steps: 1 Establish Season Goals; 2 Determine Training Objectives; 3 Set Annual Training Volume; 4 Prioritize Races; 5 Divide Season into Periods; 6 Estimate Weekly Volume. Then assign workouts (Chapter 8).
- ATP columns: Week, Mon. (date of each Monday), Race, Pri., Period, Hours/TSS, Details, Most important workouts (Weight lifting, Aerobic endurance, Muscular force, Speed skills, Muscular endurance, Anaerobic endurance, Sprint power, Testing).
See library: Figure 7.4 Example of an Annual Training Plan.
tags: type:methodology type:template level:all sport:cycling subtopic:annual-training-plan
src: Ch 7 | pp. 95-96, 110

### [CTB-C08-015] ATP Step 1: Establish season goals

- No more than three goals (more dilutes attention; fewer gives sharper focus).
- Write as intended seasonal outcomes, measurable: race result position, TT finishing time, team strategy goal (e.g., leading out sprinter), or selection (e.g., national team). Not supporting items like "lose weight" or "learn power meter" (those are objectives).
- Ask: what will I look back on with great accomplishment?
tags: type:methodology level:all sport:cycling subtopic:goal-setting
src: Ch 7 | pp. 96-97

### [CTB-C08-016] ATP Step 2: Determine training objectives (limiter-based subgoals)

- Training objectives are subgoals that "fix" a goal-specific limiter and give training feedback; achieved in workouts or low-priority races. If met, season goals are expected to be achievable. Up to four, only for limiters most likely to affect the key races.
- Each must include a calendar deadline. Advanced-ability objectives: no later than start of taper (typically 1–3 weeks before race). Basic-ability objectives: before starting build. If not achieved by those points, reconsider the related season goal.
- Limiter → ability emphasis:
  - Climbing: usually muscular endurance (or anaerobic endurance depending on climber type); example "climb Lee Hill in less than 20 minutes by April 23"; base focuses aerobic endurance and muscular force. If body weight is the limiter: "weigh 154 pounds by April 23."
  - Sprinting: sprint power; example "average 1000 watts for sprint power test by May 30"; base: muscular force and speed skills.
  - Anaerobic endurance (short crits, dropped in explosive accelerations lasting seconds to minutes): build focus on anaerobic endurance; base on aerobic endurance and speed skills; example "increase functional aerobic capacity power to 430 watts by August 7".
  - Time trialing: muscular endurance; example "increase functional threshold power to 280 watts by July 15"; base: muscular force and aerobic endurance; build heavily muscular endurance.
  - Base-period objectives: "increase my efficiency factor by 10 percent by March 1"; "leg press 2.5 times my body weight by the end of base 1" (Ch 12).
- Setting the number: start from current field test power (Appendix B). A 10 percent increase is probably near the upper limit: a stretch but doable. Finding the right target is "somewhat of a crapshoot"; gather power data from riders, coaches, BestBikeSplit.com, Strava.com, AnalyticCycling.com. Body size matters: big riders favored on flat TT/sprint, small riders on climbs.
evidence: author-experience
tags: type:methodology type:decision-rule level:all sport:cycling subtopic:training-objectives subtopic:limiters metric:power
src: Ch 7 | pp. 97-99

### [CTB-C08-017] ATP Step 3: Set annual training volume (hours or TSS)

- Volume includes all rides, strength training and crosstraining. Prefer TSS (Coggan) over hours because it combines intensity and time; intensity is the more critical fitness factor for the advanced athlete; saddle time is a poor predictor of race performance.
- Methods:
  1. Last season's total; if it went well, increase by up to 10 percent (especially for athletes in first five years). Beyond five years: keep hours the same but add high-intensity work (raises TSS perhaps 10 percent).
  2. Average weekly volume × 50 weeks (not 52, since at least two weeks will be missed to illness, injury or time off); if known absences, use the reduced number of weeks.
  3. Table 7.2 by federation category. TSS per hour: low-intensity week about 45, high-intensity about 55, long-term average 50; multiply planned hours by 45, 50 or 55.
- If hours are artificially low due to lifestyle, emphasize high-intensity training (TSS may rise though hours do not).
- If mid-season, plan volume for the remainder of the season.
evidence: author-experience
tags: type:dose type:methodology level:all sport:cycling subtopic:annual-volume subtopic:tss metric:volume metric:load
src: Ch 7 | p. 99-100

### [CTB-C08-018] ATP Step 4: Prioritize races and Table 7.3

Table 7.3 How to Determine Your Race Priorities
| RACE PRIORITY | MAXIMUM NUMBER IN A SEASON | RACE IMPORTANCE | SPECIAL RACE-DAY PREPARATION |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A | 3 | Most important. Your season's goals are determined by these. | Include a 1- to 2-week peak period prior to race week. |
| B | 8 | Of secondary importance. You want to do well, however. | Reduce training for 2 to 5 days prior to race day. |
| C | Unlimited | Least important. Use as race tune-ups, tests, hard workouts, social events, or for experience. | No special preparation. Treat these as workouts. |
- Procedure: record each week's Monday date in the "Mon." column; place each race in the row of its week (a race on Sat May 6 goes in the 5/1 row); two races on one weekend both go in that row; use expected dates if not announced; assign A/B/C in "Pri.".
- Season is designed around A races; max three (a stage race or two races in one week count as one). Reason: taper and post-race transition lose fitness, especially basic abilities, in exchange for race form; too many A races leaves no time to rebuild. Spread them out; more than 12 weeks between A races makes planning easy, fewer is increasingly complicated.
- Two designs: (1) up to three A races with 2–3 week taper each plus several B races (2–5 day mini-taper, small peak) = best possible performances; (2) no A races, many B races with a few days recovery before each = no true peak, lower performance but may be more enjoyable.
- Only one A race in the season (about 5–6 months of standard periodization leads to the first): option 1 (probably best) designate a second A race 12–16 weeks before the main one, with taper and short transition, to test training; option 2 repeat Base 3 as many times as needed to leave about 12 weeks before the A race, then start build ("you can never have enough base fitness").
- C races: treated as workouts (a day or two of reduced load), throwaway results, still race hard; used as tune-ups, fitness tests, hard workouts, fun, social events, experience. For novices, racing a lot (C races) is one of the fastest ways to learn; the first year is a learning experience.
- Starting the season: allow roughly 5 to 6 months after transition to be fully race ready.
evidence: author-experience
tags: type:decision-rule type:methodology level:all sport:cycling subtopic:race-priority subtopic:a-b-c-races
src: Ch 7 | pp. 100-103

### [CTB-C08-019] Table 7.4 The Mesocycles, Their Purposes, and Their Durations

| MESOCYCLES | WORKOUT TYPES, ABILITIES, AND GENERAL PURPOSES | DURATION (UNDER AGE 50 OR QUICK RECOVERY) | DURATION (OVER AGE 50 OR SLOW RECOVERY) | PLANNED REST AND RECOVERY DURATION |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Prep | Prepare to train: crosstraining, weights, functional strength, speed skills, general athleticism | 1–4 weeks | 1–4 weeks | Not needed |
| Base 1 | General training: aerobic endurance, muscular force, speed skills | 4 weeks | 3 weeks | Last 3–5 days of base 1 |
| Base 2 | General training: aerobic endurance, muscular force, speed skills, limited muscular endurance | 4 weeks | 3 weeks | Last 3–5 days of base 2 |
| Base 3 | General training: aerobic endurance, muscular force, speed skills, limited muscular endurance, and anaerobic endurance | 4 weeks | 3 weeks (repeat base 3) | Last 3–5 days of base 3 |
| Build 1 | Specific training: muscular endurance, anaerobic endurance, sprint power; maintain aerobic endurance, muscular force, speed skills | 4 weeks | 3 weeks | Last 3–5 days of build 1 |
| Build 2 | Specific training: muscular endurance, anaerobic endurance, sprint power; maintain aerobic endurance, muscular force, speed skills | 4 weeks | 3 weeks (repeat build 2) | Last 3–5 days of build 2 |
| Peak | Specific training: simulate a portion of the race every 72–96 hours; otherwise, recovery workouts | 1–2 weeks | 1–2 weeks | Not needed |
| Race | Specific training: short intervals at race intensity or greater and decreasing in number daily; rest | 1 week | 1 week | Not needed |
| Transition | Rest and active recovery | 2 days to 4 weeks | 2 days to 4 weeks | Not needed |
tags: type:periodization type:zones level:all level:masters sport:cycling subtopic:mesocycles
src: Ch 7 | p. 103

### [CTB-C08-020] ATP Step 5: Periodizing the season (working backward from the first A race)

1. In the "Period" column, write "race" in the first A race's week; go up and write "peak" for two rows: a 3-week taper (2 peak + 1 race). Some athletes do better with 2-week taper (some research supports 2 weeks for competitive cyclists); decide by experience; if in doubt use 2 peak + 1 race.
2. Above peak: "build 2" for 3 or 4 weeks; then likewise build 1, base 3, base 2, base 1 (3 weeks if experienced and confident of recovery, otherwise 4).
3. Above base 1: "prep" 1–4 weeks. May be omitted when few weeks remain before the first A race, or extended beyond 4 if A race is far off. Prep is laid-back, easy workouts except weights and strength. Prep may be shortened and Base 3 lengthened later (repeat) if timing allows.
4. Below first race week: label consecutive "race" rows for A races in following 1–2 weeks (back-to-back A races are common), then "tran". Early-season transition: 2–3 day break (could be 7 depending on lingering fatigue/soreness). After last A race of season: about 4 weeks (up to 6 after an exceptionally stressful year). In transition exercise is OK but training is forbidden: keep low intensity and brief; crosstraining a good option. Purpose: rest body and mind.
5. Subsequent A races: no prep, probably no base 1 or 2. Return to Base 3 if basic abilities (especially aerobic endurance) declined; maybe twice if enough weeks. If basic abilities still strong, start with Build 1 or Build 2 depending on weeks between races. Caution: do not shortchange basic abilities to do more high-intensity work; it will not make you stronger if basics are compromised. If 10 weeks or more between races, include Base 3 as starting point after first transition.
6. B races: include a few days of reduced training; adjust the plan later as needed.
tags: type:methodology type:periodization level:all sport:cycling subtopic:annual-training-plan subtopic:taper subtopic:transition
src: Ch 7 | pp. 105-107

### [CTB-C08-021] Cautions on base-period variations (small amounts of intensity)

- Advanced athletes may do a small amount of muscular endurance, anaerobic endurance and sprint power work through most of base to slightly boost specific fitness before build. Key phrase: "small amount."
- Keep such sessions few and brief; do not get carried away with high-intensity in base (counterproductive). Save longer, very difficult sessions for build, peak and race. Intense work is never done in base in traditional linear periodization; this is a suggested variation, sprinkled lightly.
evidence: author-experience
tags: type:caution type:methodology level:advanced sport:cycling phase:base subtopic:intensity-in-base
src: Ch 7 | pp. 103-104

### [CTB-C08-022] ATP Step 6: Estimate weekly volume (use of Tables 7.5 and 7.6)

- Find annual hours (Table 7.5) or annual TSS (Table 7.6) in the top row, scan down to read weekly volume for each week of each mesocycle; record in the Hours/TSS column of the ATP. TSS-based is highly recommended.
- Pattern in tables: Base weeks 1–3 build, week 4 is recovery week (equals Prep/Race level); Build 1 weeks 1–3 equal; Build 2 weeks 1–3 equal and higher; Peak week 1 reduced, week 2 lower; Race at lowest level.
- Weekly volume is not rigid: only a "ballpark guide, albeit a small ballpark"; adjust to situation each week or day; be close to it most of the time. Bad weather, missed workouts, or extra free time are normal reasons to vary. They are recommendations, not edicts.
- What counts toward weekly volume: saddle time, strength training, and other aerobic crosstraining. Does not count: stretching, passive recovery techniques, yoga, meditation. Games (basketball, tennis, football) are the athlete's call; author suggests limiting crosstraining to aerobic sports: running, swimming, off-road riding, snowshoeing, cross-country skiing, similar endurance activities.
tags: type:dose type:methodology level:all sport:cycling subtopic:weekly-volume metric:volume metric:load
src: Ch 7 | pp. 107-110

### [CTB-C08-023] ATP workout columns and how to fill them

- Remaining ATP step after volume, goals, periods: workouts, recorded on the far right: Weight Lifting column, six ability columns (Aerobic Endurance AE, Muscular Force MF, Speed Skills SS, Muscular Endurance ME, Anaerobic Endurance AnE, Sprint Power SP) and Testing (T). Mark an X for each ability workout type done that week.
- Decisions depend on the athlete's limiters and strengths (Ch 6): purpose of workouts is to improve limiters while maintaining strengths.
- Details column: brief notes such as tests in rest and recovery weeks, race comments, vacations, travel, expected missed training.
- Digital planning is quicker than paper.
- Complete the plan only through the first A race; plan later A races afterward. First-time readers should skim and wait until reading Ch 9 before deciding.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling level:all subtopic:annual-training-plan
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 115-119

### [CTB-C08-024] Weight Lifting column: strength phases by period

Two periodizations run together (weights and bike) and must not conflict; too much strength emphasis during heavy bike training hurts both. Stagger them.
- Prep: "AA" (anatomical adaptation: master movements with low loads, high reps). Most riders need at least 2 weeks of AA, 2 or 3 sessions/week (4 to 9 AA workouts [Likely typo in source — 2 weeks at 2 to 3 sessions per week equals 4 to 6 workouts; recorded as printed: 4 to 9]); no more than 2 weeks unless new to lifting. Remaining prep weeks: "MT" (muscular transition: loads gradually up, reps down), at least 2 and up to 3 weeks (4 to 6 workouts). If fewer than 2 weeks remain in prep, put MT in the first part of base 1 to complete a minimum of 2 weeks.
- Base 1: 4 consecutive weeks of "MS" (muscular strength; most challenging strength workouts). If fewer than 4 weeks remain in base 1, roll the rest into base 2. MS must be completed before the hard bike training of base 3 (critical; often violated: concurrent demanding gym and bike work gives excessive fatigue and poor training in both). With back-to-back base 3 periods, devote no more than 4 back-to-back weeks to MS; afterwards weights go to maintenance.
- Base 2, base 3, build 1, build 2, peak: "SM" (strength maintenance), 1 lift/week, much less taxing. Avoid days with both strength and high bike workload. In peak, SM has very low priority and the single weekly session must be brief enough to allow a hard bike workout the same day.
- Race period (1 week): leave blank. If 2-3 A races in consecutive weeks, pencil SM for the second week only.
- Transition: no lifting (should not). If transition is 1 or more weeks, blank. If only 1-2 days (common during the season), may return to MT, MS or SM to match the following bike period.
- Subsequent A races: may include a few MS sessions by returning to base (even base 3) if time allows and muscular endurance is the limiter; otherwise SM or blank. If A races are only a few weeks apart, weights is what gives. After a few weeks of SM, strength slowly erodes. Decision can wait until after the first A race.
- "When in doubt, leave it out." In build, peak and race put greater emphasis on bike workouts, not weights.
tags: type:periodization sport:cycling phase:anatomical-adaptation phase:strength phase:maintenance level:advanced subtopic:weight-lifting
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 117-121

### [CTB-C08-025] Ability workouts by period (linear periodization)

Linear = workouts progress in a straight line from general basic abilities to specific advanced abilities.
Basic abilities: AE, MF, SS. Advanced: ME, AnE, SP.
- Prep: only serious training is weights (replaces MF). If not lifting, X in MF every prep week and use another neuromuscular workout form (Ch 12). X in AE and SS every prep week (not hard sessions).
- Base 1: emphasis on muscular force (heavy weights low reps, 2-3x/week, or alternative MF); AE and SS stay quite easy to balance stress. X in AE and SS each week; X in MF every week only if doing MF instead of lifting. Leave the last week unmarked (rest and recovery).
- Base 2: weights/MF cut to maintenance, 1 session/week; more energy for SS and especially AE; early ME introduced (not very difficult). X in AE, MF, SS, ME every week except the last. Do not mark MF if still doing MS in the gym (e.g., short prep rolled lifting into base 1/2). MF bike workouts convert gym strength into bike-specific strength and evolve into power.
- Base 3: transitional; workouts start becoming race-specific (before this, workouts similar for all race types except duration; long road/stage races need longer workouts than crits and TTs). Mark as base 2 plus AnE (newly introduced). Last week unmarked.
- Build 1 and 2: workouts become increasingly specific to the first A race with emphasis on intensity. Basic abilities become maintenance-only (less frequent, shorter), merged into warm-ups, cooldowns or recovery rides. Multiple abilities are combined in single sessions, which keeps total time manageable (lower than base) and is more race-like. Mark the ability columns for the race type every week except the last, and X AE, SS, MF for both periods except last weeks.
  - Road race / criterium: AnE and SP at the heart, ME also big; group rides critical.
  - Stage race: same plus recovering between stages.
  - Time trial: moderately high-intensity steady pacing: great emphasis on ME, with some AnE to boost aerobic capacity.
- Peak (2-3 weeks before race): race-specific details (terrain, weather, strategies, tactics); workout duration decreases while intensity emphasis rises (Ch 13). Eliminate basic ability emphasis; you are either working very hard or recovering. Mark same columns as build but NOT MF. SS may be part of advanced workouts; AE sessions are the easy days between high-intensity sessions.
- Race week: emphasis on rest; brief workouts focused on anticipated race intensities; mark as peak.
- Transition: break from race preparation (days to a few weeks); no key workouts, structured training or volume; leave blank. Any exercise must be spur-of-the-moment, short and easy; crosstraining is OK, or take time off the bike.
tags: type:periodization sport:cycling phase:prep phase:base phase:build phase:peak phase:race phase:transition level:advanced subtopic:linear-periodization
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 121-125

### [CTB-C08-026] Rest and recovery weeks and testing placement in the ATP

- Last week of each base 1, base 2, base 3, build 1, build 2: X in AE and SS and X in Testing.
- R&R break typically 3 to 5 days; suggested workouts: AE (especially AE1) and Speed Skills sections of Appendix B.
- Field tests immediately follow the break to gauge progress and adjust zones before the next mesocycle. With a 3-4 day break + test day, 2-3 days remain in the week; with 5 days + test, 1 day remains. Assuming quick recovery from the test, start serious training with the abilities of the next mesocycle. Decide only after recovered, based on days needed (Ch 11).
- Testing: best time is after R&R, every third or fourth week of base and build; fresh after 3 to 5 days of reduced load. Power zones likely change every few weeks; heart rate zones stay fairly constant. Two tests in one day (e.g., FTP and functional aerobic capacity) are possible depending on capacity; otherwise keep at least 2 days of the R&R week for testing. Probably need a recovery day after a test day.
tags: type:methodology type:test sport:cycling metric:power metric:hr level:all subtopic:field-testing subtopic:rest-and-recovery-week
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 125-127

### [CTB-C08-027] Weekly routine principles and Table 8.1 planning template

- Lifestyle (family, career) cannot be compromised for training; weather may force indoor trainer work (usually shorter durations/volume).
- A set routine is beneficial physically and mentally; repeat the same week for several weeks if commitments unchanged. Change only at new mesocycle if needed (e.g., base to build shifts duration to intensity). Otherwise keep weeks alike.
- Perfect application is impossible; make adjustments on the fly.
- Table 8.1 template: rows Monday-Sunday; columns BASE 1, BASE 2, BASE 3, BUILD 1, BUILD 2. Pencil in group rides, races, breakthrough workouts, recovery rides, days off, weight lifting. Base 3 separated because transitional. Weekly routine excludes R&R weeks (Ch 11) and peak/race weeks (Ch 13).
- Suggested weekly routines (Tables 8.2-8.4) are examples, not gospel.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling level:all subtopic:weekly-routine subtopic:lifestyle
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 127-128, 134

### [CTB-C08-028] Group rides and C races in the weekly routine

- Build: fast group rides matter for road, stage, crit A events (similar to event; specificity). Often C races, treated as hard training sessions, not raced: no taper, day before usually easy only (B races get reduced training a few days before).
- Two group rides/week are adequate for the most serious; may be too much for slow recoverers. Avoid back-to-back fast group rides on weekends unless preparing for a stage race. One or two leaves energy for one or two more hard solo/partner sessions. Do not let group rides make up nearly all hard workouts (leaves advanced fitness underdeveloped).
- Base 1 and 2: avoid fast group rides/racing; if any, treat as aerobic endurance, not anaerobic or race-like. Too much race-like early training causes fatigue, too little basic ability work, and an early peak ("Christmas stars": peak by midwinter, burnt out late). Resist being drawn into hard-charging groups.
- Base 3: fast group ride first introduced, subdued, only 1 per week.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling phase:base phase:build level:advanced subtopic:group-rides subtopic:christmas-stars
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 128-129

### [CTB-C08-029] Scheduling weight lifting days in the week

- Do not mix the most challenging gym workouts with very challenging bike workouts in the same week or on back-to-back days: heavy MS lifting OR hard bike rides. Gym stress peaks in base 1 when bike work is low key.
- Lift about 2 times/week in prep and base 1 (exception: riders whose major limiter is muscular force). If MS extends into base 2 (common without prep), bike work stays low key.
- After MS (usually by base 2): SM, only 1 session/week, not overly challenging, any convenient day; bike workouts ramp up (base 2, certainly base 3). Group rides may start but keep easy in base 2.
- During AA, MT and MS, separate the 2 weekly sessions by 2 or 3 days: Mon/Thu, Mon/Fri, Tue/Fri, Tue/Sat, Wed/Sun work; Mon/Sat does not (only 1 day recovery from previous Saturday). Come to MS sessions rested.
- Some riders carry fatigue after SM and must keep the next day's bike session aerobic; most with well-developed muscular force can do any workout.
- Return to base 3 with MS after first race (Fig 8.1 weeks 28-30): monitor closely to avoid overreaching; do only 1 MS session that week or greatly reduce advanced bike work. 1 MS is less effective than 2, but bike training is paramount by then. If 2 MS, likely reduce advanced bike workouts; depends on limiters.
tags: type:decision-rule type:methodology sport:cycling phase:strength phase:maintenance level:advanced subtopic:weight-lifting
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 130-131

### [CTB-C08-030] Breakthrough workouts: scheduling, rest and recovery days

- Come to BTs rested and recover a day or so afterward (exception: stage race crash training).
- BT types by period: base BTs are often basic abilities (AE stress from long duration at moderate intensity; bike MF stress from heavy loads); build BTs are advanced abilities (stress from high intensity). Exceptions: advanced riders may do limited AnE and group rides in base 3 to boost aerobic capacity; these become primary later in build.
- Fatigue not relieved within 48 hours of a BT yields poor quality at the next BT; no breakthrough. Recovery workouts are as critical as BTs; replacing a scheduled easy ride or day off with a BT is counterproductive.
- Experience raises capacity (faster recovery). Novices need fewer BTs/week; juniors and seniors need more recovery and fewer BTs (see Fast After 50). Young advanced athletes (20s, early 30s, a few years in sport) may schedule several BTs/week, 4 not uncommon; be conservative. Most common mistake: too little recovery between BTs, so little progress.
- Table 8.2 (full grid in the next entry) example for an advanced rider: base 1 has 3 BTs/week (2 are MS weights); from base 2 through build, 4 BTs/week. Recovery workouts may be a day off, speed skills workout, maintenance AE session, or short low-intensity spin.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:advanced level:masters subtopic:breakthrough-workout subtopic:recovery
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 131-134

### [CTB-C08-030a] Table 8.2 Standard training weeks in Base and Build for an advanced rider (linear periodization)

Source image was blocked from automatic transcription; this grid was read directly from the book's image and transcribed by hand.

| DAY OF THE WEEK | BASE 1 | BASE 2 | BASE 3 | BUILD 1 | BUILD 2 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Monday | Weight lifting (MS) (and optional recovery ride) | Weight lifting (SM) (and optional recovery ride) | Weight lifting (SM) (and optional recovery ride) | Weight lifting (SM) (and optional recovery ride) | Weight lifting (SM) (and optional recovery ride) |
| Tuesday | Recovery | Breakthrough | Breakthrough | Breakthrough | Breakthrough |
| Wednesday | Breakthrough | Recovery | Recovery | Recovery | Recovery |
| Thursday | Recovery | Breakthrough | Breakthrough | Breakthrough (Group ride?) | Breakthrough (Group ride?) |
| Friday | Weight lifting (MS) | Recovery | Recovery | Recovery | Recovery |
| Saturday | Group ride (easy) | Group ride | Group ride or race | Group ride or race (may swap Sat.–Sun.) | Group ride or race (may swap Sat.–Sun.) |
| Sunday | Breakthrough | Recovery | Recovery (or day off) | Recovery (or day off) | Recovery (or day off) |

Table note (verbatim): "Breakthrough workouts may be long and fully aerobic or short and highly intense, depending on the rider's strengths and limiters and capacity for workload. Recovery workouts are easy rides, days off, speed skill enhancement, or low-intensity aerobic endurance workouts intended to maintain ability gains made earlier in the season."

- MS = maximum strength, SM = strength maintenance (weight-lifting phases). It is an example week, not a prescription; the head coach's rider and limiters decide.
- The per-week breakthrough counts quoted in the previous entry (3 in base 1, 4 from base 2) come from the chapter text; they are not recomputed from this grid.

### [CTB-C08-031] Stage race preparation: crash training

- Typical amateur stage races: 3-4 days, 3-5 stages; weeklong rare. Three challenges: physical/mental readiness for back-to-back races, managing energy daily, recovering between stages (tactics beyond scope; recommends Racing Tactics for Cyclists by Thomas Prehn).
- Method ("crash training"): do BT workouts on back-to-back days simulating the race, then rest a day or more. Gradually increase number of closely spaced sessions over a few weeks in build, e.g., 2 hard workouts on consecutive days this week and next; then a couple of weeks with 3 consecutive BT days each week; then 2 weeks with 4. Replicate the stage race pattern (TT, RR, crit) in the daily BTs. Include adequate recovery between sessions and groupings.
- For weeklong stage race: after 2 or 3 consecutive BT days, go for an easy ride before another 2-3 day crash.
- Schedule 3 to 5 days of rest and recovery after a crash period regardless of stages. Most advanced riders can manage about 4 days of crashing; limit may be 3.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling phase:build level:advanced subtopic:stage-race subtopic:crash-training
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | p. 132-133

### [CTB-C08-032] Relative importance of frequency, duration, intensity; use TSS for volume

- Novice: frequency primary (lifestyle change, motivation to ride regularly).
- Intermediate (second or third year): duration primary, lengthen certain rides, especially for AE.
- Advanced (three or more years): intensity usually key to racing performance; not going hard all the time (Table 8.2 has many recovery days). Volume (frequency + duration) still important but not the primary limiter.
- Advanced riders obsess on hours/miles/km; author strongly suggests Training Stress Score (TSS) because it blends frequency, duration and intensity in one number.
- Weekly hours must be right: too many gives constant fatigue and poor BT; too few undertrained. Being undertrained is far better than overtrained.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:beginner level:intermediate level:advanced metric:load metric:volume subtopic:tss subtopic:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | p. 135

### [CTB-C08-033] Choosing a periodization model: trial and error, linear as default

- No surefire way to know which model is best for you except trial and error; what works may change over time (even between mesocycles). Training is an experiment with one subject: you.
- Linear periodization (Ch 7-8) is over 50 years old, simple, and has produced high performance at every level, but is not necessarily best for you.
- Use linear if you have never planned a season. If you have tried it and want something different, pick one alternative that fits your needs and adjust until it works, always following the training principles.
- Linear: base period emphasizes duration, then gradually shifts to intensity (build, peak, race). Progress is easy to measure.
- Common concerns with linear: (1) limits the number of A-priority races you can peak for to typically two or three; (2) the peak can be maintained only about three weeks; (3) less effective for highly experienced elite athletes than for novices and intermediates; (4) monotonous, as training stays largely the same for weeks.
- Random training (no plan) can work for novices who just need to work out frequently and are judged by finishing, and for veteran athletes who hold a plan in their heads. Most athletes are better off with a written plan, even a rough outline.
tags: type:methodology type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:model-selection subtopic:linear-periodization sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 145-147

### [CTB-C08-034] Table 9.1 Adjustment for Slow-Recovery Athlete

Slow-recovery adaptation of Table 7.4 for the classic linear plan: base and build periods reduced to 3-week mesocycles; base 3 and build 2 are repeated so total pre-race training time is not reduced. Build period grows from 8 weeks (fast recovery) to 9 weeks (slow recovery). Base period stays 12 weeks. Weekly volume for each named week is taken from Table 7.5 (hours) or 7.6 (TSS) for that exact week (e.g., base 1, week 2). Prep, peak and race period lengths unchanged.

| PERIOD | MESOCYCLE WEEKS |
| :--- | :--- |
| Base 1 | Week 1, Week 2, Week 4 |
| Base 2 | Week 1, Week 2, Week 4 |
| Base 3 | Week 1, Week 2, Week 4 |
| Base 3 (repeated) | Week 2, Week 3, Week 4 |
| Build 1 | Week 1, Week 2, Week 4 |
| Build 2 | Week 1, Week 2, Week 4 |
| Build 2 (repeated) | Week 2, Week 3, Week 4 |

(Rows are grouped here from the source table, which lists each week on its own line. Week 4 is the rest and recovery week in the Table 7.4 pattern. The source image renders the sequence as weeks 1, 2, 4 for each 3-week block; whether week 3 is skipped is as printed.)
tags: type:periodization type:plan level:all subtopic:slow-recovery sport:cycling phase:base phase:build
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | p. 149

### [CTB-C08-035] 9-day week for slow-recovery riders (Table 9.2)

- Problem: a 7-day week with 3 hard days spaces them only 48 hours apart, too tight for slow recoverers; alternative is only 2 hard days per week. Workouts too close add cumulative fatigue; too far apart compromise fitness.
- Solution: 9-day weeks with 2 recovery days after each hard (breakthrough) day. 6 hard days fit in 18 days, versus 5 in the same span with a 7-day week and 2 hard days per week.
- Example mesocycle (Table 9.2): two back-to-back 9-day weeks, then 5 days of rest and recovery; the last 2 recovery days of week 2 are rolled into the 5-day R&R break, making a 21-day mesocycle. The R&R break need not be 5 days; longer or shorter based on how you feel.

| | D1 | D2 | D3 | D4 | D5 | D6 | D7 | D8 | D9 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Week 1 | BT | R | R | BT | R | R | BT | R | R |
| Week 2 | BT | R | R | BT | R | R | BT | R&R | R&R |
| Week 3 | R&R | R&R | R&R | next 9-day week starts | | | | | |

BT = breakthrough workout, R = recovery day, R&R = extended rest and recovery period. After the last R&R day the next 9-day week begins.
- Customization: one or more of the second of the two back-to-back recovery days may become a moderate workout (e.g., aerobic threshold workout in low zone 2, or a zone 3 tempo ride; see Appendix B) if you can handle more. Adjust on the fly by how you feel. If unsure, be conservative: better slightly undertrained than slightly overtrained.
- Downside: hard with a job or daily constraints demanding a 7-day week (e.g., long rides on workdays). Best for athletes with very flexible lifestyles, e.g., retired. Described more fully in Fast After 50.
tags: type:periodization type:plan level:masters level:all subtopic:9-day-week subtopic:slow-recovery sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 149-150

### [CTB-C08-036] Time-limited periodization

Problem: lifestyle limits weekly time below what the volume tables (7.2, 7.5, 7.6; daily Tables 8.3, 8.4) prescribe, though you have the physical capacity.
Example: athlete with 600 annual hours (about 13 h/week average, big week 18 h in base 3 week 3 per Table 7.5) can only do 15 h in a week; Table 7.5 shows 7 weeks above 15 hours before her first race.
- Do NOT drop to a lower annual volume (e.g., 500 h): plan would be too easy in most weeks to increase fitness.
- Instead keep the 600-hour schedule and cap weeks at 15 hours: shorten the longest workouts in those weeks.
- General rule: as duration goes down, intensity can go up. Possibly raise intensity of some workouts in the capped weeks (e.g., zone 1 recovery ride up to zone 2). Be conservative: one additional zone in a given workout is plenty, and only for a few workouts. This is the art of self-coaching.
- Alternative: move the excess hours from a chopped week to a lower-volume week, making nearly all base weeks 15-hour weeks; do the same in build, so total training time stays the same as the unaltered plan.
- These fixes are inferior to the original volumes but better than training to a lower annual volume. Applies equally to TSS-based training (Table 7.6).
tags: type:methodology type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:time-limited subtopic:volume-cap metric:volume metric:load sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 150-151

### [CTB-C08-037] Inverse weeks periodization

- Tables 7.5/7.6 raise weekly volume over the first 3 weeks of a 4-week base mesocycle (week 4 recovery). Numbers are ballpark only; most athletes closely achieve scheduled volumes. At the right end of Table 7.5 weekly increases are around 4 to 5 hours.
- Many athletes find the rising pattern overly taxing: tired by week 3 yet face the biggest week, and workout quality declines.
- Inverted pattern: biggest week first, declining to week 3, then rest week. Author does not know if the effect is physical or psychological, but it seems to improve capacity for some.
- Example, base 3, 500-annual-hour column: original Week 1: 11.0, Week 2: 13.5, Week 3: 15.0, Week 4: 7.0 hours. Inverted: Week 1: 15.0, Week 2: 13.5, Week 3: 11.0, Week 4: 7.0 hours.
- Another option for those still bothered by cumulative volume: change from 4 weeks to 3 weeks, last several days of the third still rest and recovery.
tags: type:methodology type:periodization level:all subtopic:inverse-weeks metric:volume sport:cycling phase:base
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 151-152

### [CTB-C08-038] Reverse linear periodization

- Definition: still linear but inverted: base period = high-intensity, low-duration workouts; build period = low intensity with high volume. Popular where winters are cold and daylight limited (short intense workouts in winter, long less intense in summer).
- Problem: for advanced athletes success depends on race-like intensity, not duration. The shorter the A race, the more workouts become different from the race. Advanced athletes training for short races (e.g., criterium) should focus on higher intensity in build, not long durations.
- Appropriate only if the A race is long and relatively low intensity (at or below anaerobic threshold; moderate, between aerobic and anaerobic thresholds): century, gran fondo, long time trial, possibly multi-stage race. Not appropriate if the outcome depends on very high-intensity short efforts, as in most road races.
- How to apply: swap volumes week for week: build 1 volumes (Tables 7.5/7.6) become base 2 volumes (weeks 1-4); swap base 3 and build 2 likewise. Volume drops in base 2 and 3 and rises in build 1 and 2.
- Intensities are swapped likewise (adjust Table 8.1, especially breakthrough sessions): highest-intensity work (anaerobic endurance, sprint power; Appendix B) in base 2 and 3; moderate intensity (between aerobic and anaerobic thresholds) in build 1 and 2.
- Unchanged: prep and base 1 (volume and method); weight lifting (though isolate gym workouts from high-intensity sessions in modified base 2 if the MS strength phase spills into it); peak and race periods, except workout intensity in those weeks is about anaerobic threshold (see Ch 13).
- Violating the rule that training becomes increasingly like the race as race day nears makes it a bad choice.
- If winter forces indoor riding but the goal is an intense road race: instead use the time-limited approach (cut long rides, raise planned workout intensity one zone) or rearrange so shorter workouts fall on indoor days.
tags: type:periodization type:decision-rule level:advanced level:all subtopic:reverse-linear sport:cycling phase:base phase:build
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 152-153

### [CTB-C08-039] Undulating periodization (Tables 9.3 and 9.4)

- Nonlinear: duration and intensity are alternated every few days/weekly. Popular in power sports such as weight lifting; few endurance athletes use it, but author has seen it work. Reasons to use: cannot devote all needed time weekly for linear; need variety/motivation.
- For cyclists: alternate intensity and duration weekly regardless of period: one week base-like (high duration/volume), the next build-like (high intensity). Both are done most of the year.
- With a 4-week mesocycle (last week rest, recovery, test): early season (first ~12 weeks, normally base) 2 weeks high duration and 1 week high intensity; last ~12 weeks before the race (build) 2 weeks high intensity separated by 1 week high volume.

Table 9.3 Base period (4 weeks): Base week 1 Emphasize duration; Base week 2 Emphasize intensity; Base week 3 Emphasize duration; Base week 4 Rest, Recovery, and Test (see Chapter 11).
Table 9.4 Build period (4 weeks): Build week 1 Emphasize intensity; Build week 2 Emphasize duration; Build week 3 Emphasize intensity; Build week 4 Rest, Recovery, and Test (see Chapter 11).

- "Duration" weeks do not mean all workouts equally long; capacity determines length and number of high-duration sessions (2, 3 or 4); other workouts are recovery rides. Same for intensity weeks.
- Rule: high-duration sessions are low intensity, mostly zone 2; high-intensity sessions must be low duration. Mixing high duration and high intensity in one workout is likely to lead to injury, burnout or overtraining.
tags: type:periodization type:plan level:all subtopic:undulating sport:cycling phase:base phase:build metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 154-155

### [CTB-C08-040] Block periodization (Issurin) and Tables 9.5, 9.6

- Devised in the 1980s by Russian sports scientist Vladimir Issurin for truly elite athletes (professionals; highest-category amateurs). Reasoning: the closer to potential, the harder gains are; mixing several workout types per week spreads training stress too thin and dilutes each workout's effect. Focus on one type of workout per mesocycle (a block) and repeat. Cited study: repeating same workouts for a few weeks before changing took half as many weeks to reach the same fitness as classic linear.
- Not suggested for novices, intermediates, or those who are not podium contenders at high-level races (national/world championships): they have room to advance, dilution is no issue, and focusing on one ability would let non-maintained abilities fade badly.
- Works best with a focused, somewhat linear progression: base emphasizes basic abilities, build emphasizes advanced abilities. Progress to the next block when the dominant ability is well established. Block lengths are rough guidelines. Maintenance workouts are about half as frequent as those required to develop the dominant ability initially. Prep, peak, race, transition periods as in Ch 7.

Table 9.5 Dominant and maintenance abilities by block
| BLOCK | WEEKS | DOMINANT ABILITY (FOCUS) | MAINTENANCE ABILITY (FOCUS) |
| :--- | :---: | :--- | :--- |
| Prep | 1–4 | (Preparing to train) | Muscular force |
| Early base | 4–6 | Muscular force | Speed skills |
| Late base | 4–6 | Aerobic endurance | Muscular force |
| Early build | 4 | Muscular endurance | Aerobic endurance |
| Late build | 4 | Anaerobic endurance | Muscular endurance |
| Peak | 1 | (Race-like workouts) | (Recovery between workouts) |
| Race | 1 | (Race-intensity sessions) | (Taper volume) |
| Transition | 1–4 | (Rest and recover) | Aerobic endurance |

Table 9.6 Example base or build week (elite rider; customize individually)
| MON | TUE | WED | THU | FRI | SAT | SUN |
| Maintenance | Dominant | Recovery | Dominant | Maintenance | Recovery | Dominant |
tags: type:periodization type:plan level:elite level:advanced subtopic:block-periodization sport:cycling phase:base phase:build
evidence: study (Issurin, block vs linear, as cited by author; no reference given)
src: Ch 9 | pp. 155-157

### [CTB-C08-041] A simple plan: standard week

- Core rule: the closer to race day, the more like the race workouts must become. Novice: gradually increase workout frequency per week. Intermediate: increase workout durations. Advanced (frequency and duration already set): intensity is the primary focus.
- Method: lay out a standard week fitting your lifestyle and keep it largely the same; repeat it for 2 or 3 consecutive weeks before a few days of rest and recovery (Ch 11). Only workout details change as they become more race-like.
- Advanced rider, base (about 10 to 12 weeks): workout durations lengthen a little every few weeks, intensity low (mostly zones 1 and 2); develops aerobic endurance, muscular force, speed skills.
- Build (next ~10 weeks): increase time at race-like intensity while durations slightly decrease; develops muscular endurance, anaerobic endurance, sprint power. Include fast group rides and races. Slightly shorter duration keeps recovery from being prolonged. Perhaps only 2 or 3 race-intensity workouts weekly, depending on capacity and recovery.
- Then taper volume in the last 2 or 3 weeks before the race by shortening workouts (Ch 13).
