- Key test: are you prepared to race? If unsure, workouts are not race-like enough.
tags: type:methodology type:principle level:beginner level:intermediate level:advanced subtopic:simple-plan subtopic:standard-week sport:cycling phase:base phase:build phase:taper
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 157-158

### [CTB-C08-042] Recovery duration by age and fitness

- About 48 hours for all body systems to adapt after a very high-stress workout. Delayed onset muscle soreness appears about 24-48 h after, recedes around 48 h; muscle protein synthesis also takes about 48 h. Other systems assumed similar.
- Fastest recovery: young (20s). In 50s recovery and adaptation may take up to twice as long, and slows further. Fitter athletes need less time: novices > intermediates > advanced.
- Examples: master racer in 2nd-3rd year of the sport may need 60 hours after a race or extremely hard ride; a 25-year-old category 1 rider may need only 36 hours.
- These are broad generalizations; err on the conservative side until better tools exist.
tags: type:dose type:methodology level:masters subtopic:recovery-time metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | p. 179

### [CTB-C08-043] Planning recovery: hard-easy days and weekly pattern

- Balance hard and easy workouts weekly; use a standard weekly schedule (Ch 8). Typical: easy ride or day off the day after a highly stressful session: hard-easy-hard-easy-hard-easy, with the seventh day easy for some riders, moderate for others.
- Alternative: very hard-easy-easy-hard-easy-hard-easy (very hard day could be a group ride or C-priority race). The harder the hard workouts, the easier and longer the recovery break must be.
- Too many hard days risks overtraining; too few gives little progress.
- Slow-recovery riders may benefit from 9-day weeks (Ch 9); conflicts with 7-day work weeks, best for retired or flexible-schedule riders.
- Different stress sources need different recovery: after high-duration periods, recovery is mostly sleep, nutrition, hydration; after high-intensity, the nervous system needs more attention and alternative methods may help.
- Scheduling recovery in plans removes second-guessing ("when the schedule says recovery, you just do it"); most riders, motivated before an A-priority race, lose sensitivity to fatigue and press on, so planned recovery suits most. Recovery on demand suits those who are in touch with their bodies (and requires careful morning warnings).
tags: type:periodization type:methodology level:all subtopic:hard-easy subtopic:recovery-on-demand
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11, "Strategic Recovery" | pp. 186-187, 190

### [CTB-C08-044] Recovery breaks in base and build periods

- Plan a few days of recovery every third or fourth week in base and build.
- Quick recoverers (usually under 50): about 3 weeks of training then a few days of rest. Slow recoverers (older riders, perhaps novices): about 2 weeks then a break of a few days. Exceptions exist (quick 50-somethings, slow young). May differ by period (e.g. fine with 3 weeks aerobic base, but only ~2 weeks of anaerobic endurance work in build).
- Unsure: use ~2 weeks of hard training then a few days of rest.
- Break length: minimum 3 days of greatly reduced load; most riders fully recovered within 5 days; sometimes 6-7 days needed. With a 3-day break, the next period has 18 or 25 days of training (for 2 or 3 weeks).
- During the break: short, easy rides. When legs and attitude feel normal (may take 2-4 days), do a cautiously harder workout next day with a few short intervals appropriate to the period to check readiness.
- The first day after feeling fully recovered is the time to test fitness (FTP test, Appendix B, or a B/C race) and possibly set new zones.
- Example week before a Saturday C race: rest-rest-rest-test-rest-race-rest (C races are treated as hard workouts). For a B race (few days rest before), time the recovery break right before the race (e.g. start Wednesday instead of Monday).
tags: type:periodization phase:base phase:build level:all subtopic:recovery-week
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | pp. 188-189, 191

### [CTB-C08-045] Race week and transition period recovery

- Race week: arrive at A-priority race recovered; form = race-ready and fresh (absence of fatigue). Detailed in Ch 13.
- Transition after an A race: take a recovery break. Length varies with timing in season, length/stress of preparation and race fatigue. After the first A race a very short break; after the second a bit more; after the last A race of the year a lengthy break. Range: a couple of days to as much as 6 weeks. Author tells athletes to plan 2-6 weeks after the road season.
- First A race a multiday stage race: about a week of recovery. Last race a short criterium/TT: post-season break may be short.
- No more than three A-priority races per season (Ch 7): each taper slightly loses basic fitness, especially aerobic endurance.
- Cyclocross after road: treat as extension of a shortened season (e.g. one or two A road races then one or two A cyclocross races) or make ‘cross B/C only; otherwise the risk is overtraining syndrome, long-term burnout, and a shortened next base period. Accumulating fatigue is like a snowball.
- Post-race break during the season typically only a few days.
tags: type:periodization phase:transition phase:taper level:all subtopic:transition-period
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | pp. 189-190, 192

### [CTB-C08-046] Weight lifting phases and purposes (Table 12.2)

| WEIGHT LIFTING PHASE | PURPOSE |
| Anatomical Adaptation (AA) | Become accustomed to the various exercises |
| Muscular Transition (MT) | Gradually adapt to heavier loads |
| Muscular Strength (MS) | Build neuromuscular force |
| Strength Maintenance (SM) | Maintain the strength gains made in MS |
Order: AA, MT, MS, SM. Choose a personal program from the exercise "shopping list": include at least one hip-knee-ankle extension exercise (double-leg squat, single-leg squat, dead lift, step-up, lunge, leg press), consider a leg curl for balance, and one or more core exercises; a ballistic lift may replace a traditional extension exercise. Choose one or two extension exercises per session; may alternate through season.
tags: type:periodization sport:cycling phase:anatomical-adaptation phase:strength subtopic:weight-lifting-phases level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 | pp. 205, 220

### [CTB-C08-047] Table 12.5 Periodization of Force Reps (alternative to weight lifting)

| PERIOD | TYPE OF WORKOUT | TOTAL SESSIONS | SESSIONS PER WEEK | SETS PER SESSION | REPS PER SET (EACH LEG) | RECOVERY BETWEEN SETS (MIN) |
| Prep | Flat Force Reps | 4–6 | 2–3 | 1–2 | 3–4 | 3–4 |
| Base 1 (may continue into early base 2) | Hill Force Reps | 8–12 | 2 | 3 | 3–6 | 3–4 |
| Base 2, Base 3, Build 1, Build 2 | Hill Force Reps | Indefinite | 1 | 1 | 3–4 | — |
| Peak | Hill Force Reps | 1 or 2 | 1 | 1 | 2–3 | — |
| Race | None | None | — | — | — | — |
Function: functional (on-bike) force training periodized like lifting: flat then hill, early season, then greatly reduced version for the rest of the season.
tags: type:periodization sport:cycling phase:prep phase:base phase:peak metric:power subtopic:force-reps level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 Table 12.5 | p. 224

### [CTB-C08-048] Taper definition and core principle: cut duration, keep intensity

- The pre-race taper spans the peak and race periods, the last 2 to 3 weeks before the A-priority race. Training load is reduced by cutting workout duration to gain freshness and come into form.
- Decrease duration, NOT intensity. Intensity must remain high.
- In a nutshell: a reduction in workout duration, resulting in a higher percentage of training load as intensity.
- The two critical components in any taper are volume and intensity. To reduce volume, either do fewer workouts per week or make workouts shorter. Other than possibly one day off per week during the taper, Friel recommends shorter rides.
- Doing the opposite (more volume, less intensity) is highly likely to produce a poor race: you lose race-like fitness and race with tired legs.
- The most common mistake is not becoming fresh enough, usually from too much volume in an attempt to maintain or increase fitness. For the advanced rider the key is intensity, not hours (Ch 4). If aerobic fitness is not good by now, it will not be by race day.
- Trust the plan and the reduced volume; lengthening easy days to keep fitness gives up form ("Big mistake").
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling phase:taper level:advanced subtopic:taper-principles metric:volume metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 13 "Components of Tapering", Summary | pp. 230-232, 235

### [CTB-C08-049] Taper components: race-like intensity, frequent recovery, specificity

- High-intensity taper workouts are often intervals, whatever the race type or duration. They must simulate the strategic intensities expected in the race:
  - Decisive hill: hill intervals with duration and volume closely related to, or greater than, race day (or a fast group ride with a similar hill).
  - Sprinting decides: include sprints.
  - Time trial: rehearse the greatest intensity you expect or can sustain.
  - Mimic the moments with the greatest impact on performance.
- Frequent recovery: keep intensity and duration low for the two days before each key workout, so that every third day you are eager to go fast (a hard ride every third day, two easy days between).
- Hard-day options, best first: races shorter than the goal event with similar or greater intensity; fast group rides (road race or criterium); interval workouts with another rider; solo intervals.
- Choose workouts or races that challenge the key determiner of the A-race outcome (climbing, sprinting, time trialing). If you seldom race, a race of any type helps (mental, nutritional, logistical, environmental preparation).
- Successful hard sessions build confidence; being rested beforehand makes good race simulations more likely.
- Do all hard rides on the bike you will use on race day. For stage races with road and TT stages, use both bikes with matching workouts.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling phase:taper level:advanced subtopic:race-simulation subtopic:recovery-days metric:power
src: Ch 13 "Components of Tapering" | pp. 231-232

### [CTB-C08-050] Peak period (taper): length and purpose

- The peak period typically lasts 1 to 2 weeks and ends several days before the race, when the race period begins.
- 2 weeks: before late-season races after a long, stressful build, and before very long races including stage races.
- 3 to 5 days: highly fit, experienced, fast-recovering riders; early-season races; very short A races such as a criterium of 1 hour or less.
- Purposes: (1) shed accumulated fatigue by adding easy recovery days; (2) keep fitness high with high-intensity training. Together they give race readiness and freshness.
- Key is a race-like workout simulating part of the race, followed by two short low-intensity days. The pattern is always race-like intensity followed by recovery. Recovery rides stay short while the hard workouts gradually shorten.
tags: type:periodization sport:cycling phase:peak level:advanced subtopic:peak-period
src: Ch 13 "The Peak Period" | pp. 232-233

### [CTB-C08-051] Taper summary: weekly structure

- Until the race period starts: a high-intensity ride every third day with two easy recovery days between.
- Race period: generally the last six or seven days; short race-intensity intervals several times on most days.
- What you do in the final two or three weeks can affect performance more than the preceding two or three months.
tags: type:periodization sport:cycling phase:taper level:advanced
src: Ch 13 Summary | p. 235

### [CTB-C08-052] Planning with the diary: ATP and weekly workout planning

- Keep the annual training plan (ATP) in the diary (paper: insert the plan; electronic: use its plan format). Review and update at least weekly, sometimes more often.
- Start-of-season steps in the diary: (1) note goals and training objectives (Ch 5); (2) lay out the ATP (example in Appendix A); (3) note the periodization method (Ch 7, 9); (4) plug A-priority races into calendar weeks (Ch 7); (5) schedule periods for the first race (Tables 7.1, 7.4); (6) note volume for every week leading to the first race (Tables 7.2, 7.5, 7.6); (7) design a weekly routine that fits your schedule (Ch 8).
- This takes deep thinking the first time but saves time all season; weekly workout decisions are then nearly complete using Appendix B. The ATP may take about an hour or so per year to build.
- Weekly planning: at the end of the current week (most likely Sunday) open the next week and write each day's workouts using Appendix B workout codes. For weight lifting, indicate the day(s) and type (AA, MT, MS or SM; Ch 12). Add planned durations or TSS per session. Takes only a few minutes; gives a head start, more productive workouts and more confidence.
- Plans will change; modification is expected. Confidence comes from the act of planning, not the plan itself.
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:annual-training-plan subtopic:weekly-planning
src: Ch 14 "Planning with a Diary" | pp. 239-244

### [CTB-C08-053] Appendix B: general workout structure and intensity gauges

- Workouts are categorized into the six abilities (Chapter 6). Field tests are included in the appendix.
- A workout is generally preceded by a warm-up and followed by a cooldown. Between them is the main set, the primary focus of the session. The more intense the main set, the longer the warm-up.
- Intensities are given in power and heart rate. Heart rate zones: Table 4.2. Power zones: Table 4.3. Power instructions are based on FTP (Chapter 4, "Functional Threshold").
- Heart rate and power zones don't always agree (Chapter 4, "Zone Agreement"). With both devices, the power meter measures performance and the heart rate monitor indicates effort. The power meter is the preferred intensity gauge for most workouts, with exceptions noted per workout.
- The list is not all-inclusive. Workouts can be varied and combined (multi-ability workouts) to match the A-priority race demands. Merging multiple abilities into one workout is most common in the build period (see Chapter 8).
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling level:all subtopic:workout-structure metric:power,hr
src: Appendix B intro | p. 259
evidence: author-experience

## C09 Decision Rules & Heuristics

### [CTB-C09-001] Building self-confidence: Saving successes

Principle: you must believe to achieve; setbacks are minor roadblocks that all successful athletes experience; stay confident, patient and mentally tough. Self-confidence is the quiet voice saying "I can do this"; a negative authoritarian voice ("You can't do it!") is often heard, especially on race day. People lose confidence through early failures magnified by negative people. Do both techniques daily, without exception.
Saving successes:
- Every night after lights out, review the day's training and find one thing done well (one hill, one interval, finishing a hard workout, or best workout of the season). Relive it repeatedly until falling asleep = a deposit in the success savings account. Account must grow daily.
- Withdraw (relive vividly one memory, ideally a big success: "Remember that time when I . . .") when the negative voice speaks, when anxiety arises in race week, when someone expresses doubt, and at the starting line.
- Never deposit bad experiences or unwelcome moments; let them go, do not relive them. Deposit and withdraw only positive ones.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:self-confidence subtopic:saving-successes
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Believe to Achieve" | pp. 6-7

### [CTB-C09-002] Fake it 'til you make it (act as if)

Always assume the posture and disposition of a confident athlete even if you do not feel it. Study confident athletes at races/group workouts: they stand tall and proud, head up, look people in the eyes, do not denigrate others to raise their own self-esteem, move fluidly, look calm not anxious. Posture and demeanor breed confidence; slumping posture and defeated demeanor are incompatible with confidence ("like saying no while shaking your head yes"). Use it on race day and whenever confidence wanes.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:self-confidence subtopic:act-as-if
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Believe to Achieve" | pp. 7-8

### [CTB-C09-003] Coach's screening of an athlete's mental makeup

Friel says all athletes are limited more by mind than body; when interviewing a prospective athlete he is mostly concerned with mindset. Checklist, in order:
1. Motivation (must come from within; coach cannot create it).
2. A dream that repeatedly shows up (not vague wishes), held for some time.
3. Willingness to commit; judged via past challenges in any life area: how hard, how long, did they give up or keep going (tenacity).
4. Lifestyle: random or structured; consistent training or many missed workouts. Good athletes usually lead "boring" lives doing the same things daily; consistency implies ability to follow a long-term plan. Chaotic lifestyle decreases odds but does not preclude success.
5. Family and friends supportive? Unsupportive or indifferent surroundings have made athletes fail.
6. Then build the professional team (he can provide it) only after the above meets his standards.
7. Then shape and define the dream; the physical side begins.
Mental fitness can be developed if the proper attitude is present.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:athlete-assessment subtopic:mental-fitness
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Summary: Mental Performance" | pp. 13-14

### [CTB-C09-004] Gap between required and current physical profile

Ask: is my goal climber, sprinter, time trialist or all-rounder, and is my physical makeup compatible with it? If not, what must I focus on?
- Training (and perhaps lifestyle) is defined by the size of the gap between what the goal requires and where you are physically now.
- A small gap means high potential for success.
- A large gap requires extraordinary motivation, commitment, tenacity and training consistency.
Performance can be predicted from measurable metrics (e.g., body mass, power relative to duration), but training to race has many more variables. The phenotype discussion is a generalization and a rough starting point; the individual training philosophy also strongly affects success.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:goal-feasibility subtopic:phenotypes sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 | pp. 18-19

### [CTB-C09-005] Step 3: Specific Practice - rules for executing workouts

- Everything done in a workout must be specific to its purpose and the expert's details. Stay focused on doing it as planned.
- Making a session easier: allowed on the fly when you find you aren't ready (e.g., need more recovery or the timing is wrong). Friel tells athletes they may make a workout easier and tell him later.
- Making a session harder: first consult the expert who designed it. There may be a reason for its low difficulty.
- Complex workouts: write them on a scrap of paper taped to the handlebar stem, or upload to the handlebar power device, and check from time to time.
- Other athletes are the greatest impairment to purposeful practice. Talk about the session's purpose before heading out. If a partner won't follow the plan, train alone, because compromising the purpose to ride together violates purposeful training.
- When meeting other riders on the road mid-workout, the tendency is to turn it into a gradually accelerating race. When you sense this, turn and get away from them and do the workout as planned.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:workout-execution subtopic:group-rides sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 | p. 22

### [CTB-C09-006] Example: headwind time trial, adjust power to duration not distance

Trained for a 40 km time trial expected to take about 55 minutes at a certain power. Race day: strong headwind on point-to-point course, riders of similar ability finishing about 10 minutes slower, so expect about 65 minutes. Rule: since duration (not distance) determines race intensity, reduce planned power. If not, you will likely blow up in the last few minutes and limp to the finish.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:pacing subtopic:time-trial
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 30-31

### [CTB-C09-007] Do not use age-based formulas for max heart rate or HR zones

- Avoid internet formulas based on age/gender (e.g., 220 minus age) and taking a percentage as AnT; "steer clear of all of them because they are unlikely to work."
- Reason: individuality. Measured max HRs form a bell-shaped curve; the formula is accurate near the middle but increasingly wrong at the sides, and you do not know where you are. Zones may be off by as much as 20 bpm high or low (a 40 bpm range) — no better than guessing.
- Instead: do a field test to estimate FTHR.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling metric:hr level:all subtopic:max-hr-formulas
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 48-49

### [CTB-C09-008] Zone agreement: HR zones and power zones will not always match

- Riding in HR zone 2 with power in zone 3 is common, expected, and a good thing; not a sign of faulty zones or heart.
- Advanced athletes: HR zones change very little, if at all, over the season (FTHR is stable). Small differences between tests are likely diet, emotional stress, fatigue or excitement rather than fitness. Novices are likely to see HR zones change as they develop basic fitness.
- Power zones can change significantly: FTP rises with fitness and zones rise; FTP and zones fall when fitness falls. Heart rate is a proxy for how easy/hard the ride feels; power relates to how fast you go.
- Overlap pattern: early in the season HR and power zones may overlap considerably; as fitness improves they separate (e.g., HR zone 2 later yields zone 3 power) = going faster at the same effort.
- If power zones never changed, you would never get faster at given heart rates; riding at higher power for a given HR is a key to measuring aerobic fitness improvement. Quote (LeMond): "It never gets easier, you just go faster."
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:hr,power level:advanced subtopic:zone-agreement
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 51-52, 54

### [CTB-C09-009] Start-of-season assessment: three parts, within four weeks

- Key question: "Physically and mentally, where am I now?" Three parts: physical soundness for training, mental readiness to go after a high goal, current cycling fitness.
- Complete the whole process within the first four weeks of returning to training.
- Temper early enthusiasm: aggressive early training produces "Christmas stars" who burn out before the race season.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all sport:cycling phase:prep subtopic:assessment
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "Assessment" | p. 62

### [CTB-C09-010] Identifying and addressing limiters

- Every athlete has limiters, even pros; they are more obvious in newer riders and subtle in advanced riders.
- Common weakness categories: climbing, time trialing, sprinting (Figure 2.1). Nobody is great at all three; weakness in two is common.
- Rule: compare your weaknesses with the demands of the most important races. A weakness matching a demand is a limiter (hilly course + weak climbing; sprint-finish criterium + weak sprint).
- You are unlikely to become as good at limiters as at strengths, but you can close the gap with serious training. Also use tactics (e.g., start a climb at the front and drift back so you are still in the group at the top; choose whose wheel to sit on before a sprint). Time trials offer little tactical cover: "the race of truth."
- Key questions when planning: What is your highest priority race? What is your limiter for it? Training focuses on that limiter, but maintain the strength (maintenance is easier than remedial training); the greater the limiter, the more the strength must stay strong to counterbalance it. Select races that emphasize your strengths where possible.
- Limiter-to-base link: base-period emphasis should be the basic abilities related to the limiter. E.g., anaerobic endurance limiter: base on aerobic endurance and speed skills; muscular endurance limiter: base on aerobic endurance and muscular force. All three advanced abilities must still be developed; distribute limited time and energy.
- Climbing also needs a high power-to-weight ratio: limiter may be power or weight. Increase power while shedding unnecessary weight. Track FACP and FTP against body weight over time as a climbing indicator (FACP relates to anaerobic endurance; FTP to muscular endurance; tests in Appendix B).
- Multiple demands: if two key races have different limiters (hilly road race with climbing weakness, then flat criterium with sprint limiter), consider time between races and size of each limiter (how much improvement, how much time). Decide which race and limiter matter more; focus on what you can accomplish rather than what you would like.
- Have only two or three critical races a season (Chapter 7). Determining limiters is the most critical early-season decision.
- Novices (and some intermediates): basic abilities are the typical limiters; focus on them; establishing them may take one to three years of basic training; little need for advanced abilities until then. Experienced athletes: common limiters are the advanced abilities, but they should still re-establish the basics each season before advanced work; basics erode after a taper or training break, so return to basic training to rebuild.
- Non-ability limiters (lifestyle): training inconsistency, limited training time, lack of confidence, limited support from family/friends, poor nutrition, insufficient equipment, inadequate training environment, propensity to overtrain, frequent illness or injury, inappropriate body composition, insufficient sleep, unusual psychological stress, physically demanding job, and others.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling subtopic:limiters level:all
src: Ch 6 "Ability Limiters" | pp. 81-84
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C09-011] Rest-and-recovery weeks within mesocycles: on-demand 3–5 days

- The last 3–5 days of base and build mesocycles are reduced training. Slow recoverers may need most of a week; quick ones 3 or 4 days. Duration may vary between mesocycles with stress. Treat as an on-demand decision: if still tired after 4 days, take another day or more. Recovery is important; do not neglect it (Chapter 11).
- Ideal place for B races is at the end of rest and recovery weeks, though not always possible.
tags: type:decision-rule type:recovery level:all sport:cycling subtopic:recovery-on-demand
src: Ch 7 | pp. 105, 107

### [CTB-C09-012] Planning for subsequent A races: check AE with efficiency factor

- Biggest question after first A race: how well is aerobic endurance maintained. AE is the most important basic ability and probably lost during advanced emphasis and the transition.
- Repeat the functional aerobic threshold (FAeT) test right after transition (Appendix B Field Tests) and compare efficiency factor (EF) to the last base 3 result.
  - About same: go straight to build for next A race.
  - EF lower by 5 percent or more than previous base 3 best: start back in base 3, continue until EF is less than 5 percent lower than before. Base 3 emphasis then on AE.
- If too few weeks: customize and blend rebuilding AE with advanced abilities (not optimal, sometimes necessary).
- Figure 8.1 example: after first A race and brief transition, returned to base 3 a little over 2 weeks (4 days in week 28) to rebuild AE, muscular force (MS weights) and speed skills; last week of that base 3 includes a B race; then 2 back-to-back builds, 1 peak week, 1 race week. Near-perfect when ample weeks between (likely with only two A races); three A races usually tighter (see Figs 7.2, 7.3).
- Races in a subsequent base period, especially C-priority, count as training and as advanced ability work (e.g., a crit in week 29 covers ME, AnE, SP); that week is otherwise basic abilities.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:power metric:hr level:advanced subtopic:efficiency-factor subtopic:multiple-a-races
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 125-126

### [CTB-C09-013] Using Tables 8.3/8.4 and counting weight lifting load

- Daily numbers are suggestions; rearrange to fit Table 8.1 routine; a workout may need longer duration/TSS for the event type.
- Plan daily volume at the end of each week for the coming week, not in advance. Planning is quick if training is progressing; difficult when it is not.
- Prefer TSS as the volume metric (reflects frequency, duration, intensity).
- Weight lifting in ATP volume: time in the gym is not indicative of strength gains (waiting for stations); TSS better. A method to give TSS to strength workouts is in Chapter 12.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:load level:all subtopic:tss subtopic:weight-lifting
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 136, 140

### [CTB-C09-014] Missed workouts: 1-3 days missed

- Standard calendar flow assumes moving to next mesocycle every 3-4 weeks; causes of interruption: lifestyle, illness, poor weather, failing performance goals in testing.
- 1 to 3 days away: continue plan without significant change. If a BT was missed, rearrange remaining workouts to fit it back in, never two BTs back to back (may have to leave one out). Forget non-BT missed sessions.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:missed-workouts
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | p. 140

### [CTB-C09-015] Missed workouts: 4-6 days missed

- If due to illness (cold, sore throat, flu): not ready to return to normal training even when symptoms gone (body chemistry changed; low power, high HR, elevated perceived exertion). Treat as more than six workouts missed (see 7-14 days rule).
- If lifestyle interruption and healthy/ready: treat the lost time as a rest and recovery period; ATP no longer synchronized with A race. Options:
  1. In base or build, shorten current period by 1 week; if still unsynchronized, do same for next period. Do NOT eliminate an R&R week, but may shorten it to 3 or 4 days.
  2. If peak was planned for 2 weeks, make it 1 (fitness gains more important than form).
- Both reduce race readiness; you cannot miss several workouts and have the same fitness.
- On return, make up probably 2 or 3 missed BTs: choose the most important by limiters and reschedule; push others later; eventually omit, modify or combine remaining missed workouts.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:missed-workouts subtopic:illness
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 141

### [CTB-C09-016] Missed workouts: 7-14 days and more than two weeks

7 to 14 days lost to illness:
- In build: restart in base 3. In base 2 or 3: return to the previous base period; if in base 1, repeat base 1. Stay 2 to 4 weeks or until aerobic endurance workouts show HR and power again matching pre-illness (use efficiency factor, workout AE2, Appendix B); when in doubt give another day or two.
- When EF and power return, repeat the last hard week before the interruption. If it goes well, resume original plan; if not, rest a couple of days and repeat it. Leave out 1 to 3 weeks or more of planned training (e.g., build 2 or first week of a 2-week peak). Complete the full base period.

More than two weeks missed:
- In build: return to base 3 and start over. In base: back up one period. Omit at least 2 weeks; omission priority: first week of peak, build 2, build 1, in that order. Complete all three base periods.
- If the 2 lost weeks were the peak period: continue to race week as if nothing happened.
- If 2 or 3 weeks of build 2 lost: repeat as much of build 1 as fits and reduce peak to 1 week. If more than 3 weeks lost: omit build 2 and peak, repeat as much of build 1 as possible, then race week training.
- If due to illness: be conservative; short to medium workouts, intensity primarily zones 1 and 2 until feeling normal; use EF to judge aerobic fitness re-established, then full training.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:missed-workouts subtopic:illness subtopic:efficiency-factor
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 141-142

### [CTB-C09-017] Keep it simple: linear model default and weekly review

- If overwhelmed or new, follow the linear periodization model; when in doubt, keep it simple. Experienced riders may use more complex models in Ch 9 to match individual needs. Hold off completing ATP until Ch 9 if desired.
- Review the plan at the end of each week and make small adjustments to next week's workouts and long-term direction. Planning macrocycle to microcycles is tedious the first time and easier afterward; plan gives focus and confidence.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:linear-periodization subtopic:weekly-review
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 142-143

### [CTB-C09-018] Fast- versus slow-recovery athletes: mesocycle length

- Fast-recovery athletes: 4-week base and build mesocycles (Table 7.4). About the first 3 weeks (likely a few more than 21 days; see Ch 11) at high training load (high duration or intensity or both), then a rest and recovery break typically 3 to 5 days.
- Slow-recovery athletes: need rest and recovery sooner, usually after about 2 weeks (maybe a bit more than 14 days; see Ch 11). Use 3-week subperiods: about 2 weeks hard then a few days of rest and recovery.
- How to tell: experience. If after 2 weeks of hard training you are still eager to go hard another week, you recover quickly. If you are often fatigued and quality drops in week three, you recover slowly.
- Novices and riders over 50 typically recover slowly, but not always; the author has coached fast-recovering over-50 and novice athletes and slow-recovering advanced and under-50 athletes. Be honest in self-evaluation.
- Doing long or intense workouts in a third consecutive week while fatigued is counterproductive.
tags: type:decision-rule type:periodization level:all level:masters level:beginner subtopic:recovery-speed subtopic:mesocycle-length sport:cycling phase:base phase:build
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | p. 148

### [CTB-C09-019] Weekly plan review and keeping the plan alive

- A plan is not final; adjust when time demands cause skipped workouts. Race day never changes, so modify coming weeks to get back on track (see Ch 8 Missed Workouts).
- Review on Sunday after the last ride: record the workout, then check the coming week. If it works, go; if doubts, adjust based on how the past week went, current fitness and fatigue, and known lifestyle demands.
- Avoid last-minute changes while rolling out of the driveway; they result in random training and poor race performance.
- The plan is both a guide and a record; keep a written ledger in the training diary (Ch 14) to inform next season's plan.
tags: type:decision-rule type:monitoring level:all subtopic:weekly-review subtopic:training-diary sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 158-159

### [CTB-C09-020] Keeping risk high but manageable: dose and density rules

- Be a tad restrained with dose and density.
- Dose example: do not begin the season's weight program with high loads and low reps (save for the muscular strength (MS) phase). Start with light loads and high reps; over several sessions increase loads and reduce reps. Interval sessions on the bike start at manageable intensity and build.
- Density: space high-dose workouts (heavy-load lifting, high-intensity intervals) so the body can adapt between them; adaptation cannot be rushed; may need 48 hours or more of recovery. Change from a single session is minuscule; many small increments produce great progress without breakdown.
- High-dose and high-density training is not forbidden, but must be infrequent and strictly managed. Allow time to adapt before another risky workout; otherwise risk of overtraining greatly increases.
tags: type:decision-rule type:principle level:advanced subtopic:dose-density subtopic:progression metric:load
src: Ch 10 | pp. 165-166
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C09-021] Managing overreaching: rest breaks and the daily question

- Key to avoiding overtraining while overreaching is frequent recovery: extended rest-and-recovery (R&R) breaks about every third or fourth week during base and build periods (Chapters 7-8) to shed accumulated fatigue.
- Breaks rejuvenate an overreached rider. If fatigue does not go away even after several days with no workouts during a break, the rider is overtrained (unlikely but happens with extremely motivated riders).
- Single most important question: "How do I feel today?" If "I feel tired" for several days in a row, recover regardless of period or how near a race is; stop and rest now. The tiny fitness loss is not worth the potential huge loss from overtraining.
- Periodization alternates hard days with recovery days; preplanned breaks should come at about the right times if training is appropriately hard.
tags: type:decision-rule level:advanced subtopic:overreaching subtopic:recovery-week subtopic:fatigue-monitoring
src: Ch 10 "Managing Overreaching" | pp. 168-169
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C09-022] Judging whether training stress was too much; fatigue as necessary

- No one-size-fits-all formula for training load; it is highly individualized and comes with experience.
- Sign of too much: readiness to go hard again slows. More than 36 hours to recover is a hint; still fatigued 48 hours after a workout means preceding training was probably too hard. Here fatigue = unable to repeat the workout or another similarly challenging session. This is stage 2 functional overreaching (Table 10.2). With patient fitness gains, a former stage 2 workout becomes a moderate stage 1.
- An experienced rider may deliberately do a stage 2 workout or several stage 1 sessions in a brief period (as in crash training, Chapter 8), but with carefully calculated risk, close fatigue watching and moderation.
- If not tired after 2 or 3 weeks of base or build training, training is not hard enough: risk too low for reward. Fitness and fatigue trend the same direction (Chapter 3); you must frequently become tired to become fitter. The issue is how long fatigue lasts.
- Extreme fatigue lasting several weeks leads to exhaustion and overtraining.
- Greed is the greatest emotional challenge for advanced athletes; the cure is patience. Well-designed plan (Chapters 7-8) allows deliberate overreaching with extended recovery breaks every 3 or 4 weeks.
tags: type:decision-rule type:principle level:advanced subtopic:fatigue-monitoring subtopic:overreaching metric:load
src: Ch 10 Summary | pp. 170-171
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C09-023] Using morning warnings to decide the day

- Score <7: normal training day. Score ≥7: recovery day (active = easy ride, or passive = day off).
- Score 6 or any doubt: err conservative, add rest or reduce workout stress.
- Extreme single warning (e.g. sore throat): lower stress significantly regardless of total.
- Scores always <7: training probably too light; could do more.
- If poor at sensing the need for rest (as many cyclists are), pay closer attention to morning warning trends as training weeks proceed.
- Don't skip an easy day or recovery week by training hard through it; doing so is the biggest mistake.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:morning-warnings
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | pp. 178-179, 188, 191

### [CTB-C09-024] Exceptions when shortening recovery is appropriate

- Artificially shortening recovery is not advisable when the goal is maximal fitness. It is appropriate: in a stage race (goal is being optimally rested for next stage, not maximal adaptation); during crash training (Ch 8) such as an early-season camp, after which a few days of recovery regain balance (works only if stress-recovery balance is achieved in the short term).
- Rest-and-recovery "week" after 2-3 weeks of training is not literally 7 days: some ready in 3 days, others need 5+; older and less fit need more. Days required vary between periods.
- Easy-day mistakes: making zone 1 rides become zone 3, or short active recovery become long. Recovery days must be easy; load increases pay off in high-intensity sessions, not recovery.
- Easy day definition: intensity = zone 1; duration relative to capacity: elite at ~20 h/week may do 2 h; someone training 6 h/week about 20 minutes.
- Most critical rest times: week before an A race and the week after.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:easy-day subtopic:crash-training
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | pp. 181, 191-192

### [CTB-C09-025] When to lift relative to riding

- Best: lift after riding or on a day off the bike.
- If lifting before another workout: be very cautious; fatigue/strain can greatly affect the ride, especially in the MS phase with heavy loads. MS lifting before a long ride impairs pedaling technique; before high-intensity intervals, avoid altogether. In MS, even the day after a session may be too soon for long/intense rides for some.
- If you must lift before a ride, make the ride short and easy (recovery). If lifting before a long or intense ride, separate sessions by several hours (e.g., gym early morning, ride late in day).
- Anytime in doubt about readiness to ride after lifting, ride short and easy.
- Best avoided entirely by following the periodization in Table 12.3.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling subtopic:lifting-ride-sequencing phase:hypertrophy level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 | p. 198

### [CTB-C09-026] Skipping AA and posture rules

Athletes are tempted to skip AA and go to heavy loads/few reps: usually a mistake. Learn effective movement patterns (lifting positions, posture, foot positions simulating pedals, depth) before heavy loads. Keep eyes forward, not down, to keep load on legs and not low back. Emphasize mastering pedaling-like movement under heavy load rather than a strong-looking body. Functional force workouts best avoided by riders with tender knees. Muscular force and cadence are the two components of power; develop both early in season (prep and base 1).
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling phase:anatomical-adaptation subtopic:movement-mastery level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 Summary | pp. 225-227

### [CTB-C09-027] C-priority races during the taper

- Races in the taper period should be C priority and shorter than the goal race, so you can recover and go hard again after two easy days.
- Avoid any race likely to prevent that recovery, or intentionally abandon at a preplanned point. Finishing an extremely stressful race may leave you unready in three days, spoiling the taper and threatening the A race.
- If unsure about the C race, don't do it; stick to the plan.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling phase:taper level:advanced subtopic:c-race
src: Ch 13 | p. 231

### [CTB-C09-028] Race week scheduling, travel and heat/altitude

- Race week is unlike the rest of the season: rides are very short and highly focused on race intensity, with both rest and intensity emphasized more than in the peak period.
- Easiest day is best two days before the race: day off or at most a short low-intensity ride; may be a travel day.
- The greater the venue's heat or altitude relative to recent training, the earlier in the week to arrive; even a little adaptation is better than none.
- The day before the race includes a very brief race-intensity session; stay off your legs the rest of the day.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling phase:race level:advanced subtopic:travel subtopic:heat subtopic:altitude
src: Ch 13 | p. 235

### [CTB-C09-029] Adjusting the taper after the race (troubleshooting)

- Felt flat on race day: next time a shorter taper, or harder workouts in the last three days.
- Felt tired on race day: lower total training load in the last days.
- Believe you can race at a higher level: experiment with workout types, especially intensity, on the hard days.
- A taper is not foolproof; interruptions (missed key workout, cold, bad weather) happen. Training is a scientific study with one subject: you. If it didn't work, fix it next time.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling phase:taper level:advanced subtopic:taper-adjustment
src: Ch 13 Summary | p. 236

### [CTB-C09-030] Avoid paralysis by analysis; record little but never too little

- Record data most applicable to goals and objectives, little else. Superfluous data hides what is important, hinders spotting trends and course corrections ("paralysis by analysis"). Diary should be simple, succinct, meaningful.
- Detail-oriented riders may keep much; if not into charts/data, keep it bare bones with only critical goal-related data. Recording too little is as bad as too much. Always include perceptions of how the workout went; the diary is not solely numbers.
- If you set high goals but hate recording and analyzing data, hire a coach to do the analysis and seasonal adjustments.
- Basic details to include all season: periodic test results and race performance, both numerical and subjective. These are the best indicators of progress toward goals.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:data-overload
src: Ch 14 pp. 246, 249-250

### [CTB-C09-031] Training analysis: fitness, fatigue, form via the diary

- Main role of the diary is spotting trends to adjust training toward the goal, analyzed frequently from the recorded data.
- Sort data into fitness, fatigue, form (Ch 3):
 - Fitness: the mix of climbing, sprinting and time-trialing phenotype abilities required for the next A-race. Analyze performance-specific data plus physical and mental notes for trends.
 - Fatigue: how well recovered you are entering hard workouts. You are seldom fully recovered; the question is fatigue at the start of key sessions. Overly fatigued: uninspiring session data. Frequently overly rested: wasting time that could go to hard workouts. Judge from recent morning warnings plus physical/mental notes. Morning warnings should seldom be 0 before hard rides; higher scores, even an occasional 7, indicate working at about your limit.
 - Form: race readiness plus freshness on race day. Race readiness goes beyond fitness: adaptation to expected conditions (heat, cold, wind, rain, snow) and having a race plan with strategies and tactics. Use physical/mental notes, especially in the last two to three weeks before the race, to judge the balance.
- Performance Management Chart (TrainingPeaks.com, brainchild of Dr. Andrew Coggan): one of the best gauges of fitness, fatigue and form. By making daily adjustments to workout TSS you can manage fitness and fatigue and plan a level of form for race day. Good form also needs management of other life aspects.
- Predict the A race from how training and B and C races have gone over several weeks; the assessment usually becomes clear during the taper.
tags: type:monitoring level:all system:aerobic metric:load subtopic:fitness-fatigue-form subtopic:performance-management-chart
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Training Analysis" | pp. 247-248

### [CTB-C09-032] Trend review and troubleshooting stalled fitness

- Do an in-depth diary review periodically, looking for trends; essential after a rest and recovery (R&R) week, when you are fresh. Compare with previous R&R weeks. Progress? If not, why; if yes, what is working.
- Progress markers: better performance numbers (phenotype metrics, Table 14.1) and subjective ones (hard workouts feel easier). Compare with past tests and low-priority races.
- Spot trends in specific metrics (e.g., hip-, knee-, ankle-extension loads, FTP). If not increasing, find out why; correct direction immediately.
- Also track power for durations key to your phenotype (Fig 2.1; Table 14.1). Power should rise through the season after occasional plateaus; if flat for long or decreasing, there is a problem.
- Causes, in order: (1) most common: missed/inconsistent workouts. Check consistency of race-specific objective workouts (Appendix B); become more consistent. (2) second most common, even among motivated riders: training too densely, hard workouts too close together, fatigue limiting power; needs more recovery and adaptation time. (3) other: nutrition, sleep, stress, etc. Non-numerical diary data helps resolve.
- Tests or low-priority races undertaken with some freshness provide objectives that predict race-goal achievement; watch for them.
- Make training adjustments on the fly ("a little more of this or less of that"); ask "what's important?" after each workout.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:trend-analysis subtopic:plateau subtopic:consistency
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 Summary | pp. 250-251

### [CTB-C09-033] Race plan and post-race analysis by race type

- Race plan is based on goals, addresses at least the anticipated key episodes and demands and how you will deal with them. Refine in the last few weeks and rehearse many times in workouts and lower-priority races in build and peak periods. Know your role in the team's plan well before race day. Base it on capability shown by trends so far; plans should reflect improving levels through the season.
- Criterium: expect frequent early attacks as teams and individuals vie for control; attacks become less frequent but more strategic; be prepared to respond to or initiate. Question: were you physically and mentally ready for these episodes?
- Time trial: pacing decides. Break the course into segments by terrain or distance, each with a specific plan; rehearse repeatedly in build and taper. First segment almost always controlled effort (common error: too fast, build fatigue, fade). "You can't win a time trial in the first few minutes, but you can lose it." Plan comes down to power management. Question: did you pace it as planned?
- Road race: tactical moves relative to hills, corners, wind etc. giving opportunities for attacks; plan how to respond to and initiate. Goal: nothing unexpected unless initiated by you or team. Questions: were you prepared for others' attacks? Did you use your strengths to achieve the race goal?
- Post-race: assess after every race, especially A races, individually and with teammates. Immediate analysis may be driven by emotion and impulsive conclusions; often wait a day. Evaluate the day after, good or bad. Key questions: Did you achieve the race goal? If not, why? (Crit/road) key episodes and how you managed, initiated or responded. (TT) How was pacing throughout? Unanticipated factors (equipment selection, weather, mechanicals, crashes, road conditions, nutrition, other): preventable? Did you react appropriately? What would you do differently in preparation and racing in the future? If it went well, review preparation: specific workouts (especially in the taper), race plan, pre-race nutrition, equipment selection. Record everything in the diary.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling phase:race level:all subtopic:race-analysis subtopic:race-plan subtopic:pacing
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Race Analysis" and Summary | pp. 248-251

## C10 Applied Guidance

### [CTB-C10-001] Your high-performance team

- Ask family, friends and teammates to be part of your support team; sharing responsibility for the outcome raises commitment and motivation. Tell them your goal; at minimum they can attend races. Teammates can train with you, suggest workouts, encourage, give psychological support; reciprocate.
- Enlist a coach or knowledgeable mentor (more important the higher the goal; can be remote).
- Also suggested: physical therapist, sports medicine doctor, masseuse, mechanic, bike fitter; additionally personal trainer (gym), nutritionist, chiropractor, sports psychologist. Tell each your goal. Some may never be used, but knowing they are available boosts confidence.
- Each member must be happy, positive, successful and likable; replace those who are not. Surround yourself only with people who believe in and support you; avoid those who do not.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:support-team
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Your High-Performance Team" | pp. 12-13

### [CTB-C10-002] Physical assessment by PT and annual professional bike fit

- Physical assessment: schedule at season start with a physical therapist who works with endurance athletes/cyclists. Head-to-toe exam: posture, strength, range of joint motion, muscular balance, dynamic function, structural idiosyncrasies and asymmetries. Purpose: determine unique injury risk and prepare for the bike fit.
- If risks found: PT designs a corrective exercise program (functional strengthening, mobility); ask also for bike position/equipment suggestions for the fitter, workout types that are structurally beneficial, and exercises/movements to avoid short-term. Follow it with great care, as important as bike workouts; PT should streamline it to a reasonable time. If severe structural weaknesses: schedule a follow-up exam to gauge progress and adjust.
- Bike fit: after the physical assessment, see a professional bike fitter; not spouse, friend, training partner, nor yourself. Friel has seen riders raise FTP by more than 10 percent after a fit, even those who believed their position was perfect (comparable gain would take several weeks of training).
- Repeat every season even on the same bike: muscle strength, flexibility or mass change; range of motion changes with aging, strength training, workload; components may have changed (saddle, stem, pedals, shoes); race types may differ; minor knee tenderness. Fit both road and time trial bikes.
- Top professional teams insist on assessments and fits at season start.
tags: type:protocol level:all sport:cycling phase:prep subtopic:bike-fit subtopic:physical-therapy subtopic:injury-prevention
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "Physical Assessment and Bike Fit" | pp. 62-63

### [CTB-C10-003] Recovery nutrition and hydration

- Nutrition is second in importance to sleep for recovery; nutrients provide building blocks used (during sleep) for repair of muscle, immune system, bone, injuries. Eating real, nutrient-dense food after workouts is probably the most effective thing besides sleep.
- Quality matters: macronutrients (protein, carbohydrate, fat) and micronutrients (vitamins, minerals, phytochemicals). Junk/highly processed food is nearly devoid of micronutrients (processing removes them; re-added claims shouldn't be believed). Less processed is better. Aging adults lose health by continuing to eat junk; high-stress training raises need for quality food. Pills cannot make up for a poor diet.
- Avoid supplements altogether (author's position): no evidence they improve performance or prevent illness; some may be detrimental; poor regulation (especially in the US), label claims unreliable. Eat a healthy diet rich in micronutrients and eliminate junk food.
- Sports drinks, bars, gels are junk food; acceptable for convenience on long rides (a few calories do little harm) but more expensive than real food (fruit). Sugary food at occasions (birthday) is fine; keep infrequent, depending on seriousness.
- Hydration: second tier after sleep and eating. Drink to satisfy thirst: if thirsty, drink; if not, don't. No need to weigh before and after. Studies of elite athletes drinking to thirst over days maintained body weight.
- Over age 60: thirst may decrease with age (studies on non-athletes); pay closer attention to hydration after workouts, perhaps drink a bit beyond thirst (amount unspecified). Excess hydration beyond thirst has not been shown to improve performance.
- Best drink: water. Advanced athlete riding <about 90 minutes, even high stress: water is fine. Beyond that, carbohydrate need increases for those on a high-carb diet; chronically low-carb riders do fine with little/no carbohydrate during several hours.
- Coffee and alcoholic beverages do not cause dehydration (considerable research shows they contribute to positive hydration); author does not necessarily recommend them for recovery.
tags: type:nutrition type:recovery level:masters subtopic:hydration subtopic:real-food subtopic:supplements
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | pp. 180, 183-184

### [CTB-C10-004] Alternative recovery aids: compression, pneumatic devices, massage, others

- Alternative aids may shorten recovery but may interfere with adaptation by producing the sensation of being recovered while adaptation is incomplete. Evidence mixed; benefits relatively small and individual. Use when appropriate if they work for you.
- Compression garments (stockings, sleeves, tights, suits): research inconclusive (varied sports, garments, pressures; results positive to negative). Performance gains minimal or nonexistent per author; may help recovery (most research suggests so; possible placebo).
- Pneumatic compression devices (NormaTec, Recovery Boots, RevitaPump; chambers inflated/deflated in sequence, legs toe to hip): compression plus massage-like effect; research generally positive; author's experience beneficial; pricey, often used at recovery stations; possible placebo; not everyone agrees.
- Massage: effectiveness depends on type and skill. Classic study: one leg massaged four days after intense exercise; no difference in peak torque or Doppler blood flow, but perceived soreness lower in the massaged leg. Benefits may be more psychological (well-being, less anxiety, better mood); a few studies show reduced heart rate and blood pressure and higher HRV.
- Others (each helped in some studies, none works for all): hyperbaric oxygen, low-frequency electromyostimulation, ultrasound, alternating hot/cold immersion, music, meditation, leg elevation, foam roller, stretching, Jacuzzi, flotation, chocolate milk.
- Find what works for you; only sleeping, eating, drinking must be applied consistently.
tags: type:recovery level:all subtopic:compression subtopic:massage subtopic:alternative-recovery
evidence: clinical-trial
src: Ch 11 | pp. 184-186

### [CTB-C10-005] Weight lifting guidelines (gym, equipment, set mechanics)

- Gym: home gym with dumbbells and simple equipment costs a couple hundred dollars; saves time and is more likely to be used.
- Free weights vs machines: free weights need balance (strengthen small muscles, core) but risk falling/dropping; machines are safer but don't strengthen balance muscles. New lifters: start with machines, shift to free weights gradually.
- Set progression: complete all sets of one exercise before the next (keeps warm-up, avoids waiting). Supersetting (alternating sets of two exercises, preferably different muscle groups) is OK in an uncrowded gym.
- Recovery between sets: per phase table. Too-short recoveries reduce load or reps and shift focus to endurance, which is unnecessary since the bike provides it; fatigue helps endurance but prevents strength development.
- Load: increase conservatively while reps decrease over weeks.
- Spotter: advisable for risky exercises such as heavy squats.
- Concentric (muscle shortens, lifting) vs eccentric (muscle lengthens, lowering). Eccentric allows much greater load and strains fibers (contracting while lengthening), potential damage: set heavy loads down cautiously.
- Simulate pedaling: nonspecific exercises (arm curls) are unproductive; seated knee extensions better; multi-joint exercises (squats, leg presses, step-ups) best, because hips, knees, ankles move together in pedaling.
- Agonist/antagonist: if the agonist becomes too strong there is risk to the antagonist during powerful movement; build antagonist with isolated single-joint exercise (leg curls for hamstrings vs quads). Loads/reps for antagonists differ; a normal strength difference is fine if not too great.
- Workout spacing: spread sessions evenly through the week (2/wk: Monday and Friday, not Monday and Tuesday; 3/wk: Tue/Thu/Sat, not Tue/Wed/Thu). Long gaps lose gains; the 1/wk gap is expected only in maintenance (SM).
- Exercise order: heaviest-load exercises first (fresh, safer); alternate muscle groups/movements; avoid two hip-knee-ankle extension exercises back to back (e.g., squats then step-ups).
- Starting a new phase: be conservative with loads in the first one or two sessions.
- Unusual exercises: have an experienced person show you; video yourself and compare to online technique; risk of injury is high with improper technique, heavy or ballistic loads.
- Number of exercises: program uses few; may do more if time/energy allow. Do the fewest exercises that produce results.
- Own program: if a past program worked, stay with it.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling system:neuromuscular subtopic:weight-lifting-guidelines level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 "Weight Lifting Guidelines" | pp. 197-203

### [CTB-C10-006] Protein and leucine after lifting

Research suggests neuromuscular gains are enhanced by eating 10 to 25 grams (0.35 to 0.875 ounces) of leucine-rich protein immediately after a lifting session (or within three hours of completion). Amount depends on body size and workout difficulty. Real food is fine. Examples providing about 10 g protein and roughly 1,000 mg leucine: boiled eggs (2 medium), cheddar cheese (1.5 ounces), or milk (10 ounces).
tags: type:nutrition type:dose metric:g subtopic:leucine subtopic:post-workout-protein level:all
evidence: unsupported (author cites "the research" without reference)
src: Ch 12 | pp. 203-204

### [CTB-C10-007] Diary formats and keeping it simple

- Formats: paper notebook; electronic calendar-based diary on a computer; web-based diary such as TrainingPeaks.com; other electronic options (one-time purchase or small monthly fee).
- Electronic advantages: graphs, pulling up related past workout data, pinpointing key markers, rearranging data. They save time and help interpret large amounts of power, heart rate, speed, cadence and gradient data. Some provide planning and analysis tools acting as a virtual coach. For serious racers electronic versions are probably the way to go.
- A diary need not be complex; the simpler it is, the more you get from it. Too much daily data can make you forget long-term purpose.
- Simplest form: frequently viewed list of season goals and training objectives (Ch 5), seasonal plan (Ch 7), weekly planned workouts (Ch 8), historical workout data, race results and your thoughts.
- Write goals on the cover, first page, or a card used as page marker (paper), or record and review them frequently (electronic).
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:diary-format
src: Ch 14 pp. 238, 249

## C11 Recovery, Monitoring & Lifestyle

### [CTB-C11-001] Stress, recovery and overtraining: the balancing act

- Research since the first edition: you must frequently flirt with overtraining to approach your potential as an athlete.
- Repeated fatigue affects subsequent workouts and daily life; managing fatigue is a balancing act.
- The stress-adaptation-performance loop timing depends on how effectively you recover after hard rides. Challenge: keep the time needed for this progression as brief as possible without shortchanging adaptation. This is the dilemma of short-term recovery (Chapters 10 and 11).
- Long-term recovery from cumulative fatigue also drives high performance, especially in tapering for the most important races.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:overreaching subtopic:short-term-recovery subtopic:fatigue metric:load sport:cycling
src: Prologue | p. xiii
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C11-002] Consistency, routines and priorities

- Mind and body like routines; regular schedules increase training consistency and goal achievement. Pre-race routines (food/drink, meal timing, music, warm-up, mental rehearsal, strategy talk) keep you relaxed and ready; the same applies to pre-workout routines: standard time of day, weekly pattern of workout types, pre-workout food/drink, training partners, courses for specific workouts.
- Random workouts hurt training; a weekly training plan is needed (Chapter 8).
- Daily lifestyle routine (sleep, work/school, family, friends) varies per person; think about how each affects training and organize them to foster excellence.
- Priorities: everything cannot be top priority. Most serious riders put family and friends first, career second, training third. If more than two higher-priority activities precede training, workouts will frequently be missed.
- The higher the goal, the greater the commitment and the more exacting the daily routine; the goal must be realistic relative to your life. Interruptions happen; roll with the punches and adjust. Need a standard weekly and daily routine fitting everything in order of priority.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:routine subtopic:consistency subtopic:priorities
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1 "Consistency and Routines" | pp. 11-12

### [CTB-C11-003] Step 4: Immediate Feedback and post-workout analysis

- The most effective setup is the expert present during training for immediate feedback. Feedback one day later is better than none. Workout data reviewed during or right after the workout is far more effective than data left unanalyzed for days. The sooner the feedback, the faster the progression.
- The expert is usually unable to attend every workout, so feedback is often delayed. It can be face to face, by email or by text. A weekly conversation on how training is going is a good chance to ask questions, confirm workout purposes were achieved and see the bigger picture.
- Self-coached athletes must stay mentally engaged with how they feel during workouts. If the mind drifts during pedaling-skill work or intervals, it is as if the coach left. Scrutinize what is happening. Analyze the data files from the devices as soon as possible after each session.
- Key question: did I accomplish the purpose of the workout? If yes, you are closer to the goal. If no, the expert corrects course.
- Workout analysis is covered in detail in Chapter 14.
tags: type:monitoring level:all subtopic:feedback subtopic:workout-analysis sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 | pp. 23-24

### [CTB-C11-004] Table 10.1 Common Symptoms of Overtraining Syndrome by Category

| CATEGORY | SYMPTOMS |
| Physical | Resting heart rate higher or lower than usual; Weight loss; Hungrier than normal; Loss of appetite; Lethargy; Restless sleep, insomnia; Chronic fatigue; Muscle soreness; Joint soreness; Slow-healing minor cuts; Menstrual cycle dysfunction |
| Performance | Hard workout performance greatly reduced; Bike power low at a given heart rate; Unable to complete workouts; Decreased muscular strength; Poor muscular coordination; Mediocre bike-handling skills; Deterioration of skills |
| Psychological | Moody, grumpy, and emotional; Apathetic; Low motivation to train; Poor concentration; Decreased self-esteem; Very high race anxiety; Loss of competitiveness; Depression |
| Physiological | Low peak lactate level; Heart rate low at a high power or pace; High heart rate at low to moderate power intensities; Perceived exertion high at a given power output; Low heart rate variability; Increased oxygen consumption during submaximal exercise; Reduced maximal exercise capacity |
| Immunological | Increased susceptibility to colds, flu, and allergies; Swollen lymph glands; Bacterial infection; Abnormal white blood cell differentials |
| Biochemical | Reduced muscle glycogen concentration; Elevated serum cortisol; Decreased serum ferritin; Decreased bone mineral density |
Notes: not all occur in every athlete; an overtrained rider or companion may notice only a couple.
tags: type:monitoring level:advanced subtopic:overtraining-symptoms metric:hr metric:hrv metric:power
src: Ch 10 Table 10.1 | p. 168
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C11-005] Rating fatigue on a 0-10 scale

- Fatigue can be rated on a 0 to 10 scale like the perceived-exertion scale of Ch 4; higher = greater fatigue. Serious athletes are always at least a bit tired.
- Fatigue of 7 or higher is the level related to fitness-enhancing adaptation during recovery. It should occur fairly often after hard workouts, after densely spaced moderate-to-hard workouts, and at the end of several weeks of training. When fatigue is 7 or higher, it is time for a recovery day (or several).
- No precise test of training fatigue exists (unlike VO2max or anaerobic threshold tests); recovery is more art than science, mostly based on self-perception. Recovery is highly individual, and effectiveness may vary from workout to workout.
- Non-training stressors (career, family, friends, finances, domestic stability) add to fatigue (Selye's distressors) and cannot be quantified; they must always be taken into consideration.
tags: type:monitoring metric:rpe level:all subtopic:fatigue-scale
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11, "Measuring Fatigue" | pp. 174-175

### [CTB-C11-006] Heart rate variability (HRV) as a fatigue measure

- HRV measures intervals between heartbeats. It reflects the autonomic nervous system (ANS): sympathetic (fight-or-flight; a hard workout or race counts as a threat) versus parasympathetic (calming).
- As workouts become demanding and fatigue accumulates, the sympathetic system dominates: HRV falls (timing between beats becomes more consistent). When thoroughly rested, the parasympathetic system dominates: HRV is greater (time between beats varies a lot).
- Measure at a standard time, e.g. first thing in the morning. Low variability = fatigue high; high variability = fatigue low. "Low" and "high" are relative; it takes a fair amount of collected data to know what they mean for you. Device software instructions help interpret numbers.
- Tools: chest strap, finger sensor, wrist sensor; app HRV4Training uses the phone camera.
tags: type:monitoring metric:hrv level:all subtopic:hrv
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | pp. 175-177

### [CTB-C11-007] Muscle glycogen and other fatigue-measuring tools

- Glycogen depletion is one of the most widely accepted physiological indicators of fatigue after a hard workout. Glycogen is limited compared with stored fat; when it runs low, fatigue sets in; very low = "hit the wall"/"bonked" (extreme fatigue). Restocking by eating takes several hours; restored glycogen indicates recovery in part.
- Biopsy was the only sure measure in the past. MuscleSound is a non-invasive wand device measuring muscle glycogen; costs several hundred dollars per month, used mostly in clinics and pro/collegiate teams; not practical for personal use.
- Restwise (website): assesses 11 markers of overtraining-related fatigue (e.g. resting heart rate, body weight, sleep, hydration, appetite, muscle soreness) from self-perception queries, gives training feedback, shows trends. HRV4Training: HRV via phone camera.
- Even with devices, determining fatigue still comes down to paying close attention to how you feel; nothing tops this.
tags: type:monitoring level:all subtopic:glycogen subtopic:fatigue-tools
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | pp. 176-177

### [CTB-C11-008] Table 11.1 Common Morning Warning Indicators

Procedure: place a copy by the bed. On waking, before getting up, quickly answer yes/no to each warning (no pausing to think). Add the yes scores.

| INDICATORS | WARNINGS (YES OR NO) | YES SCORE |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Appetite | Very high or very low? | 1 |
| Waking pulse | High? | 1 |
| Enthusiasm | Just want to stay in bed? | 1 |
| Motivation to train | Low? | 1 |
| Overall feeling | Very fatigued; very stressed? | 1 |
| Training Stress Balance (TrainingPeaks.com) | Lower than -30? | 1 |
| Mood | Unusually grumpy; easily angered? | 2 |
| Sleep | Very poor quality and/or inadequate length? | 2 |
| Lying-standing heart rate comparison | High differential heart rate? | 2 |
| Health | Something not right with health? | 2 |
| Muscles, joints | Soreness? | 2 |
| Heart rate variability | Low? | 2 |

Footnote: These indicators suggest stress may be too high and recovery may be needed that day. A combined Yes Score of 7 or higher suggests you need more recovery and less stress that day.
Interpretation: total fewer than 7 = normal training day; 7 or higher = recovery (active easy ride or passive day off). Score of 6 (doubt) = allow a bit more rest or reduce workout stress somewhat. One or two warnings can occur even when things go well; the cumulative weight matters. A single extreme warning can override the total (e.g. sore throat: significantly lower stress regardless of score). If the score is nearly always below 7, training is probably not hard enough; scores of 7+ every few days indicate you are doing something right.
tags: type:monitoring type:threshold level:all subtopic:morning-warnings metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | pp. 177-178

### [CTB-C11-009] Sleep as the primary recovery tool

- Bed is the best recovery device; sleep is the most powerful form of recovery and fitness enhancement. During sleep anabolic hormones repair muscle and soft tissue; also restores immune system, restructures bone, heals niggling injuries, restocks energy stores.
- Do not shorten sleep to fit more in (e.g. up late then alarm early): artificial shortening of recovery reduces adaptation, one of the greatest mistakes. Go to bed earlier, sleep until waking naturally, especially after a hard ride.
- Can't "catch up" on weekends; sleep can't be saved.
- Sleep is the single most important thing you can do besides riding hard. Never skip sleeping, eating, drinking.
- Habit rule: "Never stand if you can lean, never lean if you can sit, and never sit if you can lie down."
- Remaining hours after a hard 2-3 h ride should be dedicated to recovery.
tags: type:recovery level:all subtopic:sleep
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | pp. 181-183, 186

### [CTB-C11-010] Record final days before every A race

In the final few days before every A race, record in the training diary (Ch 14): travel details, sleep patterns, when and what you eat, mental and physical stressors, equipment choices such as wheels, pre-race warm-up, and anything else relevant. If the race goes well, repeat these details; if not, study and adjust. Taper results are not guaranteed (Merckx did not win the road worlds on his first try).
tags: type:monitoring sport:cycling phase:race level:all subtopic:training-diary
src: Ch 13 | p. 235

### [CTB-C11-011] What to record: six data categories

General rule: keep it simple. Record only info related to training objectives (subgoals indicating readiness for a seasonal goal, Ch 5). Don't feel you must record all categories; customize, and record only what you will later consider.
1. Morning warnings: assess workout readiness on waking (Ch 11, Table 11.1). After a few days choose the best indicators of unusual stress for you and record the warnings score daily; watch the trend over days (explains current fatigue or a training high).
2. Basic workout data: date, time of day, planned workout, workout as completed, course/route/venue, duration, TSS, equipment used. Also anything out of the norm: weather (cold, hot, windy, rain), training partner who made you work harder or easier, unusual events (knee not right, underinflated tires).
3. Performance-specific data: see Table 14.1.
4. Physical notes: Did you accomplish what was intended? Grade A to F. How did you feel; too hard or too easy; adequately recovered beforehand; anything to change next time.
5. Mental notes: do not neglect these; emotions and gut feelings are likely the best daily indicator of progress. Record after the workout: satisfied? good progress? motivation? training concerns?
6. Miscellaneous notes: travel, career stress/long hours, injuries and illness, family activities interrupting workouts, equipment changes (bike fit, wheels, saddle position, gearing), changes to usual diet (especially what and when eaten before or during a race), race details.
tags: type:monitoring level:all subtopic:diary-content subtopic:morning-warnings
src: Ch 14 "What to Record" | pp. 244-246

## C12 Adaptations by Population / Context

### [CTB-C12-001] Training focus by years of experience (frequency, duration, intensity)

- Measuring intensity: speedometer, heart rate monitor, power meter, lactate analyzer, perceived exertion (informed assessment of how hard you work). The higher the goal and experience, the more a power meter benefits; the author highly recommends it for all serious riders.
- Year 1 (first 2-3 years, power meter not very important): changing lifestyle and building a strong base; goal is to get out the door and ride often. Frequency is the key to progression.
- Years 2-3 (intermediate): key is increasing ride duration, building aerobic endurance; takes a couple of years.
- Experienced/advanced: focus must be intensity (once frequency and duration are well established; if not, no progress). Not every workout is high intensity; recovery sessions are necessary. Intensity of key workouts largely determines racing success. With rare exceptions intensity is the primary focus.
- Frequency and duration still matter (cannot train infrequently for short durations for weeks and race well), but are less important for measuring and ensuring progress.
- Common error: advanced athletes keep believing long-duration workouts are the key, because duration is easy and cheap to measure and worked at lower race levels. Accurate intensity measurement is neither easy nor cheap and requires study (Chapter 4).
tags: type:methodology level:beginner level:intermediate level:advanced sport:cycling subtopic:training-focus metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 31-32

### [CTB-C12-002] Training focus by experience level (basic vs advanced abilities)

- Novice riders: limiters are usually basic abilities; spend training on them for one to three years before much advanced work.
- Experienced riders: limiters are usually advanced abilities, but re-establish basics each season first.
- Only veteran riders with many years of racing should insert advanced work early in base.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:beginner level:advanced subtopic:abilities
src: Ch 6 | pp. 79, 84
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C12-003] Choosing mesocycle length: age, recovery capacity, experience

- Mesocycle duration is really about recovery; age is a simplification. Schedule breaks from serious training every few weeks regardless of age.
- Mechanism: anabolic hormones (growth hormone, testosterone, estrogen, insulin-like growth factor, others) drive recovery from hard sessions and decline with age; less hormone = slower recovery = more frequent recovery breaks. Around age 50 the decline is evident.
- Choose the "under 50 / quick recovery" (4-week) or "over 50 / slow recovery" (3-week) column from past experience of accumulating fatigue; if unsure, use age. Some over-50 recover fast, some under-50 slowly.
- Novice and intermediate athletes should use the slow-recovery column (shorter mesocycles) because recovery ability takes about three years to build.
- Different lengths may be used for base and build (e.g., 4 weeks base, 3 weeks build if recovery is quicker in base); if unsure keep both the same.
- Older riders: brief anaerobic endurance workouts year-round help maintain aerobic capacity (decline in aerobic capacity is a major issue for aging athletes).
- Novice athlete: do only base periods all season; Base 3 gives enough advanced work for race fitness and is repeated replacing all build subperiods; 1-week peak.
