evidence: author-experience
tags: type:decision-rule type:periodization level:masters level:beginner subtopic:recovery-capacity subtopic:age
src: Ch 7 | pp. 103-106

### [CTB-C12-004] Who should lift weights - population guidance

- Probably not for riders who dislike gyms or lack time: use functional force training instead. If you race for fun and don't enjoy lifting, don't lift (but potential is less likely reached).
- Some young men with too much muscle (especially upper body) needed endurance, not strength.
- Benefit most: women, novices, skinny ectomorphs, older riders (strongly suggested). Others: give it a try.
- Juniors, especially early teens: be extra cautious; growth plates still developing and susceptible to damage from heavy loads (more likely an issue under age 16, individual maturity varies). Can start at about age 12 with very light loads, focus on technique; teens may do ballistic exercises with very light loads. Should be driven by fun, no strict schedules.
- Over 50: may reap greatest benefits; race performance often limited by muscle mass loss, bone density decrease raises fracture risk; neuromuscular training improves muscle power and rebuilds bones.
- Twenty-somethings long in the sport: need less resistance training, smaller gain, though many top riders swear by lifting.
- Adult novices: benefit significantly but time is the issue; they gain rapidly from riding alone. If they lift, omit the muscular strength (MS) phase in year one; save it for year two.
- Muscle bulk: young males likely to bulk; limit amount of strength training and number of exercises; limit serious lifting to a handful of weeks then maintenance. High reps with light loads to failure give similar strength but more mass.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all level:masters level:beginner subtopic:weight-lifting-populations
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 "Weight Lifting"; "Special groups and weight lifting" | pp. 197, 203-204

## C13 Contraindications, Cautions & Safety Limits

### [CTB-C13-001] Prohibition on increasing workout difficulty without consultation

- WHAT: Making a scheduled training session harder than planned (increasing intensity, volume, or duration).
- LIMIT: Prohibited without prior consultation and approval from the expert or coach who designed the plan.
- WHO: All coached or structured-plan athletes.
- WHY: A workout's low difficulty level frequently serves a critical, deliberate recovery or metabolic purpose; adding unplanned intensity or volume disrupts the planned training progression and compromises future workouts.
- EXCEPTION: Athletes are permitted to make a workout easier on the fly whenever they feel unready (e.g., inadequate recovery, bad timing), notifying the coach afterward.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling level:all subtopic:workout-execution subtopic:coach-athlete-communication
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Step 3: Specific Practice" | p. 22

### [CTB-C13-002] Overtraining risk from excessive stress without recovery

WHAT: continued high-dose, high-density training without adequate recovery; also too-great overload. LIMIT: avoid completely (no numeric limit given here). WHO: all athletes, especially low-fitness riders early in season who skip complete breaks. WHY: overtraining is far beyond fatigue, like a severe illness, and has ended racing careers; overload too great prevents adaptation and causes breakdown. EXCEPTION/mitigation: frequent recoveries in weekly/monthly/annual plans; a day or more fully off weekly for low-fitness riders; light day may suffice for highly fit. Do not progress overly aggressively; do not try to speed supercompensation. See Chapter 10.
tags: type:caution level:all sport:cycling subtopic:overtraining
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | pp. 28, 29, 33, 34

### [CTB-C13-003] Cautions from Chapter 5 (tools, tests, training start)

- Do not ride in traffic while looking at numbers on the handlebar device (WHO: all riders, especially with new equipment).
- Avoid over-reliance on numbers; keep perceived-exertion feel for racing.
- Field tests: choose safe courses (light traffic, no stop signs, few intersections, wide bike lane); keep head up, never ride head-down in the 5-minute test; indoor trainer for aerobic capacity test only if very stable.
- Do not start the season with too much aggression ("Christmas stars" burn out).
- Highly motivated athletes risk overtraining by avoiding rest/recovery and refusing to taper; need external restraint.
- Do not have bike fit done by friend/spouse/yourself; follow PT guidance on exercises and movements to avoid until corrections take hold.
- Do not start the start-up procedures in mid race season; delay until a several-week break before the next high-priority race.
tags: type:caution level:all sport:cycling subtopic:safety
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 | pp. 59, 62-69

### [CTB-C13-004] Cautions on high-intensity and advanced ability training

- WHAT: anaerobic endurance training. LIMIT: use sparingly, only at a few select times per season; do not extend advanced ability training beyond about 8 to 12 weeks. WHO: all riders. WHY: its stress far exceeds other abilities; risk of burnout, injury, illness. EXCEPTION: none.
- WHAT: advanced-ability work in early base. LIMIT: keep short with long recoveries; only veterans with many racing years and exceptional body monitoring. WHY: easy to overdo and wreck the season.
- WHAT: changing pedaling technique. Expect temporarily reduced economy (higher HR/RPE at a given power) for weeks to months; avoid long-duration workouts when changing technique because skills revert with fatigue.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling system:anaerobic subtopic:overtraining level:all
src: Ch 6 | pp. 75-76, 79-81
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C13-005] Crash training risk

- WHAT: crash training (BT sessions on consecutive days) for stage races. LIMIT: most riders no more than 4 consecutive days before a break; some limit is 3. Be very conservative.
- WHO: most vulnerable are novices, juniors, seniors, and any rider who manages repeated stress poorly; they should first decide whether stage racing suits them.
- WHY: flirts with overtraining (Ch 10); can cause injury, illness, burnout and ruin the season; ("crash and burn").
- Benefit if done cautiously: fitness far above before. Key: watch body response, focus on recovery.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling level:beginner level:masters subtopic:crash-training subtopic:overtraining
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 132-133

### [CTB-C13-006] Return to training after illness

- WHAT: resuming normal training after illness (cold, sore throat, flu). LIMIT: not immediately even if symptoms gone; for 7+ days lost, rebuild in base until EF/power match pre-illness; after more than 2 weeks, only short/medium workouts in zones 1 and 2 until normal.
- WHY: body chemistry changed, capacity for exercise reduced (low power, high HR, high RPE).
- Also: never replace a planned easy day/day off with a BT; never schedule two BTs back to back after missed workouts.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling level:all subtopic:illness subtopic:missed-workouts
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 | pp. 141-142

### [CTB-C13-007] Cautions in planning alternatives

- Slow recovery: do not do long or intense workouts in a third consecutive hard week when fatigued; use 3-week subperiods. When unsure, take the conservative route: better slightly undertrained than overtrained.
- Time-limited: when raising intensity in capped weeks, add only one zone per workout and only for a few workouts. Do not reduce annual volume to fit time.
- Undulating: never combine high duration and high intensity in a single workout; risk of injury, burnout, overtraining.
- Reverse linear: do not use if A race is short or depends on high-intensity efforts (crits, most road races); training would become less like the race.
- Block periodization: not for novices, intermediates or non-podium contenders at national/world level; non-maintained abilities fade badly.
- 9-day week: unsuitable for athletes with rigid 7-day schedules.
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:periodization-cautions sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | pp. 148-157

### [CTB-C13-008] Overtraining: see a physician; differential diagnosis

- WHAT: deep and lingering fatigue together with any other symptom in Table 10.1.
- ACTION: see a physician to be tested for conditions with similar symptoms (chronic fatigue syndrome, Lyme disease, early infectious mononucleosis, others).
- WHY: many symptoms overlap with those diseases; the most common way to diagnose overtraining is to eliminate other possible diseases.
- Further cautions: distressful training (e.g., crash training) is serious and can be dangerous; test your limits infrequently and only when lifestyle stress is very low; do not continue hard training while fatigued; a few days of fatigue should be followed by rest regardless of plan position; pushing a few days too far can end the season.
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:overtraining subtopic:medical-referral
src: Ch 10 | pp. 164, 168, 171
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C13-009] Recovery detractors: antioxidants, icing, NSAIDs, supplements

- Vitamin C and E antioxidant supplements: a couple of studies show they reduce the fitness benefits of adaptation during recovery (WHAT: supplements; WHO: athletes; LIMIT: avoid). Author advises avoiding supplements altogether.
- Icing tired/sore muscles after workouts: 15 min icing after hard strength workout delayed creatine kinase removal versus rest; in a glycogen-depletion study (90 min ride, one leg iced, the other rested) muscle glycogen stores were reduced by 30 percent on average in the iced leg. Probably not in your interest for recovery. EXCEPTION: icing to treat an injury may still be warranted.
- NSAIDs (e.g. ibuprofen): inhibit positive muscular adaptations to exercise, delay injury healing even if reducing soreness; routine use before stressful events can cause other health complications. Not recommended except in treatment of extreme injury.
- Supplement regulation poor; label content cannot be assured.
- Overtraining risk: no workout every day at killer intensity; do not destroy easy days/recovery weeks by training hard; avoid making winter another fatigue season (overtraining syndrome).
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:nsaids subtopic:icing subtopic:antioxidants
evidence: clinical-trial
src: Ch 11 | pp. 180, 189-191

### [CTB-C13-010] Weight lifting safety cautions (Ch 12)

- Lifting to failure: do NOT for cyclists (injury risk); stop 1-2 reps short of failure (slightly less strength, much lower risk).
- 1RM testing: raises injury risk; use multi-rep estimate instead.
- Ballistic lifts: highly risky even with light loads; loads 50% of 1RM or less; learn with extremely light loads first (can take several sessions); reps 3-6 per set; warm up very light; full recovery between sets; lower the weight slowly (approx 1:2 time ratio, lowered in about twice the time as raised).
- Power clean: do not attempt if new to weight lifting; master other hip-knee-ankle extension lifts, especially dead lift, first; start with broomstick, then bar, then plates; keep under 50% 1RM; if unsure, err light.
- Dead lift: riskier than squats; novices use back instead of legs; get expert feedback; keep back straight (not rounded), head up.
- Heavy squats: use a spotter; consider a weight belt for the back, especially in MS.
- Eccentric heavy loads: set down cautiously (muscle damage potential).
- Step-up: do not use a higher platform with knee above hip (counterproductive and risky with heavy loads).
- Lunge: step too long strains the back leg's hip flexor.
- Hamstrings are fragile: lighter loads/more reps for leg curl in MS.
- Juniors (esp. under 16): growth plates susceptible to heavy loads; very light loads only.
- Novices: omit MS phase in year one.
- Weight lifting before long/intense rides: avoid; see timing rule.
- Force reps (on bike): heavy stress on knees; if prone to knee injuries, avoid them; excess can cause injury interrupting training for weeks; first time do only 1 set of 3-4 reps with reduced effort; hill force reps only after at least 4 flat force rep sessions with no knee complaints.
- Quad/hamstring imbalance can cause injuries; women may be at greater risk.
- Heart rate is of no use for force reps (lags behind effort).
tags: type:caution sport:cycling subtopic:weight-lifting-safety level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 | pp. 198-203, 208-211, 218-219, 222-223

### [CTB-C13-011] Taper cautions and common mistakes

- Increasing or maintaining high volume during taper:
  - What: Lengthening workout durations or increasing volume (hours, distance, TSS) in the final 2–3 weeks before an A-priority race.
  - Limit: Avoid completely; reduce weekly volume by 30% to 50% relative to Build 2 by the end of the peak period. Recovery rides should be about half normal duration; hard rides about 80% of normal build duration.
  - Who: All cyclists tapering for an A-priority race.
  - Why: The most common taper error is failing to shed fatigue. Volume generates fatigue; trying to maintain or raise fitness in the final two weeks causes the rider to race with tired legs and sacrifices form.
- Finishing an excessively stressful C-priority race:
  - What: Taking an overly demanding C-priority race to the finish line during the taper period.
  - Limit: Avoid such races, or intentionally abandon at a preplanned point. If unsure, do not enter.
  - Who: Riders using C races as race-simulation sessions during peak/taper.
  - Why: Extreme race stress prevents full recovery within the scheduled two easy days, preventing the subsequent hard session 3 days later, disrupting the taper structure, and threatening A-race performance.
- Using heart rate for 90-second race-period intervals:
  - What: Relying on heart rate monitor to pace or gauge 90-second race-period HIT efforts.
  - Limit: Prohibited; use power instead.
  - Who: All riders performing race-week 90-second intervals.
  - Why: Heart rate lag prevents the cardiovascular system from responding and stabilizing at target zones within 90 seconds, causing improper pacing and power overshoot.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling phase:taper level:all metric:volume metric:load metric:hr metric:power subtopic:taper-mistakes
src: Ch 13 | pp. 230-232, 235

### [CTB-C13-012] Cautions from Chapter 14 and Epilogue

- WHAT: Pressing ahead with a scheduled high-stress workout when tired. LIMIT: change it to an easier workout allowing recovery. WHY: digs a deeper hole, may ruin the season. WHO: all self-coached riders.
- WHAT: Mixing this book's workouts with another method's intensity zones or widely different methodologies. LIMIT: avoid; only slight tweaks. WHY: won't work.
- WHAT: Significantly altering work or recovery interval intensity/duration. WHY: reduces or eliminates benefit.
- WHAT: Training too densely (hard workouts too close together). WHY: fatigue limits power; need more recovery and adaptation.
- WHAT: Judging a race immediately afterward. WHY: emotion leads to impulsive conclusions; wait a day.
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:overtraining subtopic:recovery
src: Epilogue; Ch 14 | pp. 249, 250, 255

### [CTB-C13-013] Appendix B workout and test safety cautions

- MF1: if concern about knees, only 1 set of 6-8 strokes, conservative gear/effort; max 2 sets first time; err toward easy gear.
- MF2: prerequisite at least 4 flat force rep sessions with no knee complaints; stop at the first sign of any tenderness, even slight; max 3 sets; no more than twice per week with at least 48 hours between; need little or no traffic and safe U-turns; stay seated.
- MF3: stop if knees become sensitive; max twice per week with 48 hours between; do not do if prone to knee injury.
- AnE4: very hard; usually once per week, followed by at least 48 hours recovery.
- AnE1: caution on intensity and road safety (traffic, unskilled riders).
- SS4 fixed-gear: dangerous until mastered; start in an empty lot, avoid busy streets. SS5: avoid heavy traffic.
- Field tests: use a safe course (light traffic, no stop signs, few intersections), keep head up, never ride with head down; indoor trainer for T3 only if very stable.
- Novices and intermediates may benefit more from a day off than AE1 recovery rides.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling level:all subtopic:knee-caution subtopic:road-safety
src: Appendix B | pp. 260-267
evidence: author-experience

## C14 Formulas & Calculations

### [CTB-C14-001] Zone-weighted workout score (Table 3.1)

Method: after downloading heart rate or power data, software shows minutes in each zone (five-zone system; zones in Chapter 4). Assign zone weights: zone 1 = 1, zone 2 = 2, zone 3 = 3, zone 4 = 4, zone 5 = 5. Zone score = weight × minutes in zone. Workout score = sum of zone scores. Weekly training load = sum of workout scores. Simple but tedious; software (e.g. Andrew Coggan's TSS-based system on TrainingPeaks.com) does it automatically.

### TABLE 3.1 Example of a 120-Minute Workout Score Based on Time in Each Training Zone

| A. ZONE WEIGHT | B. TIME IN ZONE (TO NEAREST MINUTE) | C. ZONE SCORE<br>(COLUMN A MULTIPLIED BY COLUMN B) |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 1 | 60 | 60 |
| 2 | 16 | 32 |
| 3 | 10 | 30 |
| 4 | 30 | 120 |
| 5 | 4 | 20 |
| | Total Time: 120 minutes | Total Workout Score: 262 |

Multiply zone weight (column A) by time in zone (column B) to produce a zone score (column C).
tags: type:formula level:all sport:cycling metric:load metric:power metric:hr subtopic:workout-score
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | p. 36

### [CTB-C14-002] Training Stress Score (TSS) concept

- Developed by Coggan to combine volume and intensity into one training load number with slight emphasis on intensity.
- Formula basis: the workout's normalized power, intensity factor and duration in seconds, with the rider's FTP. The number describes stress considering duration and intensity relative to what the rider can do. [Formula itself not given in this chapter — see Coggan, Training and Racing with a Power Meter.]
- First required a power meter; can now be calculated from heart rate too (power usually somewhat more accurate).
- Software (e.g., TrainingPeaks.com) calculates TSS after upload of HR or power data. Summing workout TSS over a period (e.g., a week) gives training load.
- Thinking in TSS instead of volume shifts focus to what produces race readiness: volume plus intensity, with intensity primary. Part IV gives TSS numbers for planning seasons, weeks and workouts.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:load,power level:all subtopic:tss
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | pp. 52-54

### [CTB-C14-003] Efficiency Factor (EF) formula

Formula:
$$\text{Efficiency Factor (EF)} = \frac{\text{Normalized Power (NP)}}{\text{Average Heart Rate (HR)}}$$

Variables:
- $\text{NP}$: Normalized power in watts (W) for the steady Aerobic Threshold (AeT) portion of the ride or workout.
- $\text{Average Heart Rate}$: Average heart rate in beats per minute (bpm) across the identical AeT portion.
- Unit: W/bpm.

Interpretation:
- An increasing EF over time indicates improving aerobic endurance fitness.
- A decreasing EF indicates declining aerobic fitness (expected during periods of reduced training, such as the post-season transition).
tags: type:formula level:all sport:cycling system:aerobic metric:power,hr subtopic:efficiency-factor
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 Sidebar 5.3 | p. 68

### [CTB-C14-004] Estimated Aerobic Threshold Heart Rate (AeT HR) formula

Formula (in the absence of laboratory/clinical testing data):
$$\text{Estimated AeT HR Range} = (\text{FTHR} - 30\text{ bpm}) \pm 2\text{ bpm}$$
(General range where AeT HR typically falls: 20 to 40 bpm below FTHR).

Variables:
- $\text{FTHR}$: Functional Threshold Heart Rate in beats per minute (bpm).
- $\text{AeT HR}$: Heart rate at the aerobic threshold in beats per minute (bpm).

Worked example:
- Given $\text{FTHR} = 152\text{ bpm}$:
  $$\text{Target baseline} = 152 - 30 = 122\text{ bpm}$$
  $$\text{Target range} = 122 \pm 2\text{ bpm} \implies 120\text{ to } 124\text{ bpm}$$
tags: type:formula level:all sport:cycling system:aerobic metric:hr subtopic:aerobic-threshold
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 Sidebar 5.3 | p. 68

### [CTB-C14-005] Climbing power-to-weight comparison

Compare FACP and FTP with body weight over time as an indicator of climbing ability during race preparation (i.e., power per unit body weight). No numeric formula given. VO2max is expressed as mL oxygen per kg body weight per minute.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:power-to-weight level:all
src: Ch 6 | pp. 73, 83

### [CTB-C14-006] Annual volume estimation formulas (Chapter 7)

- Annual hours = average weekly hours (last season) × 50 (or × number of weeks actually available if known absences).
- Next-season volume = last season volume × up to 1.10 (up to +10 percent) when last season went well, mainly for athletes in first five years.
- Annual TSS ≈ planned annual hours × 45 (low-intensity), × 50 (long-term average), or × 55 (high-intensity) TSS per hour.
- Efficiency factor change check: if the new FAeT efficiency factor is down more than 10 percent versus last test, return to a short Base 3 (workout AE2).
- Training objective target: about +10 percent over current field-test power is near the upper limit.
tags: type:formula level:all sport:cycling subtopic:annual-volume subtopic:tss
src: Ch 7 | pp. 94, 98-100

### [CTB-C14-007] Predicted 1RM from multiple reps; alternative load finding

Predicted 1RM = load lifted ÷ factor (Table 12.1). Worked example from the book: 9 squats with 80 pounds, factor 0.775; 80 / 0.775 = 103 pounds predicted 1RM, used for determining exercise loads. Loads are lighter than a true 1RM test, which is risky (one maximal effort every few weeks per exercise; raises injury risk). Revisit as strength changes. Simpler method (no testing): guess reps from experience; if you can do more reps than called for, increase load for the next set; adjust up/down per phase's reps.
tags: type:formula metric:1rm level:all subtopic:1rm-estimation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 | pp. 199-200

### [CTB-C14-008] Estimating TSS for weight lifting

Duration has no bearing on strength building; load matters. No perfect method exists (author anticipates accelerometer barbell devices). Probably unnecessary; many cyclists don't quantify it. Low-tech methods:
1. RPE method: rate total workout RPE (Table 4.1, 10-high scale) and multiply by 10. Example: hard MS session RPE 9 → TSS 90.
2. Tonnage method: tonnage per set = load × reps; sum all set tonnages for the session, divide by 2,000 = session tonnage; multiply by 10 = estimated session TSS. Limitation: excludes core exercises (isometric, time-based).
On-bike force reps are captured automatically by power meter (HR less indicative). If you add weight lifting TSS and haven't previously, increase planned weekly TSS by the anticipated lifting TSS; otherwise on-bike TSS will be reduced.
tags: type:formula metric:load subtopic:tss subtopic:tonnage level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 "TSS and Weight Lifting" | pp. 224-225, 227

### [CTB-C14-009] FTP and FTHR from the 20-minute test (T2)

- FTP estimate = average power of the 20-minute test portion (not normalized power) × (1 − 0.05), i.e., subtract 5 percent.
- FTHR estimate = average heart rate of the 20-minute test portion, subtract 5 percent.
- Efficiency factor (EF) = normalized power for the AeT segment ÷ average heart rate for the same segment (AE2/T1).
- AeT heart rate default (if not lab tested) ≈ anaerobic threshold HR − 30 bpm.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:power,hr level:all subtopic:ftp subtopic:efficiency-factor
src: Appendix B, AE2, T1, T2 | pp. 260, 266
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C14-010] Normalized power (NP) - definition

Derived from an algorithm that computes the athlete's average power with greater numeric weight given to surges. NP for a workout is typically somewhat higher than the workout's average power. A power meter and software are necessary to measure NP.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:normalized-power
src: Glossary | p. 274
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C14-011] Efficiency factor (EF)

EF = normalized power ÷ average heart rate, for a steady, aerobic workout or segment such as an aerobic interval. An increasing EF over time suggests improving aerobic fitness.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:power metric:hr system:aerobic level:all subtopic:efficiency-factor
src: Glossary | p. 271
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C14-012] Intensity factor (IF)

IF = NP ÷ FTP (workout normalized power divided by the rider's functional threshold power). A power metric that quantifies workout intensity.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:intensity-factor
src: Glossary | p. 273
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C14-013] Variability index (VI)

VI = normalized power ÷ average power. Indicator of how steadily paced a workout, race, or interval is. A quotient of 1.05 or less indicates steady pacing; a VI above 1.05 indicates progressively non-steady riding marked by surging.
tags: type:formula type:threshold sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:variability-index
src: Glossary | p. 277
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C14-014] Kilojoule (kJ)

Expression of energy expended in a workout or portion of it when training with a power meter: kJ = average power (watts) × number of seconds in the workout or selected portion ÷ 1,000. May also measure cumulative training load for a period such as a week.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:power metric:load level:all subtopic:kilojoule
src: Glossary | p. 273
evidence: author-experience

### [CTB-C14-015] Training Stress Score (TSS) - definition

A method of assigning a numerical value to a session using an algorithm based on session duration and intensity. Requires a power meter, heart rate monitor, or other device for accurately measuring intensity. Because it involves both duration and intensity, cumulative TSS may be used to create a seasonal plan. [The algorithm itself is not given in the glossary.]
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:load level:all subtopic:tss
src: Glossary | p. 277
evidence: author-experience

## C15 Evidence & Key References

### [CTB-C15-001] Key references: Jones (Olympic mental attributes) and Noakes (Central Governor)

- Jones, Graham, PhD: Published in *Harvard Business Review*. Studied Olympic medalists versus non-medalists to identify psychological differentiators. Medalists exhibited meticulous goal focus, strong competitive drive, focus on excellence, immunity to distractions, quick rebound from defeat without self-flagellation, celebration of wins, systematic analysis of success, and high self-confidence. Friel uses this to argue mental toughness is an everyday cognitive practice.
- Noakes, Tim, PhD: Central governor theory. Posits that the brain regulates exercise intensity and terminates effort to preserve somatic integrity. Friel uses this framework to justify high-intensity, limit-testing training designed to condition the brain to tolerate severe fatigue.
tags: type:evidence sport:cycling level:all subtopic:evidence subtopic:mental-toughness
src: Ch 1 "Mental Toughness", "Commitment and Tenacity" | pp. 8, 10

Chapter 11 (fatigue, recovery, adaptation):
- Monitoring: Bellenger et al. 2017 (functional overreaching and autonomic HR regulation); Bosquet et al. 2008, Br J Sports Med 42(9):709–714 (is heart rate a convenient tool to monitor overreaching); Halson 2014, Sports Med 44(S2):S139–S147 (monitoring training load to understand fatigue); Kaikkonen et al. 2008, 2010 (post-exercise HRV); Ten Haaf et al. 2017 (predicting functional overreaching from subjective fatigue and readiness to train after 3 days); Thorpe et al. 2017 (morning-measured fatigue variables in soccer players); Piacentini & Meeusen 2015 (see Ch 14); Busso et al. 1994; Meeusen 2013.
- Recovery modalities: Barnett 2006 (recovery modalities in elite athletes); Bishop, Jones, Woods 2008 (recovery from training); Depauw et al. 2011 (five recovery methods); McAinch et al. 2004 (active vs. passive recovery); Mika et al. 2007; Thiriet et al. 1993; Tomlin & Wenger 2001 (fitness and recovery from intermittent exercise); Peake & Gandevia 2017; Dedering et al. 1999 (EMG and lumbar fatigue).
- Massage: Brummitt 2008; Hemmings et al. 2000; Tiidus 1997; Tiidus & Shoemaker 1995; Weerapong, Hume, Kolt 2005.
- Cold/compression/other: Tseng et al. 2013 (topical cooling/icing delays recovery from eccentric muscle damage); Tucker et al. 2012 (local cold and glycogen recovery); Jajtner et al. 2015 (electrical stimulation vs. cold-water immersion); Hill et al. 2014, Br J Sports Med 48(18):1340–1346 (compression garments meta-analysis); Sands et al. 2015 (dynamic compression); Pearcey et al. 2015 (foam rolling and DOMS).
- Sleep: Born, Muths, Fehm 1988 (sleep and nocturnal GH and cortisol release).
- Nutrition and supplements: Costill et al. 1971 (muscle glycogen on successive days); Etheridge, Philip, Watt 2008 (single protein meal and recovery after eccentric exercise); Pasiakos, Lieberman, McLellan 2014 (protein supplements systematic review); Phillips et al. 1997 (muscle protein synthesis and breakdown after resistance exercise); Maughan 1999, Maughan, King, Lea 2004 (ergogenic aids, dietary supplements); Morrison et al. 2015 and Paulsen et al. 2014 (vitamin C and E supplementation hampers endurance-training adaptations).
- NSAIDs: Mackey 2013; Schoenfeld 2012, Sports Med 42(12):1017–1028; Ziltener, Leal, Fournier 2010 (NSAIDs for athletes).
evidence: bibliography listing only; the specific claims are in Chapters 10–11
tags: type:evidence level:all subtopic:bibliography subtopic:overreaching subtopic:recovery-methods subtopic:hrv
src: Bibliography, Chapters 10–11 | pp. 288–292

### [CTB-C15-008] Bibliography Ch 12-14: strength training, taper, pacing and diary sources

Chapter 12 (muscular force training):
- Strength for cyclists/endurance: Rønnestad, Hansen, Raastad 2010 (in-season strength maintenance increases well-trained cyclists' performance); Sunde et al. 2010 (maximal strength training improves cycling economy); Vikmoen et al. 2017 (heavy strength training in well-trained female athletes); Ebben et al. 2004 (high-load vs. high-repetition training and endurance performance); Mujika, Rønnestad, Martin 2016 (muscle strength and mass effects on endurance cycling).
- Load, hypertrophy, failure: Schoenfeld 2013 (minimum intensity threshold for hypertrophy); Schoenfeld et al. 2016 (low- vs. high-load meta-analysis); Mitchell et al. 2012 (load does not determine hypertrophic gains); Fisher, Steele, Smith 2017; Davies et al. 2016 (repetition failure and strength); Goto et al. 2004; Phillips 2014.
- Power, neural, plyometrics: Cormie, McGuigan, Newton 2011 (Parts 1–2); Carroll, Riek, Carson 2001; Byrne et al. 2016 (aging, muscle power); Ebben et al. 2011 (plyometric intensity); Markovic et al. 2007; Rumpf et al. 2016 (sprint training methods); Behm & Sale 1993; Jakobsen et al. 2013; Seger et al. 1998 (eccentric vs. concentric).
- Hormones/adaptation: Craig et al. 1989; Häkkinen et al. 1988; Kraemer et al. 1998; McCall et al. 1999; Staron et al. 1994.
- Session design: Sforzo & Touey 1996, Simão et al. 2012 (exercise order); Willardson 2006 (rest intervals); Kokkonen, Nelson, Cornwell 1998 (acute stretching inhibits maximal strength).
- Muscle activity/core: Baum 2003, Hug & Dorel 2009, Jorge & Hull 1986, Bijker et al. 2002 (EMG in cycling and running); Behm, Cappa, Power 2009 (trunk activation in running); Lee & McGill 2015, 2016 (isometric training and core stiffness); McDaniel, Subudhi, Martin 2005 (torso stabilization reduces metabolic cost of cycling power).
- Post-exercise nutrient timing: Levenhagen et al. 2001; Rasmussen et al. 2000; Tipton et al. 2001.

Chapter 13 (tapering): Banister, Carter, Zarkadas 1999, Eur J Appl Physiol 79(2):182–191 (training theory and taper in triathletes); Houmard 1991; Mujika et al. 2000; Mujika & Padilla 2003, Med Sci Sports Exerc 35(7):1182–1187 (scientific bases for precompetition tapering); Mujika, Rønnestad, Martin 2016; Shepley et al. 1992; Thomas, Mujika, Busso 2009.

Chapter 14 (pacing and monitoring): Atkinson, Peacock, Passfield 2007, J Sports Sci 25(9):1001–1009 (variable vs. constant power in time trials); Cherry et al. 1997 (all-out vs. even-paced); Foster et al. 1993, Med Sci Sports Exerc 25(3):383–388 (pacing strategy and cycle time trial); González-Alonso et al. 1999, J Appl Physiol 86(3):1032–1039 (body temperature and fatigue in the heat); Piacentini & Meeusen 2015, Int J Sports Physiol Perform 10(4):524–527 (online training-monitoring system to prevent nonfunctional overreaching); Swain 1997, Med Sci Sports Exerc 29(8):1104–1108 (varying power on hills and in wind).
evidence: bibliography listing only

## C16 Notable Statements

### [CTB-C16-001] Notable quotes: High performance, attitude, overtraining, and personal growth

- "In fact, attitude comes before race results—way before." (p. xii)
- "It’s been well established that you must frequently flirt with overtraining in order to approach your potential as an athlete." (p. xiii)
- "Tapering is a complex undertaking that removes fatigue while maintaining fitness. The result is called 'form.'" (p. xiii)
- "You’ll become not only a better cyclist but also a better person for accepting the challenge." (p. xiv)

evidence: unsupported

### [CTB-C16-002] Notable statements on mental performance

- "High performance is as much a way of thinking and behaving—an attitude—as it is a race result. Get the attitude right, and the physical part, as well as the race results, take care of themselves." (p. 3)
- "The overarching question that must precede every one of them, though, is this: Are you making decisions that promote high performance or not? Is your mind in line with your ambitions for the sport?" (p. 4)
- "We usually call this 'pacing,' but it's actually emotion control. Patience means controlling your emotions in the early stages of anything you do." (p. 9)
- "The starting point for high-performance commitment is following standard training routines. The foundation of commitment is training consistency." (p. 11)
- "As a coach, I've learned that all athletes are limited more by their minds than by their bodies. Mental fitness is at least as critical to an athlete's success as physical fitness." (p. 13)

### [CTB-C16-003] Notable statements: Physical performance and purposeful training

- "Training is a research study with one subject: you." (p. 19)
- "Before you start any training session, always ask yourself this key question: What is the purpose of this workout? Don't turn a pedal until you can answer it." (p. 21)
- "Compromising the session’s purpose in order to ride together violates the basic premise of purposeful training." (p. 22)

### [CTB-C16-004] Notable statements - Chapter 4

- "The easier your easy workouts, the harder your hard workouts can be."
- "The single biggest mistake in training is to make easy workouts moderately hard (between AeT and AnT), thus decreasing recovery."
- "Training intensity is the key to success for the advanced rider."
- "It never gets easier, you just go faster." (Greg LeMond, quoted)

### [CTB-C16-005] Notable Statements on Race Preparation and Fitness (Chapter 6)

- "Regardless of whether you are a world-class rider preparing for a Grand Tour or an accomplished rider who mostly does local criteriums, there are only three physical metrics of endurance fitness that must be developed through training. That's it. Only three. They are aerobic capacity, anaerobic threshold, and economy." (p. 72)
- "Aerobic capacity, however, is the 'ticket to the club.' If you aspire to be a world-class rider, you have to have high aerobic capacity. It does not by itself determine race outcomes, as all the top riders are likewise blessed." (p. 73)
- "Poor aerobic endurance is a game-stopper." (p. 77)
- "Anaerobic endurance training should be done sparingly and with great caution. It is very strong medicine. It's not candy." (pp. 80-81)
- "Whenever a workout has the potential to produce a high reward, it also presents a high risk." (p. 81)
- "That's why the time trial is known as the race of truth. It's just you and the clock." (p. 82)

### [CTB-C16-006] Notable statements (Ch 7)

- "The closer you are to your race, the more your training must be like the race."
- "Training is principally an act of faith." (Franz Stampfl, quoted)
- "A rigidly followed plan that doesn't allow for breaks from training when you are overly tired and doesn't consider the many other lifestyle demands on your time is worse than no plan at all."
- "You can never have enough base fitness."

src: Ch 7 | pp. 89, 90, 102

### [CTB-C16-007] Notable quotes on training stress, greed, and recovery

- "For the high-performance rider, training is everything you do in life. It’s not just riding your bike; training also includes your daily routine and obligations. Performance can’t be compartmentalization into just what you do on the road. Nothing in life works that way. Everything you do contributes to your race results." (Part V intro, p. 161)
- "Effective training is the balanced blending of stress and recovery. That’s it. Nothing else." (Ch 10, p. 171)
- "As much as I hate to say it, in training, greed is the greatest emotional challenge for advanced athletes. Our insatiable desire for fitness often pushes us to the edge of what the human body can safely handle." (Ch 10, p. 170)
- "The cure for greed is patience." (Ch 10, p. 170)

### [CTB-C16-008] Notable statements Ch 11

- "Remember that workouts only create the potential for fitness. True fitness is realized when you recover and allow adaptation to occur."
- "Never stand if you can lean, never lean if you can sit, and never sit if you can lie down."
- "Sleep is the most powerful form of recovery and fitness enhancement there is."
- "You’re much better off being slightly undertrained and enthusiastic than constantly tired and apathetic."
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:quotes

### [CTB-C16-009] Notable Statements on Tapering and Form

- "I can tell you from years of training athletes for races of all types that the best predictor of race success is not *fitness*, but *form*." (p. 229)
- "I'd put my money on form over more fitness every time." (p. 229)
- "You can't become more fit by training less. ... In fact, it is not an increase in fitness that causes this phenomenon. It's form." (p. 230)
- "Eliminating fatigue gets you ready to race. And the only effective way to remove fatigue is to reduce your training load. In other words, less training means more form." (p. 230)
- "If you don't have good aerobic fitness by now, you will never have it by race day. Instead, the key to a successful taper is race-like intensity." (p. 231)
- "The bottom line is that training, especially tapering, is a scientific study with only one subject: you. As with anything else in training, if it didn't work, fix it next time." (p. 236)

### [CTB-C16-010] Notable statements (Ch 14 and Epilogue)

- "You can't win a time trial in the first few minutes, but you can lose it."
- "It's not the plan that builds confidence, but rather the act of planning."
- "You are essentially carrying out a research study that has only one subject: you."

## C17 Validity Notes

### [CTB-C17-001] Validity Notes

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] The book states that 220 minus age is unreliable for max heart rate (Ch 4, "Do not use age-based formulas for max heart rate or HR zones"). Subsequent literature (e.g., Tanaka 2001) proposed 208 - 0.7 x age, still with large individual error; consistent with the author's advice to field test.

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Statement that lactate "is not the direct cause of either fatigue or muscle soreness" and that fatigue is not caused by lactic acid ("Threshold behavior..."; anaerobic endurance): consistent with current mainstream physiology, no change.

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Author's claim that coffee and alcoholic beverages do not dehydrate and "contribute to positive hydration" (Ch 11 "Recovery nutrition and hydration"): moderate caffeine is generally not dehydrating in habitual users, but alcohol is a diuretic and impairs recovery/muscle protein synthesis; this claim should not be taken as endorsing alcohol for recovery (e.g., consensus statements from sports nutrition bodies such as ACSM/AND/DC 2016 advise moderation).

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Advice to avoid vitamin C/E antioxidant supplements to protect adaptation (Ch 11) matches the studies cited (Paulsen 2014, Morrison 2015); evidence remains mixed in later reviews. Not a change in guideline.

No other validity notes.
tags: type:caution subtopic:validity
src: Ch 4, Ch 11
